Introduction
A standard technique in mathematics is to study an algebraic object A by considering actions of A on objects
which are better understood. For instance, we might try to analyze a group G by studying the category
Rep(G) of linear representations of G, or a commutative ring R by studying the category of R-modules.
Using the language of algebraic geometry, we can see these two examples as not merely analogous but as
instances of the same general paradigm:
(a) If G is an algebraic group deﬁned over a ﬁeld k (for example, a ﬁnite group), then the category
Rep(G) of (algebraic) representations of G is equivalent to the category of quasi-coherent sheaves on
the classifying stack BG.
(b) If R is a commutative ring, then the category of R-modules is equivalent to the category of quasi-
coherent sheaves on the aﬃne scheme Spec R.
More generally, we can associate to every algebraic stack X the abelian category QC(X) of quasi-coherent
sheaves on X. Understanding this category is often key to understanding the geometry of X. In fact, we can
often recover X from the category QC(X). For example, the classical theory of Tannaka duality (see, for
example, [7]) guarantees that we can recover an aﬃne algebraic group G (over ﬁeld k) as the automorphism
group of the forgetful functor Rep(G) →Vectk, regarded as a symmetric monoidal functor (here Vectk
denotes the category of vector spaces over k).
Our aim in this paper is to adapt the theory of quasi-coherent sheaves to the setting of spectral algebraic
geometry. In §2, we will associate to every spectral Deligne-Mumford stack X a stable ∞-category QCoh(X),
which we call the ∞-category of quasi-coherent sheaves on X. This stable ∞-category is equipped with a
t-structure, and its heart QCoh(X)♥can be identiﬁed with the abelian category of quasi-coherent sheaves
on the ordinary Deligne-Mumford stack underlying X. In the special case where X arises from an ordinary
Deligne-Mumford stack, we can recover QCoh(X) as the derived ∞-category of its heart (at least at the level
of bounded objects). In general this need not be true: indeed, the stable ∞-category QCoh(X) generally
does not arise as the derived ∞-category of any abelian category. The discrepancy between QCoh(X) and
the derived ∞-category of its heart can be regarded as measuring the extent to which X is a “derived”
algebro-geometric object, and is therefore a central concern in the theory of spectral algebraic geometry.
The main theme of this paper can be summarized as follows: in good cases, we can recover a algebro-
geometric object X from the stable ∞-category QCoh(X) of quasi-coherent sheaves on X. More precisely,
specifying a map of geometric objects f : Y →X should be equivalent to giving a (symmetric monoidal)
functor f ∗: QCoh(X) →QCoh(Y) satisfying some natural conditions. In §3 we will prove a general result of
this type assuming that X is quasi-compact and the diagonal map X →X × X is aﬃne (see Theorem 3.4.2).
In fact, we do not even need to assume that X is Deligne-Mumford: our result holds for a more general class
of geometric stacks which are only assumed to admit a faithfullly ﬂat aﬃne covering, rather than ´etale aﬃne
covering (see Deﬁnition 3.4.1). For a related result in the setting of ordinary algebraic geometry, we refer
the reader to [39].
In [63], To¨en introduces the notion of an aﬃne stack over a ﬁeld k. Roughly speaking, a (higher) stack
X is aﬃne if it can be recovered from its cochain complex C∗(X; OX), suitably interpreted as some kind of
generalized commutative ring. In §4, we will give an exposition of To¨en’s theory in the special case where
the ﬁeld k has characteristic zero. Our main result is that aﬃne stacks (over a ﬁeld of characteristic zero)
are governed by a Tannaka duality principle: that is, an aﬃne stack X can be recovered from the symmetric
monoidal ∞-category of quasi-coherent sheaves on X (Corollary 4.6.19).
Remark 0.0.1. In the body of this paper, we will use the term coaﬃne stack in place of To¨en’s usage aﬃne
stack. This is to avoid a conﬂict with our own terminology (we are principally interested in studying spectral
Deligne-Mumford stacks, which may also be aﬃne).
Let k be a ﬁeld and let C be a k-linear symmetric monoidal abelian category. The category C is said to
be Tannakian if, after passing to some extension ﬁeld k′ of k, we can identify C with the category of ﬁnite-
dimensional representations of an aﬃne group scheme. When the ﬁeld k has characteristic zero, Deligne has
2
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 3
given necessary and suﬃcient conditions for C to be Tannakian (see [7]; we give a review of the statement as
Theorem 5.0.1). In §5 we will prove an ∞-categorical analogue of Deligne’s result. More precisely, we will
introduce the notion of a generalized algebraic gerbe (over a ﬁeld k of characteristic zero). We show that a
generalized algebraic gerbe X can be functorially recovered from the ∞-category QCoh(X) of quasi-coherent
sheaves on X. Moreover, we show that a symmetric monoidal ∞-category C has the form QCoh(X) (for
some generalized algebraic gerbe X) if and only if C is locally dimensional (see Deﬁnition 5.6.4): that is, if
and only if C satisﬁes an ∞-categorical generalization of Deligne’s criterion (Theorem 5.6.19).
Throughout this paper, we will make extensive use of the theory of spectral algebraic geometry introduced
in [43]. In §1 we collect some general facts about spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks which are not treated in
[43], but play a role in this paper (and in future papers in this series).
Remark 0.0.2. For other treatments of “derived” Tannaka duality, we refer the reader to the work of
Fukuyama-Iwanari ([18]) and Wallbridge ([74]).

Rozenblyum, and Carlos Simpson for useful conversations related to the subject matter of this paper. Special
thanks are due to Dennis Gaitsgory, with whom I have shared several years of conversations about quasi-
coherent sheaves in the setting of derived algebraic geometry. Most of all, I would like to thank Bertrand
To¨en: many of the themes developed here are based on his ideas, expressed in both his writings and in
personal communications.
Notation and Terminology
This paper will make extensive use of the theory of ∞-categories, as developed in [40]. We will also need the
theory of structured ring spectra, which is presented from an ∞-categorical point of view in [41]. Finally, we
will make use of the theory of geometries developed in [42], and earlier paper in this series. For convenience,
we will adopt the following reference conventions:
(T) We will indicate references to [40] using the letter T.
(A) We will indicate references to [41] using the letter A.
(V ) We will indicate references to [42] using the Roman numeral V.
(V II) We will indicate references to [43] using the Roman numeral VII.
For example, Theorem T.6.1.0.6 refers to Theorem 6.1.0.6 of [40].
We will assume general familiarity with the notations of [40] and [41]. We let Sp denote the ∞-category
of spectra. We regard Sp as endowed with the smash product monoidal structure. If M and N are spectra,
we will denote their smash product by M ⊗N. We let CAlg denote the ∞-category CAlg(Sp) of commutative
algebra objects of Sp; we will refer to the objects of CAlg as E∞-rings. We let Mod denote the ∞-category
Mod(Sp) of commutative module objects of Sp: that is, the objects of Mod are pairs (A, M), where A is an
E∞-ring and M is an A-module object of Sp. In this case, we will say that M is an A-module spectrum or
simply that M is an A-module. For each E∞-ring A, we let ModA = Mod ×CAlg{A} denote the ∞-category
ModA(Sp) of A-module spectra.
We say that an E∞-ring A is connective if the homotopy groups πiA vanish for i < 0, and that A is
discrete if the homotopy groups πiA vanish for i̸ = 0. We let CAlgcn and CAlg0 denote the full subcategory of
CAlg spanned by the connective and discrete E∞-rings, respectively. The construction A →π0A determines
an equivalence between CAlg0 and the (nerve of the) ordinary category of commutative rings.
We will
generally abuse terminology by using this equivalence to identify commutative rings with their images in
CAlg0 ⊆CAlg.
3
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 4
We will assume that the reader is familiar the theory of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks introduced in
[43]. We will identify a nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stack X with a pair (X, O), where X is an
∞-topos and O is a sheaf of E∞-rings on X. The collection of all nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford
stacks is organized into an ∞-category which we will denote by Stknc. We let Stk denote the full subcategory
of Stknc spanned by the spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks.
In this paper, we will need to consider several diﬀerent avatars of the spectrum of a commutative ring
(or E∞-ring):
• If R is a commutative ring, we let SpecZ R denote collection of prime ideals p ⊆R. We regard SpecZ R
as endowed with the Zariski topology: it has a basis of open sets given by Ux = {p ∈SpecZ R : f /∈p},
where x ranges over the elements of R. We will sometimes refer to SpecZ R as the Zariski spectrum of
the commutative ring R.
If R is an E∞-ring, we let SpecZ R denote the Zariski spectrum SpecZ(π0R) of the commutative ring
π0R.
• If R is a commutative ring, we let Specc R denote the aﬃne scheme associated to R, in the sense of
classical scheme theory. More precisely, Specc R is the ringed space (SpecZ R, O), where O is the sheaf
of commutative rings on SpecZ R given by O(Ux) = R[ 1
x] (the superscript c is intended to indicate the
word “classical”).
• If R is an E∞-ring, we let Spec R denote the aﬃne nonconnective Deligne-Mumford stack associated to
R. More precisely, we can identify Spec R with the spectrally ringed ∞-topos (X, O), where X denotes
the ∞-topos of functors CAlg´et
R →S which are sheaves with respect to the ´etale topology and O is
the sheaf of E∞-rings on X given by the forgetful functor CAlg´et
R →CAlg. Here CAlg´et
R denotes the
∞-category of ´etale R-algebras.
• If R is a connective E∞-ring, we let Specf R denote the functor CAlgcn →S given by the formula
(Specf R)(A) = MapCAlg(R, A). We will refer to Specf R as the functor corepresented by R (the super-
script f is meant to emphasize that we are thinking of the spectrum of R as a functor). Equivalently,
Specf R is the functor represented by the aﬃne spectral Deligne-Mumford stack Spec R (that is, we
have a canonical homotopy equivalence (Specf R)(A) = MapStk(Spec A, Spec R).
We say that a nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stack X is aﬃne if it has the form Spec R, for
some E∞-ring R.
More generally, we say that an object U ∈X is aﬃne if the nonconnective spectral
Deligne-Mumford stack (X/U, O |U) is aﬃne.
In this paper, we will generally be interested in geometric objects X which are more general than spectral
Deligne-Mumford stacks. We will generally treat such objects using the “functor-of-points” philosophy: that
is, we will think of X as a functor from some avatar of the category of commutative rings (usually the ∞-
category CAlgcn of connective E∞-rings) to the ∞-category S of spaces. We will generally be interested in
functors which are sheaves with respect to an appropriate Grothendieck topology on the ∞-category CAlgcn.
With this in mind, we let Shvfpqc denote the full subcategory of Fun(CAlgcn, S) spanned by those functors
which are sheaves with respect to the ﬂat topology introduced in §VII.5. For technical reasons (stemming
from the fact that CAlgcn is not small), it is often more convenient to work in a slightly larger ∞-category:
we let d
Shvfpqc denote the full subcategory of Fun(CAlgcn, bS) spanned by those functors which are sheaves
with respect to the ﬂat topology; here bS denotes the ∞-category of spaces which are not necessarily small.
1
Generalities on Spectral Deligne-Mumford Stacks
Our goal in this section is to collect some general facts about spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks which will be
needed in the later sections of this paper (and in later papers in this series). We begin in §1.1 by studying
pointts of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks. Recall that a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack X is a pair (X, O),
4
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 5
where X is an ∞-topos and O is a sheaf of E∞-rings on X. We deﬁne a point η of X to be a point of the
∞-topos X: that is, a geometric morphism η∗: X →S. If X is the spectrum of a separably closed ﬁeld k,
then the ∞-topos X is equivalent to S so that X has a unique point (up to equivalence). More generally, any
map φ : Spec k →X where k is a separably closed ﬁeld determines a point of X. One of our main results
(Proposition 1.1.15) asserts that every point of X arises in this way. Moreover, if we demand further that φ
be minimal (see Deﬁnition 1.1.10), then the ﬁeld k is uniquely determined up to (noncanonical) isomorphism.
Recall that a map of spectrally ringed ∞-topoi φ : (X, OX) →(Y, OY) is said to be ´etale if the underlying
geometric morphism φ∗: X →Y is an ´etale morphism of ∞-topoi (that is, it induces an equivalence X ≃Y/U
for some object U ∈Y) and the map of structure sheaves φ∗OY →OX is an equivalence. If f : A →B is an
´etale morphism of E∞-rings, then the induced map Spec B →Spec A is an ´etale morphism of nonconnective
spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks. In §1.2, we will prove the converse: every ´etale morphism between aﬃne
nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks arises from an ´etale morphism of E∞-rings (Theorem 1.2.1).
Using this result, we give a convenient algebraic criterion for an arbitrary map of spectral Deligne-Mumford
stacks φ : X →Y to be ´etale (Corollary 1.2.2).
Let X = (X, OX) be a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack, and let n ≥0 be an integer. We say that X is
n-localic if the ∞-topos X is n-localic: that is, if X is equivalent to an ∞-category of the form Shv(C), where
C is an n-category equipped with a Grothendieck topology. In §1.3, we will give another characterization
of the class of n-localic spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks: if n ≥1, then X is n-localic if and only if the
mapping space MapStk(Spec R, X) is n-truncated for every commutative ring R (Proposition 1.3.3).
Recall that a scheme (X, OX) is said to be quasi-compact if the underlying topological space X is quasi-
compact, and to be quasi-separated if the intersection U ∩V is quasi-compact for every pair of quasi-compact
open subsets U, V ⊆X. In §1.4, we will study analogous conditions in the setting of spectral Deligne-
Mumford stacks.
We say that a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack X = (X, O) is n-quasi-compact if the
underlying ∞-topos X is n-coherent, in the sense of Deﬁnition VII.3.1. In the case n = 0, this is equivalent
to the condition that X be quasi-compact; when n = 1, it is analogous to the requirement of being quasi-
compact and quasi-separated.
Our discussion in this section barely scratches the surface of the theory of spectral Deligne-Mumford
stacks. There are a host other other conditions on a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack X (or, more generally,
on a morphism φ : X →Y) which are play a role in spectral algebraic geometry. In §1.5, we sketch a general
paradigm for discussing properties which are local: that is, which can be tested after passing to a covering
of X. We give a few examples in §1.5; others will be introduced as the need arises.
1.1
Points of Spectral Deligne-Mumford Stacks
Let X = (X, OX) be a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack. It follows from Theorem VII.4.1 that the hypercom-
pletion X∧has enough points. Our goal in this section is to obtain an explicit description of the points of
X. We begin by introducing some terminology.
Deﬁnition 1.1.1. Let A be an E∞-ring. We will say that A is strictly Henselian if the commutative ring
π0A is strictly Henselian, in the sense of Deﬁnition VII.7.17.
Remark 1.1.2. An E∞-ring A is strictly Henselian if and only if it is strictly Henselian when regarded as
a sheaf of E∞-rings on the ∞-topos S, in the sense of Deﬁnition VII.8.3.
Proposition 1.1.3. Let A →B be a map of E∞-rings. The following conditions are equivalent:
(1) The E∞-ring B is strictly Henselian.
(2) The functor GnSp

α}
such that A →Q
α Aα is faithfully ﬂat. Then any A-algebra map A′ →B factors (up to homotopy)
through A′
α for some index α.

Proof. The equivalence (1) ⇔(2) is a special case of Proposition VII.8.13, and the equivalence (2) ⇔(3) is
tautological. The implication (4) ⇒(3) is obvious, and the converse follows from Proposition VII.8.17. The
implication (5) ⇒(4) follows by taking R = A′, and implication (4) ⇒(3) by replacing the maps {R →Rα}
by the induced map {A′ →Rα ⊗R A′}.
We will need a few elementary facts about strictly Henselian E∞-rings.
Proposition 1.1.4. Let R be a Henselian commutative ring and let I ⊆R be a proper ideal. Then the
quotient R/I is also a Henselian local ring.
Proof. Let m denote the maximal ideal of R. Suppose we are given an ´etale map R/I →R′

R′ →R′/IR′ ≃R′
0 →R/m lifts to a map R′ →R. Reducing modulo I, we obtain a map R′

φ.
Corollary 1.1.5. Let f : A →A′ be a map of E∞-rings which induces a surjective ring homomorphism
π0A →π0A′. If A is strictly Henselian and A′̸ = 0, then A′ is strictly Henselian.
Proof. This follows from Proposition 1.1.4, since the local rings π0A and π0A′ have the same residue ﬁeld.

(∗) Let A′ be an ´etale A-algebra, and suppose we are given a ﬁnite collection of ´etale maps {A′ →A′

I ⊆B(β). Since θ is faithfully ﬂat, the image of I generates the unit ideal in π0B. Changing our index β,
we may suppose that I is the unit ideal, so that the map B(β) →Q
α B(β)α is faithfully ﬂat. It follows
from (∗) that there exists an index α and a map of B(β)-algebras B(β)α →B, which determines a map of
B-algebras Bα →B.

It is often convenient to describe points of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks by Henselizing the spectra

Let η : X0 →X be a geometric point of a connective spectral Deligne-Mumford stack X = (X, OX). Then

Proposition 1.1.15. Let X = (X, OX) be a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack. The construction (η : X0 →
X) 7→(η∗∈Fun(X, S)) determines an equivalence from the ∞-category GPt(X) of minimal geometric points
of X to the subcategory of Fun(X, S) whose objects are functors which preserve small colimits and ﬁnite limits
and whose morphisms are equivalences.
Remark 1.1.16. In other words, the ∞-category of minimal geometric points of a spectral Deligne-Mumford
stack X = (X, OX) can be identiﬁed with the underlying Kan complex of the ∞-category of points of the the
∞-topos X (that is, geometric morphisms η∗: X →S).
Proof. For every object U ∈X, let X/U denote the spectral Deligne-Mumford stack (X/U, OX |U). Let Pt(U)
denote the subcategory of Fun(X/U, S) whose objects are functors which preserve small colimits and ﬁnite
limits, and whose morphisms are equivalences. Let 1 denote the ﬁnal object of X. The proof of Lemma
V.2.3.11 shows that we can write 1 as a colimit lim
−→Uα, where each X/Uα is an aﬃne spectral Deligne-Mumford

Using Remark 1.1.14 and Proposition T.6.3.5.5, we deduce that the vertical maps are equivalences. Con-
sequently, to prove that the map GPt(X) →Pt(1) is a homotopy equivalence, it suﬃces to show that each
of the maps GPt(X/Uα) →Pt(Uα) is a homotopy equivalence. We may therefore replace X by X/Uα and
thereby reduce to the case where X = Spec A for some connective E∞-ring A.
8
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 9
Let X0 = Spec(π0A). The underlying ∞-topoi of X and X0 are the same, and the canonical map
MapStk(Spec k, X0) →MapStk(Spec k, X)
is a homotopy equivalence for every discrete E∞-ring k. We may therefore replace A by π0A and thereby
reduce to the case where A is a discrete E∞-ring.
Let Fun∗(X, S) denote the full subcategory of Fun(X, S) spanned by those functors which preserve small
colimits and ﬁnite limits. Using Corollary 1.1.7, we can identify Fun∗(X, S) with the full subcategory of
C ⊆CAlgA whose objects are A-algebras B which are strictly Henselian and can be written as a ﬁltered
colimit of ´etale A-algebras. We can identify GPt(X) with the nerve of the groupoid consisting of those A-
algebras k which are separable closures of some residue ﬁeld of A. We will denote the functor θ : GPt(X) →C
by k 7→Ak. This construction can be characterized by the following universal property: for every object
k ∈GPt(X) and every ´etale A-algebra B, we have a canonical bijection
HomA(B, Ak) ≃Homk(B ⊗A k, k) ≃HomA(B, k).
Here HomR(R′, R′′) denotes the set of R-algebra maps from R′ to R′′. Note that this bijection extends
naturally to the case where B is a ﬁltered colimit of ´etale A-algebras.
For every object B ∈C, let pB denote the inverse image in A of the maximal ideal of B. Any morphism
B →B′ in C determines an inclusion of prime ideals pB′ ⊆pB. We let C0 denote the subcategory of C
consisting of those morphisms B →B′ for which pB = pB′. Since C0 contains all equivalences in C, the map
θ factors through C0. We will show that θ induces an equivalence GPt(X) →C0. From this, it will follow
that C0 is a Kan complex, hence that C0 is the largest Kan complex contained in C and therefore that θ
exhibits GPt(X) as equivalent to the largest Kan complex contained in C.
Let k ∈GPt(X), and let p = pAk. Note that HomA(A[u−1], Ak) is empty if and only if u ∈p. It follows
that p is the kernel of the map A →k, so that k is a separable closure of the residue ﬁeld κ(p) of A. Since
Ak is a ﬁltered colimit of ´etale A-algebras, the quotient (Ak/pAk) ≃Ak ⊗A κ(p) is a ﬁltered colimit of ﬁnite
´etale algebras over κ(p). Since Ak is strictly Henselian, the quotient Ak/pAk is also strictly Henselian and
therefore a separable closure of the residue ﬁeld κ(p). Let k′ be another separable closure of κ(p). The
canonical map
v : HomA(k, k′) ≃MapGPt(X)(k, k′) →MapC(Ak, Ak′) ≃HomA(Ak, Ak′) ≃HomA(Ak, k′)
is given by composition with a map v0 : Ak/pAk →k. Here v0 is a κ(p)-algebra map between separable
closures of κ(p), and therefore an isomorphism. It follows that v is bijective, which proves that θ : GPt(X) →
C0 is fully faithful.
It remains to prove that θ is essentially surjective. Let B ∈C and let p = pB. Then k = B/pB ≃B⊗Aκ(p)
is a ﬁltered colimit of ´etale κ(p)-algebras. Since k is strictly Henselian, we deduce that k is a separable closure
of κ(p). In particular, pB is the maximal ideal of B, and we can identify k with an object of GPt(X). For
every ´etale A-algebra B′, we have a canonical map
HomA(B′, B) →HomA(B′, B/pB) = HomA(B′, k) ≃HomA(B′, Ak).
Since B is Henselian, this map is bijective. Since B and Ak can both be obtained as a ﬁltered colimit of
´etale A-algebras, we conclude that B ≃Ak.

Deﬁnition 1.1.18. Let X = (X, OX) be a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack. We will say that X is empty
if X is a contractible Kan complex (that is, if X is equivalent to the ∞-category of sheaves on the empty
topological space).
Lemma 1.1.19. Let X be a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack. The following conditions are equivalent:
(1) The spectral Deligne-Mumford stack X is not empty.
(2) There exists a nonzero connective E∞-ring A and an ´etale map Spec A →X.
(3) There exists a minimal geometric point Spec k →X.
Proof. The implications (2) ⇒(3) and (3) ⇒(1) are obvious. We prove that (1) ⇒(2). Assume that (1)
is satisﬁed. Write X = (X, OX), so that there exists an object of X which is not initial. It follows that
there exists an aﬃne object U ∈X which is not initial. Then (X/U, OX |U) is equivalent to Spec A for some
connective E∞-ring A. We therefore have an ´etale map Spec A →X. Since U is not an initial object of X,
A is nonzero; this proves (2).
Proposition 1.1.20. Let f : X →Y be a morphism of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks. The following
conditions are equivalent:
(1) Let X and Y denote the underlying ∞-topoi of X and Y, respectively. Then every geometric morphism
of ∞-topoi η∗: S →Y factors through the geometric morphism f∗: X →Y.
(2) For every ﬁeld k and every map η : Spec k →Y, there exists a ﬁeld extension K of k such that the
composite map Spec K →Spec k →Y factors through f.
(3) For every ﬁeld k and every map η : Spec k →Y, the ﬁber product Spec k ×Y X is nonempty.
Proof. The implication (2) ⇒(3) is obvious, and the implication (3) ⇒(1) follows from Lemma 1.1.19. We
will show that (1) ⇒(2). Let η : Spec k →Y be a morphism of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks. We wish
to show that, after enlarging k if necessary, the map η factors through X. Without loss of generality, we may
assume that k is separably closed. Note that η determines a geometric morphism of ∞-topoi η∗: S →Y.
Using condition (1), we deduce that η∗factors as a composition

1.2
´Etale Morphisms
Let f : (X, OX) →(Y, OY) be a morphism of nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks. We say that
f is ´etale if it is ´etale when regarded as a morphism of spectrally ringed ∞-topoi. In other words, we say
that f is ´etale if the underlying geometric morphism f∗: X →Y is an ´etale morphism of ∞-topoi and
f ∗OY →OX is an equivalence of sheaves of E∞-rings on X. Our goal in this section is to relate the theory of
´etale morphisms in Stk to the theory of ´etale morphisms in CAlg. Our main result can be stated as follows:
Theorem 1.2.1. Let φ : A →B be a map of E∞-rings. Then φ is ´etale if and only if the induced map
Spec B →Spec A is an ´etale map of nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks.
Corollary 1.2.2. Let f : X →Y be a map between nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks. The
following conditions are equivalent:
(i) The map f is ´etale.
(ii) For every commutative diagram

Proof. The implication (ii) ⇒(i) is obvious, and the converse follows from Theorem 1.2.1.
The proof of Theorem 1.2.1 will occupy our attention throughout this section. We begin by treating the
case where A is a ﬁeld (regarded as a discrete E∞-ring).

Lemma 1.2.3. Let k be a ﬁeld. Suppose we are given an ´etale map of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks
f : X →Spec k. If X is aﬃne, then X ≃Spec k′ where k′ is an ´etale k-algebra.
Proof. Write Spec k = (X, O), so that X ≃(X/U, O |U) for some object U ∈X.
Write X = Spec A for
some E∞-algebra A over k. Choose a separable closure k of k, so that the category of ´etale sheaves of
sets on CAlg´et
k can be identiﬁed with the category of sets equipped with a continuous action of the Galois
group G = Gal(k/k). Note that A is connective. For every discrete commutative ring R, the mapping
space MapStk(Spec R, X) is discrete. It follows that U is a discrete object of X, which we can identify with
a set equipped with a continuous action of G. Decomposing this set into G-orbits, we deduce that X is
a coproduct of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks of the form Spec kα, where each kα is a ﬁnite separable
extension of k. Since X is quasi-compact, this coproduct is ﬁnite, from which it follows that A ≃Q
α kα is
an ´etale k-algebra.

at the point x, and let Bx = B ⊗A κ(x). Then Spec Bx →Spec κ(x) is a pullback of f and therefore ´etale.

Proposition 1.2.8. Let X be an ∞-topos containing a (−1)-truncated object U, and let i∗: X /U →X be

coproduct U ⨿U ′ is a ﬁnal object of X.
Proof. The implication (2) ⇒(1) is clear: if U ⨿U ′ is a ﬁnal object of X, then the construction X 7→U ⨿X
determines an equivalence of ∞-categories X/U ′ →X /U. Conversely, suppose that (2) is satisﬁed. Then the
pullback functor i∗: X →X /U admits a left adjoint i!. Let 1 denote a ﬁnal object of X and let V = i!i∗1.
Then
U × V ≃U × i!i∗1 ≃i!(i∗U × i∗1) ≃i!i∗(U).
Since i∗(U) is an initial object of X /U, the object U × V ≃i!i∗U is an initial object of X. Let U ′ = τ≤−1V ,
so that U × U ′ is an initial object of X. It follows that U ⨿U ′ is also a (−1)-truncated object of X. The
identity map id : i!i∗1 →V induces a map i∗1 →i∗V in X /U, which determines a map
1 ≃i∗i∗1 →i∗i∗V ≃U ⨿U×V V ≃U ⨿V.
Composing with the projection map V →τ≤−1V , we obtain a map 1 →U ⨿U ′, so that U ⨿U ′ is a
(−1)-truncated, 0-connective object of X and therefore a ﬁnal object of X.
Deﬁnition 1.2.9. Let f∗: X →Y be a geometric morphism of ∞-topoi. We will say that f∗is a clopen
immersion if it satisﬁes the equivalent conditions of Proposition 1.2.8: that is, if it is both ´etale and a closed
immersion. We will say that a map of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks f : (X, OX) →(Y, OY) is a clopen
immersion if it is ´etale and the underlying geometric morphism f∗: X →Y is a clopen immersion of ∞-topoi.
We will need a few standard results from commutative algebra.
Lemma 1.2.10. Suppose we are given a pushout diagram of commutative rings
A

A′
φ′

B
/ B′,
where ψ is faithfully ﬂat. If B′ is ﬁnitely generated over A′, then B is ﬁnitely generated over A.
Proof. If B′ is ﬁnitely generated over A′, then there exists a ﬁnitely generated A-subalgebra B0 ⊆B such
that A′ ⊗A B0 →B′ is surjective. It follows that A′ ⊗A (B/B0) ≃0, so the faithful ﬂatness of A′ over A
implies that B/B0 ≃0. We conclude that B ≃B0 is ﬁnitely generated.
Lemma 1.2.11. Suppose we are given a pushout diagram of commutative rings
A

Proof. Lemma 1.2.10 implies that we can write B as a quotient A[x1, . . . , xn]/I, for some ideal I. Then B′ can
be identiﬁed with the quotient of A′[x1, . . . , xn] by the ideal IA′[x1, . . . , xn]. Since B′ is of ﬁnite presentation
over A′, we conclude that IA′[x1, . . . , xn] is ﬁnitely generated. We may therefore choose a ﬁnitely generated
subideal I0 ⊆I such that I0A′[x1, . . . , xn] = IA′[x1, . . . , xn].
Since A′[x1, . . . , xn] is faithfully ﬂat over
A[x1, . . . , xn], we conclude that I = I0 is ﬁnitely generated, so that B is of ﬁnite presentation over A.
Lemma 1.2.12. Let φ : A →B be a map of connective E∞-rings. Suppose there exists a faithfully ﬂat
morphism A →A′ which induces an ´etale morphism φ′ : A′ →B′, where B” = A′ ⊗A B. Then φ is ´etale.
Proof. It follows from Lemma 1.2.11 that π0B is ﬁnitely presented as a commutative algebra over π0A. It
will therefore suﬃce to show that the relative cotangent complex LB/A is trivial (Lemma VII.8.9). Since A′
is faithfully ﬂat over A, it will suﬃce to show that
A′ ⊗A LB/A ≃B′ ⊗B LB/A ≃LB′/A′
vanishes, which follows from Corollary A.7.5.4.5 (since φ′ is ´etale).
Proof of Theorem 1.2.1. Let φ : A →B be a map of E∞-rings which induces an ´etale map f : Spec B →
Spec A of nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks. We wish to prove that φ is ´etale. We ﬁrst treat
the case where A is discrete.
Let eφ : SpecZ A →Z be deﬁned as in Remark 1.2.4. Lemma 1.2.5 guarantees the existence of an integer
n ≥0 such that eφ(x) ≤n for every point x ∈SpecZ A. We proceed by induction on n. If n = 0, then B ≃0
and there is nothing to prove. Let us therefore assume that n > 0.
Let U ⊆SpecZ A be the open subset corresponding to the image of φ. Since Spec B is quasi-compact, U
is quasi-compact. We can therefore write U as a union of ﬁnitely many open sets of the form SpecZ A[ 1
ai ].
Note that the elements φ(ai) generate the unit ideal in π0B. According to Lemma 1.2.12, it will suﬃce to
show that each of the localizations B[
1
φ(ai)] is ´etale over A[ 1
ai ]. We may therefore replace A by A[ 1
ai ] and
thereby reduce to the case where the map f is surjective.
Since f is an ´etale surjection, there exists a faithfully ﬂat ´etale map A →C such that the induced
map Spec C →Spec A factors through f. Using Lemma 1.2.12, we are reduced to proving that the map
C →C ⊗A B is ´etale. We may therefore replace A by C and thereby reduce to the case where f admits
a section s : Spec A →Spec B. Note that the underlying map of E∞-rings B →A induces a surjection
π0B →π0A (since it admits a right inverse). Proposition 1.2.7 implies that s induces a closed immersion at
the level of ∞-topoi. Since s is ´etale, we deduce that s is a clopen immersion: that is, Spec B decomposes
as a disjoint union X ` Spec A (see Proposition 1.2.8). It follows that B ≃A × B′, where X = Spec B′.
Let φ′ denote the restriction of φ to X, and deﬁne eφ′ as in Remark 1.2.4. For each x ∈SpecZ A, we have
eφ′(x) = eφ(x) −1 ≤n −1. It follows from the inductive hypothesis that B′ is ´etale over A, so that B is
´etale over A as desired. This completes the proof of Theorem 1.2.1 in the case where A is discrete.
We now treat the general case.
Write Spec A = (X, OX) and Spec B = (Y, OY).
Then (Y, π0 OY) is
´etale over (X, π0 OX). It follows from the ﬁrst part of the proof that π0B is ´etale over π0A. Using Theorem
A.7.5.0.6, we can choose an ´etale A-algebra B′ and an isomorphism of π0A-algebras α0 : π0B′ ≃π0B.
Theorem A.7.5.4.2 implies that α0 can be lifted (in an essentially unique way) to a map of A-algebras
α : B′ →B. Since Spec B and Spec B′ are both ´etale over Spec A, we conclude that α induces an ´etale map
g : Spec B →Spec B′. Since α0 is an isomorphism of commutative rings, the map g induces an equivalence
at the level of underlying ∞-topoi, and is therefore an equivalence. It follows that B ≃B′ is ´etale over A,
as desired.
We close this section with a useful result concerning ´etale morphisms between E∞-rings.
Proposition 1.2.13. Suppose we are given maps of E∞-rings A
f→B
g→C, where g is ´etale and faithfully
ﬂat. Then f is ´etale if and only if g ◦f is ´etale.
15
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 16
Proof. The “only if” direction is obvious. For the converse, assume that g ◦f is ´etale. Lemma 1.5.13 implies
that f is ﬂat. It will therefore suﬃce to show that the commutative ring π0B is ´etale over π0A. Replacing
A, B, and C by their connective covers, we can reduce to the case where A, B, and C are connective. We
have a ﬁber sequence
C ⊗B LB/A →LC/A →LC/B.
Since C is ´etale over both A and B, we have LC/A ≃LC/B ≃0. It follows that C ⊗B LB/A ≃0. Since
C is faithfully ﬂat over B, this implies that LB/A ≃0 (Remark VII.5.3). To complete the proof that B is
´etale over A, it will suﬃce to show that π0B is ﬁnitely presented as a commutative algebra over π0A (Lemma
VII.8.9). We ﬁrst prove that π0B is ﬁnitely generated over π0A. Since g is ´etale, the commutative algebra
π0C is ﬁnitely presented over π0B. We may therefore choose a ﬁnitely generated π0A-subalgebra R ⊆π0B
and an ´etale morphism R →R′ such that π0C ≃(π0B) ⊗R R′. Since π0C is ﬁnitely generated over π0A, we
may assume (after enlarging R if necessary) that the map R′ →π0C is surjective. Since π0C is faithfully ﬂat
over π0B, we conclude that the inclusion R ,→π0B is surjective, so that π0B = R is ﬁnitely generated over
π0A. Choose a surjection S →π0B, where S is ﬁnitely presented over π0A. Let I denote the kernel of φ; we
wish to show that I is a ﬁnitely generated ideal. Using the structure theory of ´etale morphisms (Proposition
VII.8.10, we can choose an ´etale morphism S →S′ and an isomorphism π0C ≃π0B ⊗S S′. Replacing S by
the quotient S/J for some ﬁnitely generated ideal J ⊆I, we can assume that S′ is faithfully ﬂat over S. It
follows that the canonical map S” →π0C is surjection with kernel S′ ⊗S I. Since π0C is ﬁnitely presented
over π0A, the ideal S′ ⊗S I is ﬁnitely generated as a module over S′. Because S′ is faithfully ﬂat over S, the
ideal I is ﬁnitely generated as an S-module, as desired.
1.3
Localic Spectral Deligne-Mumford Stacks
Let X = (X, OX) be a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack. We say that X is n-localic if the ∞-topos X is
n-localic. In this section, we will give a reformulation of this condition (Proposition 1.3.3) in terms of the
functor represented by X (at least when n ≥1).
Deﬁnition 1.3.1. Let n ≥0. A spectral Deligne-Mumford n-stack is a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack X
with the following property: for every discrete commutative ring R, the mapping space
MapStk(Spec R, X)
is n-truncated. A spectral algebraic space is a spectral Deligne-Mumford 0-stack.
Remark 1.3.2. Let X = (X, OX) be a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack. The condition that X be a spectral
algebraic space depends only on the underlying 0-truncated spectral Deligne-Mumford stack (X, π0 OX).
Our main result is:
Proposition 1.3.3. Let X = (X, OX) be a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack, and let n ≥1 be an integer. The
following conditions are equivalent:
(1) The ∞-topos X is n-localic.
(2) For every commutative ring R, the mapping space MapStk(Spec R, X) is n-truncated. That is, X is a
spectral Deligne-Mumford n-stack.
The proof will require a few general observations about n-localic ∞-topoi.
Lemma 1.3.4. Let X be an ∞-topos, let n ≥0 be an integer, and let U be an n-truncated object of X. If X
is n-localic, then X/U is n-localic.
16
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 17
Proof. If X is n-localic, then we can write X as a topological localization of P(C), for some small n-category C
(see the proof of Proposition T.6.4.5.7). Let us identify X with the corresponding subcategory of P(C). Then
X/U is a topological localization of P(C)/U. According to Proposition T.6.4.5.9, it will suﬃce to show that the
∞-topos P(C)/U is n-localic. The presheaf U : Cop →S classiﬁes a right ﬁbration of ∞-categories θ : eC →C.
Since U is n-truncated, the ﬁbers of θ are n-truncated Kan complexes, so that eC is also an n-category. We
complete the proof by observing that there is a canonical equivalence of ∞-categories P(C)/U ≃P(eC).
Lemma 1.3.5. Let X and Y be ∞-topoi, and suppose that Y is n-localic for some n ≥0. Let Fun∗(X, Y)
denote the full subcategory of Fun(X, Y) spanned by the geometric morphisms f ∗: X →Y. Then Fun∗(X, Y)
is equivalent to an n-category.
Proof. Let τ≤n X and τ≤n Y be the underlying n-topoi of X and Y. Since Y is n-localic, we can identify
Fun∗(X, Y) with the full subcategory of Fun(τ≤n−1 X, τ≤n−1 Y) spanned by those functors which preserve
small colimits and ﬁnite limits. The desired result now follows from the observation that τ≤n−1 Y is equivalent
to an n-category.
Lemma 1.3.6. Let X = (X, OX) and Y = (Y, OY) be spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks. Assume that OY is
n-truncated, and that X is n-localic. Then the mapping space MapStk(Y, X) is n-truncated.
Proof. There is an evident forgetful functor θ : MapStk(Y, X) →MapLTop(X, Y), where the codomain of θ
is n-truncated by Lemma 1.3.5. It will therefore suﬃce to show that the ﬁber of θ over every geometric
morphism f ∗: X →Y is n-truncated. Unwinding the deﬁnitions, we see that this ﬁber can be identiﬁed
with a summand of the mapping space MapShvCAlg(Y)(f ∗OX, OY), which is n-truncated by virtue of our
assumption that OY is n-truncated.
Remark 1.3.7. Using exactly same argument, we can deduce the analogous result for spectral schemes. In
particular, every connective 0-localic spectral scheme represents a functor which carries discrete E∞-rings to
discrete spaces.
Proof of Proposition 1.3.3. The implication (1) ⇒(2) follows from Lemma 1.3.6. Assume now that (2) is
satisﬁed. Replacing OX by π0 OX, we may assume that OX is discrete. It follows from Theorem VII.8.42 that
there exists a 1-localic spectral Deligne-Mumford stack Y = (Y, OY) and a 2-connective object U ∈Y such
that X ≃(Y/U, OY |U). To prove that X is 1-localic, it will suﬃce to show that the object U is n-truncated
(Lemma 1.3.4). Let Y0 be the full subcategory of Y spanned by those objects Y ∈Y such that MapY(Y, U)
is n-truncated. We wish to show that Y0 = Y. Since Y0 is closed under small colimits in Y, it will suﬃce to
show that Y0 contains every object Y for which (Y/Y , OY |Y ) ≃Spec R is aﬃne (note that in this case, R is
automatically discrete). We now observe that MapY(Y, U) can be identiﬁed with the homotopy ﬁber of the
forgetful map
MapStk(Spec R, X) →MapStk(Spec R, Y).
Here MapStk(Spec R, X) is n-truncated by assumption (2), and MapStk(Spec R, Y) is 1-truncated by Lemma
1.3.6, so that the homotopy ﬁber is also n-truncated.
Corollary 1.3.8. Let X be a spectral algebraic space. Then X is 1-localic.
Remark 1.3.9. Suppose we are given a pullback diagram of connective spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks
X′
/

X

Y′
/ Y .
Assume that X and Y′ are spectral Deligne-Mumford n-stacks, and that Y is a spectral Deligne-Mumford
(n + 1)-stack. Then X′ is a spectral Deligne-Mumford n-stack. In particular, if Y′ and Y are aﬃne and X
is a spectral Deligne-Mumford n-stack, then X′ is a spectral Deligne-Mumford n-stack.

1.4
Quasi-Compactness of Spectral Deligne-Mumford Stacks
Let X = (X, OX) be a spectral scheme. We will say that X is quasi-compact if the ∞-topos X is quasi-
compact. Our goal in this section is to study quasi-compactness and related ﬁniteness conditions in the
setting of spectral algebraic geometry.
Deﬁnition 1.4.1. Let X = (X, OX) be a nonconnective spectral scheme or a nonconnective spectral Deligne-
Mumford stack, and let n ≥0 be an integer. We will say that X is n-quasi-compact if the ∞-topos X is
n-coherent (see §VII.3). We will say that X is quasi-compact if it is 0-quasi-compact. We will say that X is
∞-quasi-compact if it is n-quasi-compact for every integer n.
Proposition 1.4.2. Let A be an E∞-ring. Then the nonconnective spectral scheme SpecZar(A) and the
nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stack Spec´et(A) are ∞-quasi-compact.
Proof. Since the Grothendieck topologies on GnSp
´et
and GnSp
Zar are ﬁnitary, this follows from Proposition VII.3.19
together with the construction of spectra described in §V.2.2.
Corollary 1.4.3. Let (X, OX) be a nonconnective spectral scheme or a nonconnective spectral Deligne-
Mumford stack. Then the ∞-topos X is locally coherent.
Proposition 1.4.4. Let f : (X, OX) →(Y, OY) be a morphism of nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford
stacks (or nonconnective spectral schemes). Let n ≥0 be an integer. The following conditions are equivalent:
(1) For every n-coherent object U ∈Y, the pullback f ∗U is n-coherent.
(2) For every aﬃne object U ∈Y, the pullback f ∗U is an n-coherent object of X.
(3) There exists a full subcategory Y0 ⊆Y with the following properties:
(a) Each object U ∈Y0 is n-coherent.
(b) For each U ∈Y0, the pullback f ∗(U) is n-coherent.
(c) For each object Y ∈Y, there exists an eﬀective epimorphism ` Yi →Y , where each Yi ∈Y0.
Moreover, if n > 0, then these conditions imply:
(4) For every relatively (n −1)-coherent morphism u : U →Y in Y, the pullback f ∗(u) is a relatively
(n −1)-coherent morphism in X.
Proof. We proceed by induction on n. The implication (1) ⇒(2) is immediately from Proposition 1.4.2.
To see that (2) ⇒(3), we take Y0 to be the collection of all objects U ∈Y such that (Y/U, OY |U) is
aﬃne. We next show that (3) ⇒(4) if n > 0. Let u : U →Y be an (n −1)-coherent morphism in Y; we
wish to show that f ∗(u) is a relatively (n −1)-coherent morphism in X. Choose an eﬀective epimorphism
`
i∈I Yi →Y , where each Yi ∈Y. Using Corollary VII.3.11, we are reduced to proving that the induced map
f ∗(U ×Y
`
i∈I Yi) →f ∗(`
i∈I Yi) is relatively (n −1)-coherent. We may therefore replace Y by some Yi and
thereby reduce to the case where Y is n-coherent. Then U is (n −1)-coherent. Using (2) together with the
inductive hypothesis, we deduce that f ∗Y is n-coherent and that f ∗U is (n −1)-coherent, so that f ∗(u) is
relatively (n −1)-coherent as desired.
We now prove that (3) implies (1). Fix an n-coherent object U ∈Y; we wish to prove that f ∗(U) is
an n-coherent object of X. Choose an eﬀective epimorphism `
i∈I Ui →U where each Ui ∈Y0. Since U is
quasi-compact, we may assume without loss of generality that I is ﬁnite. Using (2) and Remark VII.3.5, we
conclude that ` f ∗(Ui) is an n-coherent object of X. Moreover, the map ` f ∗(Ui) →f ∗(U) is an eﬀective
epimorphism which is (n −1)-coherent if n > 0 (by virtue of (4)). Using Proposition VII.3.9 we conclude
that f ∗U is n-coherent as desired.

AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 19
Deﬁnition 1.4.5. Let f : (X, OX) →(Y, OY) be a morphism of nonconnective spectral schemes or noncon-
nective spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks. We will say that f is n-quasi-compact if it satisﬁes the equivalent
conditions (1) and (2) of Proposition 1.4.4. We will say that f is quasi-compact if it is 0-quasi-compact, and
∞-quasi-compact if it is n-quasi-compact for every integer n ≥0.
Example 1.4.6. Let f : X →Y be a map between aﬃne nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks
(or aﬃne nonconnective spectral schemes).
Then f is ∞-quasi-compact; this follows immediately from
Proposition 1.4.2.
Remark 1.4.7. Let f : X →Y be a map of nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks and let
0 ≤n ≤∞. The following conditions are equivalent:
(1) The map f is n-coherent.
(2) For every ´etale map Spec A →Y, the ﬁber product Spec A ×Y X is n-coherent.
Proposition 1.4.8. Let X be a quasi-compact nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stack, and let n > 0.
The following conditions on X are equivalent:
(1) For every pair of maps Spec A →X ←Spec B, the ﬁber product Spec A ×X Spec B is (n −1)-coherent.
(2) Every map f : Spec A →X is (n −1)-coherent.
(3) For every pair of maps Spec A →X
u←Spec B where u is ´etale, the ﬁber product Spec A ×X Spec B is
(n −1)-coherent.
(4) Every ´etale map f : Spec A →X is (n −1)-coherent.
(5) For every pair of ´etale maps Spec A →X ←Spec B, the ﬁber product Spec A ×X Spec B is (n −1)-
coherent.
(6) The nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stack X is n-coherent.
Proof. The equivalences (1) ⇔(2) and (3) ⇔(4) are obvious. The equivalences (2) ⇔(3) and (4) ⇔(5)
follow from Remark 1.4.7, and the equivalence (5) ⇔(6) follows from Corollary VII.3.10.

/f ∗X is an ´etale surjection of ∞-topoi. Assume that
m > 0. According to Corollary VII.3.10, it will suﬃce to show that if we are given aﬃne objects U, V ∈X/X,
then the ﬁber product U ×X V is (m −1)-coherent. By the inductive hypothesis, it suﬃces to show that
f ∗(U ×X V ) ≃f ∗U ×f ∗X f ∗V is (m −1)-coherent. This follows from the m-coherence of f ∗X, since f ∗U
and f ∗V are aﬃne.
20
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 21
We now discuss the relationship of Deﬁnition 1.4.1 with classical scheme theory.
Lemma 1.4.12. Let X be a topological space. The following conditions are equivalent:
(1) The ∞-topos Shv(X) is coherent.
(2) The ∞-topos Shv(X) is 1-coherent.
(3) The topological space X is coherent: that is, the collection of quasi-compact open subsets U ⊆X is
closed under ﬁnite intersections and forms a basis for the topology of X (in particular, X is quasi-
compact).
Proof. The implication (1) ⇒(2) is obvious. We prove that (2) ⇒(3). For each U ⊆X, let χU ∈Shv(X)
be the sheaf given by the formula
χU(V ) =
(
∆0
if V ⊆U
∅
otherwise.
We note that χU is a quasi-compact object of X if and only if U is quasi-compact as a topological space.
If Shv(X) is 1-coherent, then the collection of quasi-compact objects of Shv(X) are closed under products.
Since the construction U 7→χU carries ﬁnite intersections to ﬁnite products, we conclude that the collection
of quasi-compact open subsets of X is closed under ﬁnite intersections. We claim that the quasi-compact
open subsets form a basis for the topology of X. To prove this, choose an arbitrary open subset U ⊆X. Since
Shv(X) is 1-coherent, there exists an eﬀective epimorphism θ : ` Fi →χU, where each Fi is a quasi-compact
object of Shv(X). For each index i, we have τ≤−1 Fi ≃χUi for some open set Ui ⊆X. It follows that θ
induces an eﬀective epimorphism `
i χUi →χU, so that U = S Ui. We claim that each Ui is quasi-compact:
equivalently, each of the sheaves χUi is a quasi-compact object of Shv(X). This follows from the observation
that we have eﬀective epimorphisms Fi →χUi.
We now complete the proof by showing that (3) implies (1). Assume that X is a coherent topological
space. Let C ⊆Shv(X) be the full subcategory spanned by objects of the form χU, where U is a quasi-
compact open subset of X. Since the quasi-compact open subsets of X form a basis for the topology of X,
the ∞-category C generates Shv(X) under small colimits. It will therefore suﬃce to show that C consists
of coherent objects of Shv(X). We prove by induction on n that the objects of C are n-coherent. The case
n = 0 is clear. Assume that the objects of C are known to be n-coherent for n ≥0. We wish to prove that
if U ⊆X is a quasi-compact open subset, then χU is (n + 1)-coherent. According to Corollary VII.3.10, it
will suﬃce to show that for every pair of objects χV , χV ′ ∈C, every ﬁber product χV ×χU χV ′ is n-coherent.
Unwinding the deﬁnitions, this is equivalent to the statement that V ∩V ′ is quasi-compact, which follows
from our assumption that the quasi-compact open subsets of X are closed under ﬁnite intersections.
Proposition 1.4.13. Let (X, OX) be a 0-localic spectral scheme, so that X ≃Shv(X) for some topological
space X, and (X, π0 OX) is a scheme (see Proposition VII.2.37). Then:
(1) The spectral scheme (X, OX) is 0-quasi-compact if and only if the scheme (X, π0 OX) is quasi-compact.
(2) If 1 ≤n ≤∞, then (X, OX) is n-quasi-compact if and only if the scheme (X, π0 OX) is quasi-compact
and quasi-separated.
Proof. Assertion (1) follows from the observation that a topological space X is quasi-compact if and only if
the ∞-topos Shv(X) is quasi-compact. Assertion (2) follows from Lemma 1.4.12.
Proposition 1.4.14. Let (X, OX) be a spectral scheme and let (Y, OY) = Spec´et
Zar(X, OX) be the associated
spectral Deligne-Mumford stack. Let 0 ≤n ≤∞. The following conditions are equivalent:
(1) The spectral scheme (X, OX) is n-quasi-compact.
(2) The spectral Deligne-Mumford stack (Y, OY) is n-quasi-compact.

AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 22
Proof. We proceed by induction on n; when n = 0, the desired result follows from Lemma VII.9.7 (see
Remark VII.3.2).
Assume ﬁrst that n > 0 and that Y is n-coherent; we wish to prove that X is n-coherent. The inductive
hypothesis shows that an object U ∈X is (n −1)-coherent if and only if f ∗U is an (n −1)-coherent object
of Y. Since f ∗commutes with ﬁnite products and the collection of (n −1)-coherent objects of Y is closed
under ﬁnite products, we conclude that the collection of (n −1)-coherent objects of X is closed under ﬁnite
products. Since X is locally coherent, it follows immediately that X is n-coherent.
We next prove that if n > 0, then f ∗carries relatively (n −1)-coherent morphisms in X to relatively
(n −1)-coherent morphisms in Y. Suppose that u : U →X is a relatively (n −1)-coherent morphism in X.
We wish to prove that f ∗(u) is a relatively (n−1)-coherent morphism in Y. Choose an eﬀective epimorphism
` Xi →X, where each (X/Xi, OX |Xi) is aﬃne. According to Corollary VII.3.11, it will suﬃce to show that
the induced map
f ∗(U ×X
a
Xi) →f ∗(
a
Xi)
is relatively n-coherent. We may therefore replace X by some Xi and thereby reduce to the case where
(X/X, OX |X) is aﬃne. Since u is relatively (n −1)-coherent, it follows that U is (n −1)-coherent. By the
inductive hypothesis, f ∗U is an (n −1)-coherent object of Y. Since (Y/f ∗X, OY |f ∗X) is aﬃne, f ∗X is an
n-coherent object of Y (Proposition 1.4.2), so the map f ∗U →f ∗X is relatively (n −1)-coherent as desired.
We now claim that if n > 0 and X is n-coherent, then Y is n-coherent. We note that X is quasi-compact,
so there exists an eﬀective epimorphism u : `

(n−1)-coherent, so that the map u is relatively (n−1)-coherent. The above argument shows that f ∗(u) is a
relatively (n−1)-coherent eﬀective epimorphism. Since f ∗(U) ≃`

1.4.2 again), we can invoke Proposition VII.3.9 to conclude that f ∗1 is an n-coherent object of Y: that is,
the ∞-topos Y is n-coherent.
To simplify the notation, we will now conﬁne our attention to the quasi-compactness of spectral Deligne-
Mumford stacks. The results below have analogues in the setting of spectral schemes, which can be proven
either directly (using the same arguments) or by invoking Proposition 1.4.14.
Proposition 1.4.15. Let f : X →Y be a map of nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks and let
0 ≤n ≤∞. Then:
(1) If Y is n-quasi-compact and f is n-quasi-compact, then X is n-quasi-compact.
(2) If X is n-quasi-compact and Y is (n + 1)-quasi-compact, then f is n-quasi-compact.
Proof. We proceed by induction on n. We begin with assertion (1). Assume that f and Y are n-quasi-
compact; we wish to prove that X is n-quasi-compact. Choose an ´etale surjection Spec R →Y. Then the
ﬁber product X′ = Spec R ×Y X is n-coherent. We have an ´etale surjection X′ →X, so that X is quasi-
compact. This completes the proof when n = 0. Assume now that n > 0. In view of Proposition 1.4.8, it
will suﬃce to show that every map Spec A →X is (n −1)-quasi-compact. Using Proposition 1.4.11, we are
reduced to showing that the induced map
u : Spec R ×Y Spec A →X′
is (n −1)-quasi-compact.
Since X′ is n-quasi-compact and Spec R ×Y Spec A is (n −1)-quasi-compact
(using Proposition 1.4.8 and the n-quasi-compact of Y), the (n −1)-quasi-compactness of u follows from the
inductive hypothesis.
We now prove (2). Assume that X is n-quasi-compact and that Y is (n + 1)-quasi-compact. We wish
to show that for every map Spec R →Y, the ﬁber product X′ = Spec R ×Y X is n-quasi-compact. By (1),
it will suﬃce to show that the projection map X′ →X is n-quasi-compact. This follows from Proposition
1.4.11, since the map Spec R →Y is n-quasi-compact by Corollary 1.4.6.
22
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 23
Corollary 1.4.16. Suppose we are given maps f : X →Y and g : Y →Z of nonconnective spectral
Deligne-Mumford stacks. Then:
(i) If f and g are n-quasi-compact, then g ◦f is n-quasi-compact.
(ii) If g is (n + 1)-quasi-compact and g ◦f is n-quasi-compact, then f is n-quasi-compact.
Corollary 1.4.17. Let Z be a quasi-compact nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stack and let n ≥0.
Then Z is (n + 1)-quasi-compact if and only if the following condition is satisﬁed: for every pair of maps
X →Z ←Y where X and Y are n-quasi-compact, the ﬁber product X ×Z Y is n-quasi-compact.
Proof. The “if” direction follows immediately from Proposition 1.4.8 (take X and Y to be aﬃne). Conversely,
suppose that Z is (n + 1)-quasi-compact. If X is n-quasi-compact, then the map X →Z is n-quasi-compact.
It follows from Proposition 1.4.11 that the projection map X ×Z Y →Y is n-quasi-compact. Since Y is also
n-quasi-compact, Proposition 1.4.15 implies that X ×Z Y is n-quasi-compact.
Corollary 1.4.18. The collection of ∞-quasi-compact nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks is
closed under the formation of ﬁber products.
1.5
Local Properties of Spectral Deligne-Mumford Stacks
In this section, we study properties of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks X which are of a local nature on X,
in that they can be tested after passing to any ´etale covering of X.
Deﬁnition 1.5.1. Let f : X →Y = (Y, O) be an ´etale morphism of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks.
We will say that a collection of ´etale morphisms {fα : Xα →Y} is jointly surjective if the induced map
`
α Xα →Y is surjective (see Deﬁnition 1.1.21).
Deﬁnition 1.5.2. Let P be a property of nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks. We will say that
P is local for the ´etale topology if the following conditions hold:
(i) For every ´etale morphism of nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks f : X →Y, if Y has the
property P, then X also has the property P.
(ii) Given a jointly surjective collection of ´etale maps {Xα →Y}, if each Xα has the property P, then Y
has the property P.
Remark 1.5.3. Let P be a property of nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks which is local for
the ´etale topology. A nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stack (X, OX) has the property P if and only
if, for every aﬃne U ∈X, the aﬃne nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stack (X/U, OX |U) has the
property P. Consequently, P is determined by the full subcategory CAlg(P) ⊆CAlg spanned by those
E∞-rings A such that Spec A has the property P. Using the fact that P satisﬁes ´etale descent, we deduce
that CAlg(P) has the following properties:
(i) If f : A →A′ is an ´etale morphism of E∞-rings and A ∈CAlg(P), then A′ ∈CAlg(P).
(ii) Given a ﬁnite collection of ´etale maps {A →Aα} such that A →Q
α Aα is faithfully ﬂat, if each
Aα ∈CAlg(P), then A ∈CAlg(P).
Conversely, given a full subcategory CAlg(P) ⊆CAlg satisfying conditions (i) and (ii), we obtain a property
P of nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks as follows: a pair (X, OX) has the property P if and
only if, whenever we have an equivalence (X/U, OX |U) ≃Spec A, the E∞-ring A belongs to CAlg(P).
Recall that an E∞-ring A is said to be Noetherian if A is connective, π0A is a Noetherian commutative
ring, and πnA is a ﬁnitely generated module over π0A for every integer n.
23
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 24
Deﬁnition 1.5.4. Let X = (X, OX) be a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack. We will say that X is locally
Noetherian if, whenever U ∈X is aﬃne so that (X/U, OX |U) ≃Spec A, the E∞-ring A is Noetherian.
Proposition 1.5.5. The property of being a locally Noetherian spectral Deligne-Mumford stack is local for
the ´etale topology.
Lemma 1.5.6. Let f : A →B be a faithfully ﬂat map of E∞-rings. If B is Noetherian, then A is Noetherian.
Proof. We ﬁrst show that π0A is a Noetherian commutative ring. We claim that the collection of ideals
in π0A satisﬁes the ascending chain condition. To prove this, it will suﬃce to show that the construction
I 7→Iπ0B determines an injection from the partially ordered set of ideals of π0A to the partially ordered
set of ideals of π0B. Since π0B is ﬂat over A, the map I ⊗π0A π0B is an injection with image Iπ0B. Given
a pair of ideals I, J ⊆π0A we have an exact sequence of π0A-modules
0 →I ∩J →I ⊕J →I + J →0.
This sequence remains exact after tensoring with π0B, so that (I ∩J)π0B = Iπ0B ∩Jπ0B. It follows that
if Iπ0B = Jπ0B, then the inclusion (I ∩J)π0B ,→Iπ0B is bijective, so that I/(I ∩J) ⊗π0A π0B ≃0. Since
π0B is faithfully ﬂat over π0A, this implies that I/(I ∩J) = 0, so that I ⊆J. A similar argument shows
that J ⊆I, so that I = J. This completes the proof that π0A is a Noetherian commutative ring.
Since f is faithfully ﬂat, we have πnB ≃πnA ⊗π0A π0B. Since πnB ≃0 for n < 0, the faithful ﬂatness of
π0B over π0A implies that πnA ≃0. This proves that A is connective. To complete the proof, we must show
that each πnA is ﬁnitely generated as a module over π0A. Since πnB is ﬁnitely generated as a π0B-module,
we can choose a ﬁnitely generated submodule M ⊆πnA such that the map
M ⊗π0A π0B →πnA ⊗π0A π0B ≃πnB
is surjective. The cokernel of this map is given by (πnA)/M ⊗π0A πnB. Since π0B is faithfully ﬂat over π0A,
we deduce that (πnA)/M ≃0, so that πnA ≃M is ﬁnitely generated as a module over π0A.
Proof of Proposition 1.5.5. In view of Remark 1.5.3, it will suﬃce to prove the following assertions:
(i) If f : A →A′ is an ´etale morphism of E∞-rings and A is Noetherian, then A′ is also Noetherian.
(ii) Given a ﬁnite collection of ´etale maps {A →Aα} such that A →Q
α Aα is faithfully ﬂat, if each Aα is
Noetherian, then A is Noetherian.
Assertion (i) is obvious, and assertion (ii) follows immediately from Lemma 1.5.6.
We now turn our attention to properties of morphisms.
Deﬁnition 1.5.7. Let P be a property of morphisms between nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford
stacks. We will say that P is local on the source with respect to the ´etale topology. if the following conditions
hold:
(i) For every composable pair of morphisms
X
f→Y
g→Z,
if f is ´etale and g has the property P, then g ◦f has the property P.
(ii) Given a jointly surjective collection of ´etale maps {fα : Xα →Y} and a morphism g : Y →Z, if each
of the composite maps g ◦fα has the property P, then g has the property P.
Example 1.5.8. Let P be the property of being an ´etale morphism between nonconnective spectral Deligne-
Mumford stacks. Then P is local on the source with respect to the ´etale topology.

Lemma 1.5.10. Let φ : A →B be an ´etale morphism of E∞-rings, and let M be a B-module spectrum. If
M is ﬂat over A, then it is ﬂat over B.
Proof. If M is ﬂat over A, then the tensor product B ⊗A M ≃(B ⊗A B) ⊗B M is ﬂat over B. Since φ is
´etale, B is a retract of B ⊗A B, so that M ≃B ⊗B M is a retract of (B ⊗A B) ⊗B M and therefore also ﬂat
over B.

(1) The map φ is ﬂat.
(2) The map φ induces a ﬂat morphism Spec B →Spec A of nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford
stacks.

Proposition 1.5.12. The property of being a ﬂat morphism (between nonconnective spectral Deligne-

Remark 1.5.14. Let k be a commutative ring, regarded as a discrete E∞-ring. Let (X, OX) be a nonconnec-
tive spectral Deligne-Mumford stack over k. We say that (X, OX) is ﬂat over k if the map (X, OX) →Spec k
is ﬂat, in the sense of Deﬁnition 1.5.9. In this case, the structure sheaf OX is automatically discrete. Suppose
that X is 1-localic and OX is discrete, so we may identify (X, OX) with an ordinary Deligne-Mumford stack
X over k (Proposition VII.8.36). Then (X, OX) is ﬂat over k if and only if X is ﬂat over k (in the sense
of classical algebraic geometry). Consequently, Proposition VII.8.36 yields an equivalence between the ∞-
category of 1-localic spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks which are ﬂat over k and the ∞-category of ordinary
Deligne-Mumford stacks which are ﬂat over k.

Remark 1.5.17. Let k′ be an E∞-ring and let k = τ≥0k′ be its connective cover. The proof of Proposition

1.5.17). Consequently, it suﬃces to show that the unit and counit transformations

(Y, OY) →GF(X, OX)
are equivalences whenever (X, OX) is a nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stack which is ﬂat over B
or (Y, OY) is a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack which is ﬂat over A. These assertions are local on X and Y;
we may therefore reduce to the aﬃne case, where the desired result follows from Proposition A.7.2.2.24.
We now introduce an analogue of Deﬁnition 1.5.2 for the ﬂat topology.
Deﬁnition 1.5.20. Let f : X →Y be a map of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks. We will say that f is a
ﬂat covering if the following conditions are satisﬁed:
27
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 28
(1) The map f is ﬂat.
(2) For every quasi-compact open substack V ,→Y, there exists a quasi-compact open substack U ,→V
such that f induces a surjection U →V.
Example 1.5.21. Let f : X →Y be an ´etale surjection.
Then f is a ﬂat covering.
Condition (1) of
Deﬁnition 1.5.20 is obvious. To prove (2), we ﬁrst replace Y by V and thereby reduce to the case where
Y is quasi-compact. Choose an ´etale surjection Spec A →Y. Write Spec A ×Y X as (X, OX). For every
aﬃne object X ∈X, we can write (X/X, OX |X) ≃Spec B for some ´etale A-algebra B, so that the map
SpecZ B →SpecZ A has image given by some open subset UX ⊆SpecZ A (Proposition VII.0.2). Since f
is surjective, the open sets UX cover SpecZ A. Since SpecZ A is quasi-compact, this open cover has a ﬁnite
subcover. Taking the disjoint union of the corresponding objects of X, we obtain an aﬃne object X ∈X
such that the induced map (X/X, OX |X) →Spec A is surjective. Since X is quasi-compact, the image of
(X/X, OX |X) in X is a quasi-compact open substack U ⊆X having the desired properties.
Deﬁnition 1.5.22. Let P be a property of nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks. We will say
that P is local for the fpqc topology if the following conditions hold:
(i) For every ﬂat morphism of nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks f : X →Y, if Y has the
property P, then X also has the property P.
(ii) Given a collection of ﬂat morphisms {Xα →Y}, if each Xα has the property P and the induced map
` Xα →Y is a ﬂat covering, then Y has the property P.
Proposition 1.5.23. Let P be a property of nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks. Then P is
local for the fpqc topology if and only if the following conditions are satisﬁed:
(1) The property P is local for the ´etale topology (Deﬁnition 1.5.22).
(2) If f : A →B is a ﬂat morphism of E∞-rings such that Spec A has the property P, then Spec B has the
property P. The converse holds provided that f is faithfully ﬂat.
Proof. If P is local for the fpqc topology, then condition (2) is obvious and condition (1) follows from
Example 1.5.21. Conversely, suppose that conditions (1) and (2) are satisﬁed. We ﬁrst verify condition (i)
of Deﬁnition 1.5.22. Let f : X →Y be a ﬂat morphism of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks, and assume
that Y has the property P. We wish to show that X has the property P. By virtue of assumption (1), this
condition is local with respect to the ´etale topology on X. We may therefore assume that X ≃Spec B and
that the map f factors as a composition
X →Spec A
f ′′
→Y
where f ′′ is ´etale. Then Spec A has the property P. Since f is ﬂat, B is ﬂat over A. It then follows from (2)
that X ≃Spec B has the property P, as desired.
We now verify condition (ii). Let f : X →Y be a ﬂat covering and suppose that X has the property P;
we wish to show that Y has the property P. The assertion is local with respect to the ´etale topology on Y,
so we may suppose that Y ≃Spec A is aﬃne. In particular, Y is quasi-compact. Replacing X by an open
substack if necessary (and using (1)), we can reduce to the case where X is quasi-compact. We can then
choose an ´etale surjection Spec B →X. Then Spec B has the property P (by (1)) and B is faithfully ﬂat
over A, so that Spec A has the property P by (2).
Example 1.5.24. Let P be the property of being a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack, so that a nonconnective
spectral Deligne-Mumford stack (X, OX) has the property P if and only if the structure sheaf OX is connective.
Then P is local with respect to the fpqc topology.
Example 1.5.25. Let P be the property of being an n-truncated spectral Deligne-Mumford stack, so that
(X, OX) has the property P if and only if the structure sheaf OX is connective and n-truncated. Then P is
local with respect to the fpqc topology.
28
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 29
Deﬁnition 1.5.26. Let P be a property of morphisms between nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford
stacks. We will say that P is local on the source with respect to the fpqc topology if the following conditions
hold:
(i) For every composable pair of morphisms

g→Z,
if f is ﬂat and g has the property P, then g ◦f has the property P.
(ii) Given a collection of ﬂat morphisms {fα : Xα →Y} which determine a ﬂat covering `

morphism g : Y →Z, if each of the composite maps g ◦fα has the property P, then g has the property
P.
Proposition 1.5.23 has the following analogue for properties of morphisms between nonconnective spectral
Deligne-Mumford stacks, which is proven in the same way:
Proposition 1.5.27. Let P be a property of morphisms between nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford
stacks. Then P is local on the source for the fpqc topology if and only if the following conditions are satisﬁed:
(1) The property P is local on the source for the ´etale topology (Deﬁnition 1.5.22).
(2) Suppose we are given a pair of maps

g→Z

if B is faithfully ﬂat over A.
Example 1.5.28. The property of being a ﬂat morphism is local with respect to the fpqc topology. In
view of Proposition 1.5.12, it will suﬃce to show that the property of ﬂatness satisﬁes condition (2) of
Proposition 1.5.27. The ﬁrst assertion is obvious (since the collection of ﬂat morphisms is closed under
composition, by Remark 1.5.16). Conversely, suppose that B is faithfully ﬂat over A and that we are given
a map g : Spec A →Z such that the composite map Spec B →Spec A →Z is ﬂat. We wish to show that g
is ﬂat. This follows immediately from the deﬁnitions, together with Lemma 1.5.13.
2
Quasi-Coherent Sheaves
Let X = (X, O) be a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack. In this section, we will introduce the notion of a quasi-
coherent sheaf on X. The collection of quasi-coherent sheaves on X is naturally organized into a ∞-category,
which we will denote by QCoh(X). The ∞-category QCoh(X) is our principal object of study in this paper.
We begin in a more general setting. Let (X, O) be an arbitrary spectrally ringed ∞-topos. In §2.1, we
will introduce a stable ∞-category ModO, whose objects are O-module objects in the ∞-category Stab(X) ≃
ShvSp(X) of sheaves of spectra on X. Our main result (Proposition 2.1.8) asserts that if O is discrete and
X is 1-localic, then ModO contains D+(A) as a full subcategory, where A is the abelian category of discrete
sheaves of O-modules on X.
Suppose now that X = (X, O) is a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack. In §2.3, we deﬁne a full subcategory
QCoh(X) ⊆ModO, which we call the ∞-category of quasi-coherent sheaves on X. It is uniquely characterized
by the following pair of assertions:
(a) Let F ∈ModO be a sheaf of O-modules on X. Then the condition that F be quasi-coherent is of a local
nature on X. In particular, F is quasi-coherent if and only if, for every aﬃne U ∈X, the restriction
F |U is a quasi-coherent sheaf on the spectral Deligne-Mumford stack (X/U, O |U) (Remark 2.3.7).
29
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 30
(b) Suppose that X = Spec A is aﬃne. Then the global sections functor Γ : ModO →ModA admits a
fully faithful left adjoint, whose essential image is the full subcategory QCoh(X) ⊆ModO (Proposition
2.3.11).
The class of quasi-coherent sheaves can be given an alternative characterization using the language of ge-
ometries developed in [42]. We will describe the relevant geometries in §2.2. This material is of a somewhat
technical nature (and is not really needed in the rest of the paper); the reader should feel free to skip over
it.
Recall that a scheme X is said to be quasi-aﬃne if X is quasi-compact and there exists an open immersion
of X into an aﬃne scheme. This notion has an obvious generalization to the setting of spectral algebraic
geometry: we say that a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack X is quasi-aﬃne it is equivalent to a quasi-compact
open substack of an aﬃne spectral Deligne-Mumford stack. In §2.4, we will study the class of quasi-aﬃne
spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks. Our main results are that a quasi-aﬃne spectral Deligne-Mumford stack
X = (X, O) can be recovered from the E∞-ring of global sections A = Γ(X; O) (Proposition 2.4.9), and that
the global sections functor induces an equivalence of stable ∞-categories QCoh(X) →ModA (Proposition
2.4.4).
Let f : (X, OX) →(Y, OY) be a map of spectrally ringed ∞-topoi. Then f determines a pair of adjoint
functors
ModOY
f ∗
/ModOX .
f∗
o
If X = (X, OX) and Y = (Y, OY) are spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks, then the pullback functor f ∗carries
quasi-coherent sheaves on Y to quasi-coherent sheaves on X. In §2.5, we will study conditions on f which
guarantee that the pushforward f∗also preserves quasi-coherence.
In particular, we will show that f∗
preserves coherence when the morphism f is quasi-aﬃne (Corollary 2.5.15). We will deduce this from a more
general statement (Proposition 2.5.12) which we will later use to prove analogous results in the setting of
quasi-compact, quasi-separated spectral algebraic spaces.
Let X be a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack and let F be a quasi-coherent sheaf on X.
In §2.6, we
will study properties of the quasi-coherent sheaf F which can be tested locally on X. We are particularly
interested in ﬁniteness conditions on F. For example, when X is locally Noetherian (Deﬁnition 1.5.4), we
will show that there is a well-behaved full subcategory Coh(X) ⊆QCoh(X), which we call the ∞-category of
coherent sheaves on X (Deﬁnition 2.6.20).
Let X be a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack.
Then X determines a functor X : CAlgcn →S, given
informally by X(R) = MapStk(Spec R, X). In §V.2.4, we saw that X is determined by the functor X, up to
canonical equivalence. In §2.7, we will explain how to reconstruct the ∞-category QCoh(X) directly from
the functor X. More precisely, we associate to any functor X : CAlgcn →S an ∞-category QCoh(X);
roughly speaking, an object F ∈QCoh(X) is a rule which associates to each point η ∈X(R) an R-module
η∗F, depending functorially on R. Our main result (Proposition 2.7.18) asserts that if X is represented by
a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack X, then the ∞-categories QCoh(X) and QCoh(X) are equivalent to one
another. Consequently, we may view the construction X 7→QCoh(X) as a reasonable extrapolation of the
theory of quasi-coherent sheaves to the setting of non-representable functors.
2.1
Sheaves on a Spectrally Ringed ∞-Topos
Let X be a topological space and let O be a sheaf of commutative rings on X. A sheaf of O-modules is a sheaf
of abelian groups F on X such that F(U) is equipped with the structure of a module over the commutative
ring O(U) for every open subset U ⊆X, which depends functorially on U. Our goal in this section is to
introduce an ∞-categorical analogue of the theory of sheaves of modules. We will replace the topological
space X with an arbitrary ∞-topos X, and O by an arbitrary sheaf of E∞-rings on X.
Deﬁnition 2.1.1. Let X be an ∞-topos and let O ∈ShvCAlg(X) be a sheaf of E∞-rings on X. Recall that
O can be identiﬁed with a commutative algebra object of the symmetric monoidal ∞-category ShvSp(X) of
30
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 31
sheaves of spectra on X (see §VII.9.25). We let ModO denote the ∞-category ModO(ShvSp(X)) of O-module
objects of ShvSp(X). Then ModO can be regarded as a symmetric monoidal ∞-category with respect to the
relative tensor product ⊗O (see §A.3.4.4). We will refer to the objects of ModO as sheaves of O-modules on
X, or sometimes just as O-modules.
Warning 2.1.2. Let X be a topological space and let O be a sheaf of commutative rings on X. Then we can
identify O with a sheaf of E∞-rings on the ∞-topos Shv(X). In this case, Deﬁnition 2.1.1 does not recover
the classical theory of sheaves of O-modules on X, because we allow ourselves to consider sheaves of spectra
rather than sheaves of abelian groups. In a moment, we will prove that the ∞-category ModO is stable and
equipped with a natural t-structure (Proposition 2.1.3). The classical theory of sheaves of O-modules can be
recovered as the abelian category Mod♥
O. Moreover, the ∞-category ModO is closely related to the derived
∞-category of the abelian category Mod♥
O (see Proposition 2.1.8).
We now summarize some of the formal properties of Deﬁnition 2.1.1.
Proposition 2.1.3. Let X be an ∞-topos and O a sheaf of E∞-rings on X. Then:
(1) The ∞-category ModO is stable.
(2) The ∞-category ModO is presentable and the tensor product ⊗O : ModO × ModO →ModO preserves
small colimits separately in each variable.
(3) The forgetful functor θ : ModO →ShvSp(X) is conservative and preserves small limits and colimits.
Suppose that O is connective. Then:
(4) The ∞-category ModO admits a t-structure, deﬁned by (ModO)≥0 = θ−1 ShvSp(X)≥0 and (ModO)≤0 =
θ−1 ShvSp(X)≤0.
(5) The t-structure on ModO is accessible: that is, the ∞-category (ModO)≥0 is presentable (see Proposition
A.1.4.5.13).
(6) The t-structure on ModO is compatible with the symmetric monoidal structure on ModO. In other
words, (ModO)≥0 contains the unit object of ModO and is stable under tensor product.
(7) The t-structure on ModO is compatible with ﬁltered colimits: in other words, (ModO)≤0 is stable under
ﬁltered colimits in ModO.
(8) The t-structure on ModO is right complete.
Proof. Assertion (1) follows from Proposition A.7.1.1.4, assertion (2) follows from Theorem A.3.4.4.2, and
assertion (3) follows from Corollaries A.3.4.3.2 and A.3.4.4.6. Suppose that O is connective. We observe that
(ModO)≥0 can be identiﬁed with the ∞-category of O-module objects of ShvSp(X)≥0, so that (5) follows
from Theorem A.3.4.4.2. Assume (4) for the moment. To prove (6), we note that the unit object of ModO
has image O ∈ShvSp(X)≥0 ⊆ShvSp(X), and that for every pair of objects F, G ∈ModO, the image in
ModSp(X)≥0 of the relative tensor product F ⊗O G can be identiﬁed with the geometric realization of a
simplicial object whose entires are tensor products F ⊗O ⊗· · · ⊗O ⊗G. Since F, G, and O are connective,
the above tensor product is connective (Proposition VII.1.16); because ModSp(X)≥0 is closed under colimits
we conclude that F ⊗O G is connective. Assertion (7) follows from (3) together Proposition VII.1.7, and
assertion (8) follows from (7) and Proposition A.1.2.1.19.
It remains to prove (4). Since the full subcategory ShvSp(X)≥0 ⊆ShvSp(X) is closed under small colimits
and extensions, we conclude that (ModO)≥0 is closed under small colimits and extensions in ModO (since the
forgetful functor θ preserves small colimits, by (3)). Using Proposition A.1.4.5.11, we deduce the existence
of an accessible t-structure ((ModO)≥0, Mod′
O) on ModO. To complete the proof, it will suﬃce to show that
Mod′
O = (ModO)≤0. Suppose ﬁrst that F ∈Mod′
O. Then the mapping space MapModO(G[1], F) is contractible
for every object G ∈(ModO)≥0. In particular, for every connective sheaf of spectra M ∈ShvSp(X)≥0, the
31
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 32
mapping space MapModO((M ⊗O)[1], F) ≃MapShvSp(X)(M[1], θ(F)) vanishes, so that θ(F) ∈ShvSp(X)≤0
and therefore F ∈(ModO)≤0.
Conversely, suppose that F ∈(ModO)≤0. We wish to prove that F ∈Mod′
O. Let C denote the full
subcategory of ModO spanned by those objects G ∈ModO for which the mapping space MapModO(G[1], F)
is contractible. We wish to prove that C contains (ModO)≥0. Condition (3) shows that θ induces a functor
(ModO)≥0 →ShvSp(X)≥0which is conservative and preserves small colimits; moreover, this functor has a
left adjoint F, given informally by the formula F(M) ≃O ⊗M. Using Proposition A.6.2.2.11, we conclude
that (ModO)≥0 is generated under the formation of geometric realizations by the essential image of F. Since
C is stable under colimits, it will suﬃce to show that C contains the essential image of F. Unwinding the
deﬁnitions, we are reduced to proving that the mapping space
MapModO(F(M)[1], F) ≃MapShvSp(X)(M[1], θ(F))
is contractible for every connective sheaf of spectra M on X, which is equivalent to our assumption that
θ(F) ∈ShvSp(X)≤0.
Remark 2.1.4. Let X be an ∞-topos and let O be a connective sheaf of E∞-rings on X. We will say a sheaf
of O-modules M is connective if it is connective as an object of ShvSp(X): that is, if it belongs to (ModO)≥0.
We will often denote the ∞-category (ModO)≥0 by Modcn
O .
Remark 2.1.5. Let X be an ∞-topos and let O be a connective sheaf of E∞-rings on X. Then we can
identify π0 O with a commutative ring object in the underlying topos of X. Unwinding the deﬁnitions, we
can identify the heart Mod♥

of (π0 O)-module objects in the underlying topos of X.
Warning 2.1.6. The t-structure of Proposition 2.1.3 is generally not left-complete.

Lemma 2.1.9. Let X be an ∞-topos, O a connective sheaf of E∞-rings on X, and X a discrete object of X.

2.2
Module Geometries
Let (X, O) be a spectrally ringed ∞-topos. In §2.1, we studied the ∞-category ModO of sheaves of O-module
spectra on X. In this section, we will study the dependence of this ∞-category on the pair (X, O). We begin
with a simple observation.
Remark 2.2.1. Let Mod = Mod(Sp) denote the ∞-category of pairs (A, M), where A is an E∞-ring and
M is an A-module spectrum. There are evident forgetful functors

(∗) The ∞-category Mod admits small limits. Moreover, a diagram f : K◁→Mod is a limit diagram

functors p and q preserve small limits.
Let X be an ∞-topos. Assertion (∗) implies that the canonical isomorphism of simplicial sets

In other words, we may identify a Mod-valued sheaf on X with a pair (O, F), where O is a sheaf of E∞-rings
on X and F is a O-module. To exploit this observation, we need a few simple facts about compact objects
of the ∞-category Mod.

Proposition 2.2.2. The ∞-category Mod is compactly generated. Moreover, an object (A, M) ∈Mod is
compact if and only if the following pair of conditions is satisﬁed:
(1) The E∞-ring A is a compact object of CAlg.
(2) The A-module M is perfect (that is, M is a compact object of ModA; see §A.7.2.5).
The proof of Proposition 2.2.2 requires the following preliminary.

Proof of Proposition 2.2.2. Let Sp
