p admits a left adjoint, given informally by the formula X 7→(S, X); here S denotes the sphere spectrum.
Since p preserves ﬁltered colimits, this left adjoint carries compact objects of Sp to compact objects of Mod;
in particular, we deduce that the object (S, S[n]) ∈Mod is compact for every integer n. Similarly, q has a
left adjoint, given informally by the formula A 7→(A, 0). Since q preserves small colimits, we deduce that
the object (A, 0) ∈Mod is compact whenever A is a compact E∞-ring.
Fix a compact E∞-ring A, and let C ⊆ModA denote the full subcategory spanned by those A-modules
M such that (A, M) is a compact object of Mod. The functor (A, M) 7→(A, M[−1]) is a self-equivalence of
Mod, and therefore carries compact objects to compact objects. It follows that C is closed under translation
in ModA. Using Propositions T.4.3.1.10, we conclude that every pushout diagram in ModA is a q-pushout
diagram in Mod and therefore a pushout diagram in Mod. Since the collection of compact objects in Mod
is stable under pushouts, we deduce that C is stable under pushouts in Mod. It follows that C is a stable
subcategory of ModA, which is obviously stable under retracts. Since the collection of compact objects
in Mod is stable under coproducts, we conclude that (S, S) `(A, 0) ≃(A, A) is compact, so that A ∈C.

It follows that C contains all perfect A-modules (see Deﬁnition A.7.2.5.1). This proves that every object
(A, M) ∈Mod satisfying conditions (1) and (2) is compact.

deduce from Corollary T.5.5.2.9 (and Remark T.5.5.2.10) that F admits a right adjoint G. To prove that F
is fully faithful it suﬃces to show that G is conservative. Let α : (A, M) →(B, N) be a morphism in Mod
such that G(α) is an equivalence. In particular, we deduce that
MapMod((C, 0), (A, M)) ≃MapAlg(C, A) →MapAlg(C, B) ≃MapMod((C, 0), (B, N))
is an equivalence for every compact object C ∈CAlg; since CAlg is compactly generated, it follows that α
induces an equivalence A ≃B. Similarly, we have
MapMod((S, S[n]), (A, M)) ≃Ω∞+nM →Ω∞+nN ≃MapMod((S, S[n]), (B, N))
for every integer n, so that α induces an equivalence of spectra M ≃N. It follows that α is an equivalence,
as desired.

Proposition 2.2.6. Let G be a geometry, and let q : G′ →G be a Cartesian ﬁbration of ∞-categories.

images {q(fα) : q(Uα) →q(X)} is an admissible covering in G. Then the collection of admissible morphisms
and admissible coverings exhibit G′ as a geometry.

that Ω∞M →Ω∞N is an equivalence so that M ≃N.
There is an evident Cartesian ﬁbration GM
disc →GSp
disc which satisﬁes the hypotheses of Proposition 2.2.7.
Consequently, every structure of geometry on the ∞-category GSp
disc determines a structure of geometry on
GM
disc. In particular, the geometries GSp
Zar and GSp
´et deﬁned in §VII.2 and §VII.8 determine geometries GM
Zar
and GM
´et. Using Proposition 2.2.7 and Remark 2.2.1, we can identify LTop(GM
Zar)op and LTop(GM
´et)op with
the full subcategories of RingTopM
Zar and RingTopM
´et spanned by those triples (X, O, F), where O and F are
connective as sheaves of spectra on X.
We conclude this section by showing that the geometries GM
Zar and GM
´et can be described as geometric
envelopes. First, we treat the analogous question for the discrete topology.
Proposition 2.2.10. The ∞-category Mod≥0 of Example 2.2.9 is generated by compact projective objects.
Moreover, an object (A, M) ∈Modcn is compact and projective if and only if A is a compact projective object
of the ∞-category CAlgcn of connective E∞-rings, and M is a ﬁnitely generated projective A-module (that
is, a retract of an A-module of the form An; see Proposition A.7.2.5.20).
Proof. We have an evident forgetful functor qcn : Modcn →Algcn, which is a restriction of the forgetful
functor q : Mod →CAlg of Remark 2.2.1 and therefore preserves small colimits (Lemma 2.2.3). It follows
that the left adjoint A 7→(A, 0) of qcn carries compact projective objects of CAlgcn to compact projective
objects of Modcn. Similarly, the forgetful functor pcn : Modcn →Spcn preserves sifted colimits (Lemma 2.2.3)
so its left adjoint M 7→(S, M) preserves compact projective objects (here S denotes the sphere spectrum):
that is, the objects (S, Sn) ∈Modcn are compact and projective.
Since compact projective objects are
stable under ﬁnite coproducts, we deduce that if A ∈CAlgcn is compact and projective, then the coproduct
(A, 0) `(S, Sn) ≃(A, An) is compact and projective in Modcn. If M is a ﬁnitely generated projective A-
module, then it is a retract of An for some integer n, so that (A, M) is also a compact projective object of
Modcn.
Let C denote the full subcategory of Modcn spanned by objects of the form (A, Am), where A is a free
algebra of the form S{x1, . . . , xn}. Then C admits small coproducts and consists of compact projective
objects of Modcn, so Proposition T.5.5.8.22 shows that the inclusion C ⊆Modcn extends to a fully faithful
embedding F : PΣ(C) →Modcn. Since C contains (S, S) and (S{x}, 0), the argument of Example 2.2.9
shows that F is essentially surjective, so that Modcn ≃PΣ(C) is generated by compact projective objects.
Moreover, this shows that an object (A, M) ∈Modcn is compact and projective if and only if it is a retract
of an object of (P, P m) in C. In particular, A is a retract of P ≃S{x1, . . . , xn}, so that A is a compact
projective object of CAlg≥0. Then M is a retract of P m ⊗P A ≃Am and is therefore a ﬁnitely generated
projective A-module.
Notation 2.2.11. We let TM
Zar denote the full subcategory of (Modcn)op spanned by objects of the form
(A, Am) where A has the form S{x1, . . . , xn}[ 1

that a morphism in TM

Similarly, we let TM

will say that a morphism in TM

Remark 2.2.13. Using Propositions V.3.2.5 and V.3.2.8, we see that it is possible to introduce a number
of variations on the pregeometries TM

S-algebra (in the sense of Deﬁnition VII.8.24) and M is a ﬁnitely generated projective A-module.
2.3
Quasi-Coherent Sheaves
Let X = (X, O) be a spectrally ringed ∞-topos. In §2.1, we deﬁned a stable ∞-category ModO whose objects
are sheaves of O-module spectra on X. In this section, we specialize to the case where (X, O) is a spectral
43
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 44
Deligne-Mumford stack. In this case, we will deﬁne a full subcategory QCoh(X) ⊆ModO, whose objects we
will refer to as quasi-coherent sheaves on X.
Remark 2.3.1. The theory of quasi-coherent sheaves developed here is closely related to the theory of
quasi-coherent sheaves in classical algebraic geometry. Let X be a Deligne-Mumford stack and let OX be
its structure sheaf (regarded as a sheaf of commutative rings). We can associate to X an abelian category
AX, whose objects are ´etale sheaves of (discrete) OX-modules. Let Aqc
X denote the full subcategory of AX
spanned by the quasi-coherent sheaves (in the sense of classical algebraic geometry).
If (X, O) is a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack, then the ∞-category QCoh(X) deﬁned below is a stable
∞-category equipped with a t-structure, and its heart QCoh(X)♥is a equivalent (as an abelian category) to
Aqc
X , where X is the underlying Deligne-Mumford stack of (X, O): see Remark 2.3.20 below.
If X is a 1-localic ∞-topos and O is a discrete sheaf of E∞-rings on X, then Proposition 2.1.8 furnishes
a fully faithful embedding of stable ∞-categories D+(AX) →ModO, which induces an equivalence onto the
full subcategory S
n∈Z(ModO)≤n ⊆ModO . Under this equivalence, S
n∈Z QCoh(X)≤n corresponds to the
full subcategory D+
qc(AX) ⊆D+(AX) spanned by those cochain complexes whose cohomologies are quasi-
coherent (that is, belong to the subcategory Aqc
X ⊆AX): see Proposition 2.3.21. In many cases, one can
show that this subcategory is equivalent to the derived ∞-category D+(Aqc
X ).
Remark 2.3.2. In this section, we will devote our attention to the study of quasi-coherent sheaves on
(possibly nonconnective) spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks. There is an entirely parallel theory of quasi-
coherent sheaves on spectral schemes. All of the results of this section carry over to the setting of spectral
schemes, with essentially the same proofs.
We now proceed to deﬁne the ∞-category of quasi-coherent sheaves on a (possibly nonconnective) spectral
Deligne-Mumford stack. Let p : RingTopM →RTop be the forgetful functor which associates to each triple
(X, O, F) the underlying ∞-topos X. We can identify the ∞-category Mod with the inverse image p−1{S}
of the ﬁnal object S ∈RTop. Since p is a coCartesian ﬁbration, the inclusion Mod →RingTopM admits
a left adjoint Γ : RingTopM →Mod. Concretely, the functor Γ assigns to each triple (X, O, F) the pair
(O(1), F(1)) ∈Mod, where 1 denotes a ﬁnal object of X (so that F(1) is a module spectrum over the
E∞-ring O(1)). By restriction, Γ determines a functor
Γ´et : RingTopM
´et →Mod .
Applying the constructions of §V.2.2 in the case of the geometry GnM
´et
of Example 2.2.8, we obtain the
following result:
Proposition 2.3.3. The functor Γ´et : RingTopM
´et →Mod admits a right adjoint Spec´et
M : Mod →RingTopM
´et .
There is an evident forgetful functor q : GnM
´et
→GnSp
´et . Composition with q induces a functor LTop(GnSp
´et ) →
LTop(GnM
´et ), which is given informally by the formula (X, O) 7→(X, O, 0). Unwinding the deﬁnitions, we see
that the relative spectrum functor associated to q is given by the forgetful functor RingTopM
´et →RingTop´et.
From this observation, we immediately deduce the following results:
Proposition 2.3.4. Let A be an E∞-ring, and let M be an A-module. Then the object Spec´et
M(A, M) ∈
RingTopM
´et can be identiﬁed with a triple (X, O, F), where the spectrally ringed ∞-topos (X, O) is given by
Spec A ∈RingTop´et.
Proposition 2.3.5. Let (X, O, F) ∈RingTopM
´et be a GnM
´et -scheme. Then (X, O) is a nonconnective spectral
Deligne-Mumford stack.
Deﬁnition 2.3.6. Let X = (X, O) be a nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stack, and let F be a sheaf
of O-modules on X. We will say that F is quasi-coherent if the triple (X, O, F) is a GnM
´et -scheme. We let
QCoh(X) denote the full subcategory of ModO spanned by the quasi-coherent sheaves of O-modules on X.
44
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 45
In other words, a sheaf F ∈ModO is a quasi-coherent sheaf on a nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford
stack (X, O) if X admits a covering by objects Uα such that each of the triples (X/Uα, O |Uα, F |Uα) has the
form Spec´et
M(Aα, Mα), for some E∞-ring Aα and some Aα-module spectrum Mα.
Remark 2.3.7. Let X = (X, O) be a nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stack, and let F be a sheaf
of O-modules on X.
The condition that F be quasi-coherent is local on X.
In other words, for every
morphism U →V in X, if F |V is a quasi-coherent sheaf on (X/V , O |V ), then F |U is a quasi-coherent sheaf
on (X/U, O |U). Conversely, if we are given an eﬀective epimorphism `
α Uα →V and each restriction F |Uα
is a quasi-coherent sheaf on (X/Uα, O |Uα), then F |V is a quasi-coherent sheaf on (X/V , O |V ). This follows
immediately from Proposition V.2.3.10.
Our next goal is to describe quasi-coherent sheaves over aﬃne spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks. We
begin with a few general remarks.
Lemma 2.3.8. Let A be an E∞-ring, let M be an A-module, and let (X, O, F) = Spec´et
M(A, M). Let U ∈X be
an object such that (X/U, O |U) ≃Spec B is aﬃne. Then we have a canonical equivalence M ⊗A B →F(U).
Proof. Let C denote the full subcategory of Mod(A,M)/ spanned by those objects (B, N) such that the map
A →B is ´etale and the map M ⊗A B →N is an equivalence. Using the construction of §V.2.2, we can
identify X with Shv(Cop) and F with the sheaﬁﬁcation of the presheaf of spectra (B, N) 7→N on Cop. Using
Corollary VII.6.14 (and Proposition VII.5.7) we deduce that this presheaf is already a sheaf. In other words,
for every ´etale A-algebra B, we have F(U) ≃M ⊗A B, where U ∈X is such that Spec B ≃(X/U, O |U).
Invoking Theorem 1.2.1, we deduce that this condition holds whenever (X/U, O |U) is aﬃne.
Lemma 2.3.9. Let (X, O) ≃Spec A be an aﬃne nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stack. Let F be a
quasi-coherent O-module and let M = Γ(X; F) be the global sections of F, regarded as an A ≃Γ(X; O)-module.
Then the canonical map (X, O, F) →Spec´et
M(A, M) is an equivalence (in the ∞-category RingTopM
´et ).
Remark 2.3.10. We can regard Lemma 2.3.9 as a converse to Proposition 2.3.4: it implies that a GnM
´et -
scheme is aﬃne if and only if the underlying nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stack is aﬃne.
Proof. Let C be the opposite of the full subcategory of CAlgA spanned by the ´etale A-algebras, so that we
can identify X with the ∞-topos Shv(C) (see §V.2.2). Since F is quasi-coherent, there exists a collection of
objects Uα ∈X which cover the ﬁnal object such that each (X/Uα, O |Uα, F |Uα) is an aﬃne GnM
´et -scheme.
In particular, (X/Uα, O |Uα) is an aﬃne nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stack (Proposition 2.3.4)
and therefore has the form Spec Bα for some ´etale A-algebra Bα ∈C (Theorem 1.2.1). Without loss of
generality, we may assume that the set of indices α is ﬁnite. Let B = Q Bα so that Spec B ≃(X/U, O |U)
for U = `
α Uα; we observe that (X/U, O |U, F |U) is an aﬃne GnM
´et -scheme of the form Spec´et
M(B, N) for some
B-module N.
Let us abuse notation by interpreting the pair (O, F) as a functor Cop →Mod. Using Lemma 2.3.8, we
deduce that the canonical map F(R) ⊗R R′ →F(R′) is an equivalence whenever R →R′ is a morphism in C
such that the ´etale map A →R factors through B. Let B• be the ˇCech nerve of the faithfully ﬂat morphism
A →B. Since F is a sheaf, we have M = F(A) = lim
←−F(B•). The proof of Theorem VII.6.12 shows that
the canonical map M ⊗A B →F(B) is an equivalence, so that M ⊗A R →F(R) is an equivalence for any
´etale map A →R which factors through B. Let Spec´et
M(A, M) ≃(X, O, F′), so that the map M →F(A)
induces a morphism of sheaves of O-modules F′ →F. Using Lemma 2.3.8, we deduce that α induces an
equivalence F′(R) →F(R) whenever A →R is an ´etale map which factors through B. Since F′ and F are
sheaves, they are determined by their restriction to this covering sieve on C so that α is an equivalence as
desired.
Proposition 2.3.11. Let (X, O) ≃Spec A be an aﬃne nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stack. Then
the global sections functor Γ : ModO →ModA admits a fully faithful left adjoint, whose essential image is
precisely the collection of quasi-coherent O-modules.
45
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 46
Proof. Let F : ModA →ModO be the functor described informally by the formula Spec´et
M(A, M) ≃
(X, O, F(M)). Unwinding the deﬁnitions, we deduce immediately that F is a left adjoint to Γ. It is clear
that F carries A-modules to quasi-coherent O-modules. Conversely, Lemma 2.3.9 implies that every quasi-
coherent O-module belongs to the essential image of F. To prove that F is fully faithful, it suﬃces to show
that for every A-module M, the unit map M →Γ(X, F(M)) is an equivalence, which is a special case of
Lemma 2.3.8.
Proposition 2.3.12. Let (X, O) be a nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stack and let F be a sheaf of

(1) The sheaf F is quasi-coherent.

Proposition 2.3.13. Let (X, O) be a nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stack. Then:
(1) The ∞-category QCoh(X) is closed under small colimits in ModO.
(2) The ∞-category QCoh(X) is stable.
(3) The ∞-category QCoh(X) is presentable.
Proof. We ﬁrst prove (1). Suppose we are given a small diagram {Fα} of quasi-coherent O-modules, having a
colimit F ∈ModO. We wish to prove that F is quasi-coherent. The assertion is local on X: it therefore suﬃces
to show that F |U ≃lim
−→Fα |U is a quasi-coherent sheaf on X/U whenever (X/U, O |U) is aﬃne. Replacing
X by X/U, we may assume that (X, O) is aﬃne. In this case, the desired result follows from Proposition
2.3.11. Using exactly the same argument, we deduce that QCoh(X, O) is closed under shifts in the stable
∞-category ModO. Assertion (2) now follows from Lemma A.1.1.3.3.
To prove (3), we let X0 ⊆X denote the full subcategory spanned by those objects U for which the
∞-category QCoh(X/U) is presentable. We wish to prove that X0 = X. According to Remark 2.1.11, the
construction U 7→ModO |U deﬁnes a limit-preserving functor χO : Xop →d
Cat∞. This functor is classiﬁed
by a Cartesian ﬁbration p : eX →X. Let eX

which correspond to quasi-coherent sheaves on X/p(X). Remark 2.3.7 guarantees that p|eX

46
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 47
ﬁbration, which is classiﬁed by another functor χ′

Cat∞(given informally by U 7→QCoh(X/U)).
Since the condition of quasi-coherence is local (Remark 2.3.7), Proposition T.3.3.3.1 shows that χ′

a limit diagram. The functor χ′

∞-categories which admit small colimits and those functors which preserve small colimits. The ∞-category

Since χ′

in X. It will therefore suﬃce to show that X0 contains every object U ∈X such that (X/U, O |U) is aﬃne,
which follows immediately from Proposition 2.3.11.
Proposition 2.3.14. Let (X, O) be a nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stack. Then the full sub-
category QCoh(X) ⊆ModO contains the unit object O and is stable under tensor products, and therefore
inherits a symmetric monoidal structure from the symmetric monoidal structure on ModO (see Proposition
A.2.2.1.1).
Proof. The assertion is local, so we may assume that (X, O) ≃Spec A is an aﬃne nonconnective spectral
Deligne-Mumford stack. Let F : ModA →ModO be the functor described in Proposition 2.3.11, so that
the essential image of F is the full subcategory QCoh(X) ⊆ModO. Then F(A) ≃O, so that O is quasi-
coherent. To show that QCoh(X) is stable under tensor products, it suﬃces to show that F(M) ⊗O F(N) is
quasi-coherent, for every pair of A-modules M, N ∈ModA.
Let C be the full subcategory of CAlgop
A spanned by the ´etale A-algebras, so that we can identify X with
Shv(C). For any sheaf of O-modules F, we can identify the pair (O, F) with a Mod-valued sheaf Cop →Mod
on C. Using Lemma 2.3.8, we see that F(M) and F(N) are given by the formulas
F(M)(B) = M ⊗A B
F(N)(B) = N ⊗A B.
It follows that F(M) ⊗O F(N) is the sheaﬁﬁcation of the presheaf
B 7→F(M)(B) ⊗O(B) F(N)(B) ≃(M ⊗A B) ⊗B (N ⊗A B) ≃(M ⊗A N) ⊗A B.
As in the proof of Lemma 2.3.8, this presheaf is already a sheaf which we will denote by F.
We have
F(A) ≃M ⊗A N so the above formula shows that the canonical map F(A) ⊗A B →F(B) is an equivalence
for every ´etale A-algebra B; in other words, the counit map F(Γ(X; F)) →F is an equivalence, so that F
belongs to the essential image QCoh(X) ⊆ModO of the functor F.
We now restrict our attention to the case of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks (X, O): that is, we assume
that the structure sheaf O is connective. In this case, the ∞-category QCoh(X) inherits a t-structure.
Lemma 2.3.15. Let A be a connective E∞-ring, let Spec´et A = (X, O), and let F : ModA →ModO be the
fully faithful embedding of Proposition 2.3.11. Then F is t-exact.
Proof. The functor F is left adjoint to the global sections functor F 7→Γ(X; F), which is obviously left
t-exact. It follows formally that F is right t-exact. To complete the proof, we will show that F is left
t-exact: that is, if M ∈(ModA)≤0, then F(M) ∈(ModO)≤0. Let X0 be the full subcategory of X spanned
by those objects U ∈X such that F(M)(U) ∈Sp≤0. We wish to prove that X0 = X. Since F is a sheaf and
the full subcategory Sp≤0 ⊆Sp is stable under limits, we deduce that X0 is stable under colimits in X. It
will therefore suﬃce to show that X0 contains all objects U ∈X such that (X/U, O |U) is an aﬃne spectral
Deligne-Mumford stack Spec´et B. Lemma 2.3.8 gives a canonical equivalence F(M)(U) ≃M ⊗A B. The
desired result now follows from Theorem A.7.2.2.15, since Theorem 1.2.1 guarantees that B is ´etale (and in
particular ﬂat) over A.
Proposition 2.3.16. Let X = (X, O) be a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack.
Then the full subcategory
QCoh(X) ⊆ModO is compatible with the t-structure of Proposition 2.1.3. More precisely, if F ∈ModO
is quasi-coherent, then the truncations τ≥n F and τ≤n F are quasi-coherent, for every integer n. Conse-
quently, the full subcategories QCoh(X)≥0 = QCoh(X)∩(ModO)≥0 and QCoh(X)≤0 = QCoh(X)∩(ModO)≤0
determine a t-structure on the ∞-category QCoh(X).
47
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 48
Proof. Replacing F by its translates if necessary, it will suﬃce to show that if F is quasi-coherent, then τ≥0 F
and τ≤−1 F are quasi-coherent. This assertion is local on X; we may therefore assume that (X, O) ≃Spec A
is an aﬃne spectral Deligne-Mumford stack (where A is a connective E∞-ring). Let F : ModA →ModO
be the functor described in Proposition 2.3.11. Since F is quasi-coherent, we may assume without loss of
generality that F = F(M) for some A-module M. Since A is connective, there is a ﬁber sequence
M ′ →M →M ′′
where M ′ is a connective A-module and M ′′ ∈(ModA)≤−1. Applying the exact functor F, we obtain a ﬁber
sequence
F(M ′) →F →F(M ′′)
in ModO. Lemma 2.3.15 guarantees that F(M ′) ∈(ModO)≥0 and F(M ′′) ∈(ModO)≤−1. We therefore
obtain identiﬁcations F(M ′) ≃τ≥0 F and F(M ′′) ≃τ≤−1 F which prove that τ≥0 F and τ≤−1 F are quasi-
coherent.
Notation 2.3.17. If X = (X, O) is a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack, we will often let QCoh(X)cn denote
the full subcategory QCoh(X)≥0 ⊆QCoh(X) deﬁned in Proposition 2.3.16. We will say that a quasi-coherent
sheaf F is connective if it belongs to QCoh(X)cn.
The basic properties of the t-structure on QCoh(X) are summarized in the following result:
Proposition 2.3.18. Let X = (X, O) be a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack. Then:
(1) The t-structure on QCoh(X) is accessible (see Deﬁnition A.1.4.5.12).
(2) The t-structure on QCoh(X) is compatible with ﬁltered colimits: that is, the full subcategory QCoh(X)≤0
is closed under ﬁltered colimits.
(3) The t-structure on QCoh(X) is both right and left complete.
Proof. To prove (1), it suﬃces to show that the full subcategory QCoh(X)cn is presentable (Proposition
A.1.4.5.13). This follows from Proposition T.5.5.3.12, since QCoh(X)cn can be identiﬁed with the ﬁber prod-
uct QCoh(X) ×ModO Modcn
O . Assertion (2) follows from Proposition 2.3.13 together with the corresponding
result for ModO (Proposition 2.1.3).
We now prove (3). Since ModO is right-complete (Proposition 2.1.3), we deduce that T

n(ModO)≤−n contains only zero objects. Combining this observation with (2), we deduce that QCoh(X)
is right-complete (see Proposition A.1.2.1.19).
The proof that QCoh(X) is left-complete requires a bit more eﬀort. Consider the full subcategory X0 ⊆X
spanned by those objects U ∈X for which the t-structure on QCoh(X/U) is left-complete. To complete the
proof, it will suﬃce to show that X0 = X.
Using Proposition 2.3.11, Lemma 2.3.15, and Proposition
A.7.1.1.13, we deduce that X0 contains every object U ∈X for which XU = (X/U, O |U) is aﬃne. It will
therefore suﬃce to show that X0 is closed under small colimits in X. Since the conditions of being quasi-

Remark 2.3.19. Let GM

Remark 2.3.20. Let X = (X, O) be nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stack, and let X0 denote
the underlying ordinary Deligne-Mumford stack (see Remark VII.8.41). If F is a quasi-coherent sheaf on
(X, O), then each homotopy group πn F can be identiﬁed with a quasi-coherent sheaf on X0, in the sense of
classical algebraic geometry. If O is connective, then this construction induces an equivalence from the heart
QCoh(X)♥of the ∞-category QCoh(X) of quasi-coherent sheaves on (X, O) to (the nerve of) the abelian
category of quasi-coherent sheaves on X0. For a more general assertion of this nature, see Corollary 2.5.24.
We close this section by giving a diﬀerent characterization of quasi-coherence.
Proposition 2.3.21. Let X = (X, O) be a nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stack and let F be a
sheaf of O-modules on X. Then F is quasi-coherent if and only if it satisﬁes the following conditions:
(1) For every integer n, the homotopy group πn F is a quasi-coherent τ≥0 O-module on X (which we can
identify with a quasi-coherent sheaf on the underlying ordinary Deligne-Mumford stack of (X, O); see
Remark 2.3.20).
(2) The object Ω∞F ∈ShvS(X) ≃X is hypercomplete.
Proof. Replacing O by its connective cover if necessary, we may assume that O is connective. If F is quasi-
coherent, then Proposition 2.3.16 implies that each homotopy group πn F is quasi-coherent as a O-module.
To prove that (2) is satisﬁed, it suﬃces to work locally on X; we may therefore assume that (X, O) ≃Spec A
for some connective E∞-ring A. Let F be the functor of Proposition 2.3.11, so that F ≃F(M) for some
A-module M. Let C denote the full subcategory of CAlgop
A spanned by the ´etale A-algebras, so we may
identify X with the ∞-topos Shv(C) and the pair (O, F) with a sheaf Cop →Mod on C. We note that for
each B ∈C, we have
F(M)(B) ≃M ⊗A B ≃lim
←−τ≤n(M ⊗A B) ≃lim
←−(τ≤nM) ⊗A B ≃lim
←−F(τ≤nM)(B).
It follows that F ≃F(M) ≃lim
←−F(τ≤nM) is a limit of truncated objects of ModO (Lemma 2.3.15), so that
Ω∞F is a limit of truncated objects of X and therefore hypercomplete.
Now suppose that F ∈ModO satisﬁes conditions (1) and (2). We wish to prove that F is quasi-coherent.
Note that F ≃lim
−→τ≥−n F by Proposition 2.3.18. Since the collection of quasi-coherent sheaves is closed
under colimits in ModO, it suﬃces to prove that each τ≥−n F is quasi-coherent. Replacing F by (τ≥−n F)[n],
we may assume that F is connective. Since the condition of being quasi-coherent is local on X, we may
suppose that (X, O) ≃Spec A is an aﬃne spectral Deligne-Mumford stack, where A is a connective E∞-ring;
let C and F : ModA →ModO be deﬁned as above.
We now argue by induction on m that each truncation τ≤m F is quasi-coherent. For m < 0, this is
obvious. If m ≥0, it follows from the existence of a ﬁber sequence
τ≤m−1 F →τ≤m F →(πm F)[m].
Using Proposition 2.3.11, we may suppose that the tower {τ≤m F} is obtained from a tower of A-modules
{Mm}m≥0. Using Lemma 2.3.15, we deduce that for m ≤m′, the map Mm′ →Mm exhibits Mm as an m-
truncation τ≤mMm′. Since ModA is left complete (Proposition A.7.1.1.13), the A-module M ≃lim
←−Mm has
the property that τ≤mM ≃Mm for every integer m. For every ﬂat A-algebra B, we also obtain an equivalence
τ≤m(M ⊗A B) ≃Mm ⊗A B, so that M ⊗A B ≃lim
←−(Mm ⊗A B). It follows that F(M) ≃lim
←−m F(Mm) ≃

←−τ≤m F in the ∞-category ModO. In particular, we obtain a map α : F →F(M). To prove that F is
quasi-coherent, it will suﬃce to show that α is an equivalence. Since F and F(M) are both connective, this
is equivalent to the requirement that α induces an equivalence Ω∞F →Ω∞F(M) in ShvS(X) ≃X. Since
Ω∞F is hypercomplete (by (2)) and Ω∞F(M) ≃lim
←−Ω∞F(Mm) is hypercomplete (since it is an inverse limit

of truncated objects of X), it will suﬃce to show that the map Ω∞(α) : Ω∞F →Ω∞F(M) is ∞-connective.
This is clear, since for every integer m ≥0, the truncation τ≤mΩ∞(α) can be identiﬁed with the equivalence
τ≤mΩ∞F ≃Ω∞(τ≤m F) ≃Ω∞F(Mm) ≃Ω∞F(τ≤mM) ≃Ω∞τ≤mF(M) ≃τ≤mΩ∞F(M).
2.4
Quasi-Aﬃne Spectral Deligne-Mumford Stacks
Let X = (X, O) be a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack, let 1 denote a ﬁnal object of X, and let A = O(1) be
the E∞-ring of global sections of O. Evaluation at the object 1 determines a functor θ : QCoh(X) ⊆ModO →
ModA. If X is aﬃne, then Proposition 2.3.11 implies that θ is an equivalence of ∞-categories. In this section,
we will study a weaker condition on X which guarantees that θ is an equivalence of ∞-categories: namely,
the condition that X is quasi-aﬃne.
Deﬁnition 2.4.1. Let X be a nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stack. We say that X is quasi-aﬃne
if X is quasi-compact and there exists an open immersion j : X →Spec´et R for some E∞-ring R.
Remark 2.4.2. Let X be a quasi-aﬃne spectral Deligne-Mumford stack. Then X is schematic. In particular,
X is a spectral algebraic space.
Suppose that X is a quasi-aﬃne nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stack. Then there exists an
open immersion j : X →X′, where X′ is aﬃne. The following pair of results asserts that there is a canonical
choice of X′, for which the ∞-categories QCoh(X) and QCoh(X′) are equivalent.
Proposition 2.4.3. Let X = (X, OX) be a quasi-compact nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stack and
let 1 denote a ﬁnal object of X. The following conditions are equivalent:
(1) The nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stack X is quasi-aﬃne.
(2) The canonical map X →Spec OX(1) is an open immersion.
Proposition 2.4.4. Let X = (X, OX) be a quasi-aﬃne nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stack. Let 1
denote a ﬁnal object of X and let A = OX(1). Then evaluation at 1 induces an equivalence of ∞-categories
e : QCoh(X) →ModA.
Before giving the proofs of Propositions 2.4.3 and 2.4.4, we establish the following techinical result:
Proposition 2.4.5. Let X be a nonconnective quasi-compact spectral Deligne-Mumford stack and let j :

(2) Suppose that R is connective. Then there exists an integer n such that j∗(QCoh(X)≥0) ⊆(ModR)≥−n.
(3) Suppose we are given a pullback diagram of nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks

Proof of Proposition 2.4.4. We can identify e : QCoh(X) →ModA with the pushforward functor j∗associ-

case M = A[n]. We may easily reduce to the case n = 0, in which case the desired result follows from the
deﬁnition of A.
Warning 2.4.7. Let X = (X, OX) be a quasi-aﬃne spectral Deligne-Mumford stack, let 1 be a ﬁnal object
of X, and let A = OX(1). Then the canonical map j : X →Spec´et A is an open immersion (Proposition
2.4.3). However, A is generally not connective. In fact, A is connective if and only if j is an equivalence (that
is, if and only if the spectral Deligne-Mumford stack X is aﬃne). The “if” direction is obvious. Conversely,
suppose that A is connective. The open immersion j determines a quasi-compact open subset U ⊆SpecZ A,
consisting of those prime ideals which fail to contain some ﬁnitely generated ideal I = (x1, . . . , xn) ⊆π0R.
Let M = (π0A)/I, which we regard as a discrete A-module. Then M[ 1
xi ] ≃0 for 1 ≤i ≤n, so that M is
annihilated by the pullback functor ModA ≃QCoh(Spec´et A)
j∗
→QCoh(X). Proposition 2.4.4 implies that
the pullback functor j∗is an equivalence of ∞-categories, so that M ≃0. It follows that I is the unit ideal
in π0A, so that j is an equivalence.
In spite of Warning 2.4.7, every quasi-aﬃne spectral Deligne-Mumford stack admits an open immersion
into the spectrum of a connective E∞-ring:
Proposition 2.4.8. Let X = (X, OX) be a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack. The following conditions are

Spec´et R0 is an open immersion, corresponding to some quasi-compact open subset U ⊆SpecZ R0. For each

(∗) Let x be an element of π0R0 such that Ux ⊆U. Then there exists an integer m > 0 such that xm can
be lifted to an element of π0R.
For every pair of integers i ≤i′, let φi′,i : π0Ri′ →π0Ri be the canonical map. To prove (∗), we show
that for each i ≥0, some positive power xm of x lies in the image of the map φi,0 : π0Ri →π0R0. Since
π0R ≃π0Rn, (∗) will follow if we prove this in the case i = n. We proceed by induction on i, the case
i = 0 being trivial. Assume therefore that there exists an integer m > 0 such that xm = φi,0(y) for some
y ∈π0Ri. We will prove that some positive power of y lies in the image of the map φi+1,i. Using Theorem
A.7.4.1.26, we deduce that τ≤i+1 OX is a square-zero extension of τ≤i OX by the module (πi+1 OX)[i + 1].
It follows that Ri+1 is a square-zero extension of Ri by the module Γ(πi+1 OX)[i + 1]. In particular, the
image of the map φi+1,i is the kernel of a derivation d : π0Ri →π−i−2Γ(πi+1 OX). We wish to prove that
d(ym′) = 0 for some m′ > 0. Since d is a derivation, we have d(ym′) = m′ym′−1dy. It will therefore suﬃce to
show that dy ∈π−i−2Γ(πi+1 OX) is annihilated by some power of y. Note that Γ(πi+1 OX) has the structure
of a module over R0. Moreover, Corollary 2.4.6 implies that j∗
0Γ(πi+1 OX) is equivalent to πi+1 OX, which a
discrete sheaf of spectra on X. Since Ux ⊆U, we deduce that Γ(πi+1 OX)[ 1
x] is discrete. Since i + 2̸ = 0, it
follows that every element of π−i−2Γ(πi+1 OX) is annihilated by a power of x, and therefore by a power of
y. This completes the proof of (∗).
Write U as a union of open sets S
1≤i≤n Uxi for some elements xi ∈π0R0. Using (∗), we may assume

corresponding to V , and for 1 ≤i ≤n let Vi denote the open substack of V corresponding to Vi. Since
V is the inverse image of U in SpecZ R, the canonical map j : X →Spec´et R factors through V. We claim
that j induces an equivalence X →V. To prove this, it suﬃces to show that each of the induced maps
X ×V Vi →Vi is an equivalence. In view of Proposition 2.4.5, this is equivalent to the assertion that X × is
aﬃne. This follows from Remark VII.8.43, since the 0-truncation of X ×V Vi is given by Spec R0[ 1
xi ].

We conclude this section by introducing a relative version of the notion of a quasi-aﬃne spectral Deligne-
Mumford stack.
Deﬁnition 2.4.12. Let f : X →Y be a map of nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks. We will
say that f is aﬃne if, for every map Spec´et R →Y, the ﬁber product X ×Y Spec R is quasi-aﬃne. We will
say that f is quasi-aﬃne if, for every map Spec´et R →Y, the ﬁber product X ×Y Spec´et R is quasi-aﬃne.
The following assertions regarding aﬃne and quasi-aﬃne morphisms follow immediately from the deﬁni-
tion:
Proposition 2.4.13.
(1) Any equivalence of nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks is aﬃne.
Any aﬃne morphism is quasi-aﬃne.
(2) Let f : X →Y be a map of nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks, and suppose that Y is
aﬃne. Then f is aﬃne (quasi-aﬃne) if and only if X is aﬃne (quasi-aﬃne).

2.5
Pullbacks and Pushforwards of Quasi-Coherent Sheaves
Let f : (Y, OY) →(X, OX) be a map of spectrally ringed ∞-topoi. Then f induces a symmetric monoidal
functor f ∗: ShvSp(X) →ShvSp(Y) and a morphism f ∗OX →OY of commutative algebra objects of ShvSp(Y).
We therefore obtain a composite functor
ModOY →Modf ∗OY →ModOX .
We will generally abuse notation by denoting this functor also by f ∗.
Proposition 2.5.1. Let f : (Y, OY) →(X, OX) be a map of nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks.
Then the pullback functor f ∗: ModOX →ModOY carries quasi-coherent sheaves on X to quasi-coherent
sheaves on Y.
Proof. The assertion is local on X and Y. We may therefore assume that both (X, OX) and (Y, OY) are aﬃne,
in which case the desired result follows immediately from the characterization of quasi-coherent sheaves given
by Proposition 2.3.11.
Remark 2.5.2. Proposition 2.5.1 has an obvious analogue in the setting of spectral schemes, which can be
proven in the same way.
Let f : (Y, OY) →(X, OX) be as in Proposition 2.5.1. The induced functor f ∗: ModOX →ModOY is
a colimit-preserving functor between presentable ∞-categories, and therefore admits a right adjoint which
we will denote by f∗. In this section, we will study some conditions which guarantee that the functor f∗
preserves quasi-coherence. We ﬁrst need some general remarks.
Proposition 2.5.3. Let X be an ∞-topos and suppose we are given a diagram σ :

(2) The diagram σ is a pushout square and the map f is (−1)-truncated.
(3) The diagram σ is a pullback square, f ′ is (−1)-truncated, and if we let i∗: X/V ′ →X/V ′ /V denote the
corresponding closed immersion, then i∗U ′ is a ﬁnal object of X/V ′ /V .
Proof. The equivalence of (1) and (3) is a matter of unwinding deﬁnitions, and the implication (1) ⇒(2)

Deﬁnition 2.5.5. Let (X, OX) be a nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stack. A scallop decomposition
of X consists of a sequence of (−1)-truncated morphisms

Example 2.5.6. Let X be a quasi-aﬃne nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stack. Then X admits a
scallop decomposition.
Remark 2.5.7. We will show later that a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack admits a scallop decomposition
if and only if it is a quasi-compact, quasi-separated spectral algebraic space.
Before stating the next result, we need to introduce a bit of terminology. Let (X, OX) be a nonconnective
spectral Deligne-Mumford stack. We say that an object U ∈X is semiaﬃne if it is quasi-compact and there
exists a (−1)-truncated map U →X in X, where X is aﬃne. We will say that a morphism f : U →V in X
is semiaﬃne if the ﬁber product U ×V X is semiaﬃne, whenever X ∈X is aﬃne.

Corollary 2.5.9. Let X = (X, OX) be a nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stack which admits a
scallop decomposition. Suppose that C ⊆X is a full subcategory which contains all aﬃne objects of X and is
closed under pushouts. Then C contains the ﬁnal object of X.
Deﬁnition 2.5.10. Let f : X →Y be a map of nonconnective spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks. We will
say that f is relatively scalloped if, for every map Spec´et R →Y, the ﬁber product X ×Y Spec´et R admits a
scallop decomposition.
Example 2.5.11. Every quasi-aﬃne morphism is relatively scalloped (see Example 2.5.6).
Proposition 2.5.12. Let f : X = (X, OX) →(Y, OY) = Y be a relatively scalloped map of nonconnective
spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks. Then the pushforward functor f∗: ModOX →ModOY carries quasi-coherent
sheaves to quasi-coherent sheaves. Moreover, the induced functor QCoh(X) →QCoh(Y) commutes with small
colimits.
Proof. The assertion is local on Y; we may therefore assume without loss of generality that Y = Spec´et R is
aﬃne. Assume ﬁrst that X = Spec´et A is aﬃne. Let F ∈QCoh(X) be the quasi-coherent sheaf associated to
some A-module M, and let F′ ∈QCoh(Y) be the quasi-coherent sheaf associated to the image of M in ModR.
The canonical map A ⊗R M →M determines a map f ∗F′ →F, which is adjoint to a map of OY-modules
F′ →f∗F. We claim that this map is an equivalence. For this, we must show that u : F′(U) →(f∗F)(U) ≃
F(f ∗U) is an equivalence of spectra for each U ∈Y. The collection of those objects U which satisfy this
condition is stable under colimits. We may therefore assume that U is representable by an ´etale R-algebra
R′. In this case, u can be identiﬁed with the canonical equivalence
M ⊗R R′ →M ⊗A (A ⊗R R′).
58
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 59
This proves that f∗carries quasi-coherent sheaves on X to quasi-coherent sheaves on Y. Moreover, the
restricted functor f∗: ModA ≃QCoh(X) →QCoh(Y) ≃ModR is right adjoint to the base-change functor
N 7→N ⊗R A, and can therefore be identiﬁed with the forgetful functor ModA →ModR, which commutes
with small colimits.
We now treat the general case. For each object U ∈X, let ΓU : ModOX →ModOY denote the composite
functor
ModOX →ModOX |U(ShvSp(X/U)) →ModOY .
Let us say that U is good if ΓU restricts to a colimit-preserving functor from QCoh(X) into QCoh(Y). The
construction U 7→ΓU carries pushout square to pullback squares. It follows that the collection of good
objects of X is stable under ﬁnite colimits. Since every aﬃne object of X is good (by the ﬁrst part of the
proof), Corollary 2.5.9 implies that the ﬁnal object of X is good.
Proposition 2.5.13. Let f : X = (X, OX) →(Y, OY) = Y be a relatively scalloped map of spectral Deligne-
Mumford stacks. Assume that Y is quasi-compact. Then there exists an integer n such that the pushforward
functor f∗: QCoh(X) →QCoh(Y) carries QCoh(X)≥0 into QCoh(Y)≥−n.
Proof. Since Y is quasi-compact, we can choose an ´etale surjection Spec´et R →Y for some connective E∞-
ring R. Replacing Y by Spec´et R, we may assume that Y is aﬃne so that X admits a scallop decomposition.
We deﬁne the class of good objects U ∈X as in the proof of Proposition 2.5.12. For every good object U ∈X,
let ΓU : QCoh(X) →QCoh(Y) be deﬁned as in the proof of Proposition 2.5.12. Let us say that U is n-good
if ΓU(QCoh(X)≥0) ⊆QCoh(Y)≥−n. Note that if we are given a pushout diagram
U
/

U ′

V
/ V ′
in X, then we have a ﬁber sequence of functors
ΓU ′ ⊕ΓV →ΓV ′ →ΓU[1].
It follows that if U ′ and V are n-good and U is (n −1)-good, then V ′ is also n-good. Let us say that a good
object U ∈X is very good if it n-good for some integer n ≥0. It follows that the collection of very good
objects of X is closed under pushouts. Any aﬃne object of X is 0-good, and therefore very good. Using
Corollary 2.5.9, we deduce that the ﬁnal object of X is very good, which implies the desired result.

If we are willing to restrict our attention to truncated quasi-coherent sheaves on spectral Deligne-Mumford

Notation 2.5.17. Let X = (X, OX) be a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack.
We let Mod♥
OX denote the
heart of the ∞-category ModOX: it can be identiﬁed with (the nerve of) the abelian category of sheaves
of discrete modules over π0 OX. We will say that an object of Mod♥
OX is quasi-coherent if it belongs to
QCoh(X)♥= QCoh(X) ∩Mod♥
OX. We will say that an object F ∈Mod♥
OX is pseudo-coherent if, for every
aﬃne object U ∈X, there exists a composition series
0 = F0 ,→F1 ,→· · · ,→Fn = F |U
such that each quotient Fi / Fi−1 is a subobject of some quasi-coherent object Gi ∈QCoh(U)♥, where
U = (X/U, OX |U).
We have the following coherence result:
Theorem 2.5.18. Let f : (Y, OY) →(X, OX) be an n-quasi-compact morphism between spectral Deligne-
Mumford stacks. Let F ∈(ModOY)≤0 be sheaf of OY-modules satisfying the following conditions:
60
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 61
(a) For 0 ≤i < n, π−i F is quasi-coherent.
(b) The sheaf π−n F is pseudo-coherent.
Then f∗F also satisﬁes conditions (a) and (b).
The proof of Theorem 2.5.18 will require a few preliminaries. Let X = (X, OX) be a spectral Deligne-
Mumford stack. Since the t-structure on ModOX restricts to a t-structure on the full subcategory QCoh(X),
we can identify QCoh(X)♥with a full subcategory of the abelian category Mod♥
OX which is closed under the
formation of kernels, cokernels, and extensions. Our ﬁrst goal is to extend these observations to pseudo-
coherent sheaves.
Lemma 2.5.19. Let X = (X, O) be a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack, and suppose we are given a morphism
α : F →G in the abelian category Mod♥
O. If F is quasi-coherent and G is pseudo-coherent, then ker(α) and
im(α) (formed in the abelian category Mod♥
O) are quasi-coherent.
Proof. The assertion is local on X; we may therefore assume that X is aﬃne so that there exists a ﬁnite
ﬁltration
0 = G0 ,→G1 ,→· · · ,→Gn = G
such that each quotient Gi / Gi−1 is a subobject of a quasi-coherent object Hi ∈QCoh(X)♥. Let Ki denote
the kernel of the composite map
F
α→G →G / Gi .
For each index i, α induces a monomorphism
Ki / Ki−1 ,→Gi / Gi−1 →Hi .
Thus Ki−1 can be identiﬁed with the kernel of a map Ki →Hi. Note that Kn ≃F is quasi-coherent. It
follows by descending induction on i that each Ki is quasi-coherent. In particular, K0 = ker(α) is quasi-
coherent. Using the exact sequence
0 →ker(α) →F →im(α) →0,
we see that im(α) is quasi-coherent as well.
Lemma 2.5.20. Let X = (X, O) be a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack, and suppose we are given an exact
sequence
0 →F′ →F →F′′ →0
in the abelian category Mod♥
O.
(a) If F′ and F′′ are psuedo-coherent, then F is pseudo-coherent.
(b) If F is pseudo-coherent, then F′ is pseudo-coherent.
(c) If F′ is quasi-coherent and F is pseudo-coherent, then F′′ is pseudo-coherent.
Proof. Assertion (a) follows immediately from the deﬁnitions. We next prove (b). Without loss of generality,

Lemma 2.5.21. Let f : (Y, OY) →(X, OX) be an aﬃne morphism between spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks.
Let F ∈(ModOY)≤0 be such that π0 F is pseudo-coherent.
Then (f∗F) ∈(ModOX)≤0, and π0(f∗F) is
pseudo-coherent.
Proof. We ﬁrst note that the pushforward functor f∗is left t-exact. Let F ∈(ModOY)≤0 be such that π0 F

Combining Theorem 2.5.18 with the quasi-coherence criterion of Proposition 2.3.21, we obtain:
Corollary 2.5.22. Let f : (Y, OY) →(X, OX) be a map of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks which is n-
quasi-compact for all n ≥0. Then the induced functor f∗: ModOY →ModOX carries QCoh(Y)≤0 into
QCoh(X)≤0.
We conclude this section with a few remarks about the behavior of the pushforward functor f∗in the
case where f : X →Y is a “highly connected” aﬃne morphism.
Proposition 2.5.23. Let X = (X, OX) and Y = (Y, OY) be spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks. Let f : X →Y
be an aﬃne morphism, let n ≥0 be an integer, and suppose that the ﬁber of the map OY →f∗OX is
n-connective. Then:
(1) The pushforward functor f∗: QCoh(X)cn
≤n →QCoh(Y)cn
≤n is fully faithful.
(2) The pushforward functor f∗: QCoh(X)cn
≤n−1 →QCoh(Y)cn
≤n−1 is an equivalence of ∞-categories.
63
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 64
Proof. The assertion is local on Y, so we may assume without loss of generality that Y is aﬃne. Write
Y = Spec A for some connective E∞-ring A.
Since f is aﬃne, we can assume X = Spec B for some
connective A-algebra B. Let u : A →B denote the underlying map of E∞-rings, so that ﬁb(u) is an n-
connective spectrum. To prove (1), we must show that the forgetful functor φn : (Modcn
B )≤n →(Modcn
A )≤n
is fully faithful. We observe that φn has a left adjoint ψn, given by M 7→τ≤n(B ⊗A M). We wish to
show that the counit map ψn ◦φn →id is an equivalence. Unwinding the deﬁnitions, we must show that
if M ∈(Modcn
B )≤n, then the canonical map θ : B ⊗A M →M exhibits M as an n-truncation of B ⊗A M.
Since M is n-truncated, this is equivalent to the requirement that ﬁb(θ) is (n + 1)-connective. Let θ0 be
the multiplication map B ⊗A B →B, so that ﬁb(θ) ≃ﬁb(θ0) ⊗B M. Since M is connective, it will suﬃce
to show that ﬁb(θ0) is (n + 1)-connective.
Note that θ0 admits a section s, so we can identify ﬁb(θ0)
with the coﬁb(s) = B ⊗A coﬁb(u). We complete the proof of (1) by observing that coﬁb(u) = ﬁb(u)[1] is
(n + 1)-connective.
We now prove (2). Let φn−1 and ψn−1 be deﬁned as above; we wish to show that the unit map id →
φn−1 ◦ψn−1 is an equivalence. In other words, we wish to show that if N ∈(Modcn
A )≤n−1, then the canonical
map N →B ⊗A N induces an isomorphism πiN →πi(B ⊗A N) for i < n. We have a long exact sequence
πi(ﬁb(u) ⊗A N) →πiN →πi(B ⊗A N) →πi−1(ﬁb(u) ⊗A N).
It therefore suﬃces to show that the homotopy groups πi(ﬁb(u) ⊗A N) vanish for i < n. This is clear, since
ﬁb(u) is n-connective and N is connective.
Corollary 2.5.24. Let X = (X, OX) and Y = (Y, OY) be spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks. Let f : X →

≤n ≃
QCoh(Y)cn
≤n.

Trn Y
ψ

X
/ Y
where the upper horizontal map is an equivalence. It will therefore suﬃce to show that the vertical maps
induce equivalences of ∞-categories

≤n
ψ∗: QCoh(Trn Y)cn

Both of these assertions follow immediately from Proposition 2.5.23.
2.6
Local Properties of Quasi-Coherent Sheaves
Let X be a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack. In §2.3, we introduced the ∞-category QCoh(X) of quasi-
coherent sheaves on X. In this section, we will study some ﬁniteness conditions on objects of QCoh(X). In
particular, when X is locally Noetherian, we will introduce a full subcategory Coh(X) ⊆QCoh(X), which we
call the ∞-category of coherent sheaves on X.
We begin by discussing some ﬁniteness conditions on modules over E1-rings.
Deﬁnition 2.6.1. Let A be a connective E1-ring and let M be a left A-module. We will say that M is
perfect to order n if, for every ﬁltered diagram {Nα} in (LModA)≤0, the canonical map
lim
−→
α
Exti
A(M, Nα) →Exti
A(M, lim
−→Nα)
injective when i = n and bijective when i < n.
64
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 65
Remark 2.6.2. If A is a connective E1-ring, then a left A-module M is almost perfect (see Deﬁnition
A.7.2.5.10) if and only if it is perfect to order n for every integer n.
Remark 2.6.3. In the situation of Deﬁnition 2.6.1, suppose that M is perfect to order n. When restricted to
(LModA)≤n−1, the functor N 7→MapLModA(M, N) commutes with ﬁltered colimits. In particular, τ≤n−1M
is a compact object of (LModA)≤n−1.
Remark 2.6.4. Let A be a connective E1-ring, and let M be an A-module which is perfect to order n for
some integer n. Then M ∈(LModA)≥−m for some m. This follows immediately from Remark 2.6.3.
Remark 2.6.5. Let A be a connective E1-ring, and suppose we are given a map f : M →M ′ such that the
induced map πiM →πiM ′ is surjective when i = n and bijective for i < n. Let N ∈(LModA)≤0. Then the
induced map Exti
A(M ′, N) →Exti
A(M, N) is injective for i = n and bijective for i < n. It follows that if M
is perfect to order n, so is M ′.
Remark 2.6.6. Let A be a connective E1-ring. If M is an A-module which is perfect to order n + 1, then
τ≤nM is also perfect to order n + 1 (this is a special case of Remark 2.6.5).
Remark 2.6.7. Let A be a connective E1-ring and let M be a compact object of (LModA)≤n. Since LModA
is compactly generated, we deduce that M is a retract of τ≤nM ′ for some compact object M ′ ∈LModA.
Since M ′ is perfect to order n + 1, so is M (Remark 2.6.6). Combining this observation with Remark 2.6.3,
we deduce that an object of (LModA)≤n is compact in (LModA)≤n if and only if it is perfect to order n + 1.
Remark 2.6.8. Let A be a connective E1-ring and suppose we are given a ﬁber sequence of left A-modules
M ′ →M →M ′′.
If M ′ is perfect to order n, then M is perfect to order n if and only if M ′′ is perfect to order n. This follows
immediately from an inspection of the associated long exact sequence of Ext-groups.
Remark 2.6.9. Suppose we are given a ﬁnite collection of connective E1-rings {Ai}1≤i≤n having product
A. Let M be a left A-module, so that M ≃Q Mi where each Mi is a left module over Ai. Then M is perfect
to order n if and only if each Mi is perfect to order n.
Deﬁnition 2.6.10. Let A be a connective E1-ring, let M be a left A-module, and let n be an integer. We
will say that M is ﬁnitely n-presented if M is connective, perfect to order n + 1, and the homotopy groups
πiM vanish for i > n.
Remark 2.6.11. Using Remark 2.6.7, we see that a left A-module M is ﬁnitely n-presented if and only if
it is a compact object of (LModcn
A )≤n.
Our next result gives a formulation of Deﬁnition 2.6.1 which is well-adapted to making inductive argu-
ments:

(2) Let n > 0 and suppose we are given a map of A-modules φ : Ak →M which induces a surjection
π0Ak →π0M. Then M is perfect to order n if and only if ﬁb(φ) is perfect to order (n −1).

Proposition 2.6.13. Let f : A →B be a map of connective E1-rings and let M be a left A-module. If M
is perfect to order n as an A-module, then B ⊗A M is perfect to order n as a B-module. The converse holds
if B is faithfully ﬂat (as a right module) over A.

hypothesis implies that B ⊗A M ′ is perfect to order (n −1) as a B-module. Using Proposition 2.6.12 again,
we deduce that B ⊗A M is perfect to order n as a B-module.

We now introduce some terminology to place Proposition 2.6.13 in a more general context.
Deﬁnition 2.6.14. Let CAlgcn ×CAlg Mod denote the ∞-category of pairs (AM), where A is a connective
E∞-ring and M is an A-module spectrum. Let P be a property of objects (A, M) ∈CAlgcn ×CAlg Mod. We
will say that P is local for the fpqc topology if the following conditions are satisﬁed:
(1) Let f : A →B be a ﬂat morphism of connective E∞-rings, let M be an A-module. If (A, M) has the
property P, then (B, B ⊗A M) has the property P. The converse holds if f is faithfully ﬂat.
(2) Suppose we are given a ﬁnite collection of pairs (Ai, Mi) ∈CAlgcn ×CAlg Mod, each of which has the
property P. Then the product (Q Ai, Q Mi) ∈C has the property P.
The following result collects some examples of local properties of module spectra:
Proposition 2.6.15. The following conditions on an object (A, M) ∈Mod ×CAlg CAlgcn are local with
respect to the fpqc topology:
(1) The condition that M is n-connective (when regarded as a spectrum), where n is a ﬁxed integer.
(2) The condition that M is almost connective: that is, M is (−n)-connective for n ≫0.
(3) The condition that M is n-truncated (that is, that πiM ≃0 for i > n), where n is a ﬁxed integer.
(4) The condition that M is truncated (that is, that πiM ≃0 for i ≫0).
(5) The condition that M is has Tor-amplitude ≤n, where n is a ﬁxed integer.
(6) The condition that M is ﬂat.
(7) The condition that M is perfect to order n over A, where n is a ﬁxed integer.
(8) The condition that M is ﬁnitely n-presented over A, where n ≥0 is a ﬁxed integer.
(9) The condition that M is almost perfect over A.
(10) The condition that M is perfect over A.
Lemma 2.6.16. Let f : A →B be a faithfully ﬂat morphism of connective E∞-rings and let M be a left
A-module. If B ⊗A M has Tor-amplitude ≤n over B, then M has Tor-amplitude ≤n over A (see Deﬁnition
A.7.2.5.21).
Proof. Let N be a discrete A-module; we wish to show that M ⊗A N is n-truncated. Since B is faithfully
ﬂat over A, it suﬃces to show that
B ⊗A (M ⊗A N) ≃(B ⊗A M) ⊗B (B ⊗A N)
is n-truncated. This follows from our assumption that B ⊗A M has Tor-amplitude ≤n, since B ⊗A N is a
discrete B-module.
67
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 68
Proof of Proposition 2.6.15. Assertions (1) and (3) follow from Proposition A.7.2.2.13, and assertions (2)
and (4) follow immediately from (1) and (3). Assertion (5) follows from Lemma 2.6.16. Assertion (6) follows
from (5) and (1). Assertion (7) follows from Proposition 2.6.13 and Remark 2.6.9. Assertion (8) follows
from (1), (3), and (7).
Assertion (9) follows from (7).
Assertion (10) follows (5), (9), and Proposition
A.7.2.5.23.
Deﬁnition 2.6.17. Let P be a property of objects of CAlgcn ×CAlg Mod which is local with respect to the
fpqc topology, let X be a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack, and let F ∈QCoh(X). We will say that F has the
property P if, for every ´etale morphism f : Spec A →F, the pullback f ∗F ∈QCoh(Spec A) corresponds to
an A-module M such that (A, M) has the property P.
Let us spell out the contents of Deﬁnition 2.6.17 in the examples provided by Proposition 2.6.15. Let F
be a quasi-coherent sheaf on a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack X. We say that F is:
(1) n-connective if, for every ´etale map f : Spec A →X, the pullback f ∗F ∈ModA is n-connective. This
is equivalent to the requirement that F ∈QCoh(X)≥n.
(2) almost connective if, for every ´etale map f : Spec A →X, the pullback f ∗F ∈ModA is almost
connective.
If X is quasi-compact, then this condition is equivalent to the requirement that F ∈
QCoh(X)≥−n for some integer n.
(3) n-truncated if, for every ´etale map f : Spec A →X, the pullback f ∗F ∈ModA is n-truncated. This is
equivalent to the requirement that F ∈QCoh(X)≤n.
(4) locally truncated if, for every ´etale map f : Spec A →X, the pullback f ∗F ∈ModA is n-truncated for
some integer n. If X is quasi-compact, this is equivalent to the requirement that F ∈QCoh(X)≤n for
some integer n.
(5) of Tor-amplitude ≤n if, for every ´etale map f : Spec A →X, the pullback f ∗F ∈ModA is of Tor-
amplitude ≤n.
(6) ﬂat if, for every ´etale map f : Spec A →X, the pullback f ∗F ∈ModA is ﬂat.
(7) perfect to order n if, for every ´etale map f : Spec A →X, the pullback f ∗F ∈ModA is perfect to order
n.
(8) ﬁnitely n-presented if, for every ´etale map f : Spec A →X, the pullback f ∗F ∈ModA is ﬁnitely
n-presented.
(9) almost perfect if, for every ´etale map f : Spec A →X, the pullback f ∗F ∈ModA is almost perfect.
(10) perfect if, for every ´etale map f : Spec A →X, the pullback f ∗∈ModA is perfect.
Remark 2.6.18. In case (4), our terminology does not quite conform to the general convention of Deﬁnition
2.6.17. We use the term “locally truncated” rather than “truncated” to emphasize the fact that if F ∈
QCoh(X) is a quasi-coherent sheaf whose pullback f ∗F is truncated for every ´etale map f : Spec A →X,
then F need not belong to S QCoh(X)≤n unless we assume that X is quasi-compact.
We have the following analogue of Proposition 1.5.7:
Proposition 2.6.19. Let P be a property of objects of CAlgcn ×CAlg Mod which is local for the fpqc topology,
let X be a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack, and let F be a quasi-coherent sheaf on X. Then:
(1) If f : Y →X is a ﬂat morphism and F has the property P, then f ∗F has the property P. In particular,
if F is n-connective (almost connective, n-truncated, locally truncated, of Tor-amplitude ≤n, ﬂat,
perfect to order n, ﬁnitely n-presented, almost perfect, perfect), then f ∗F has the same property.
68
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 69
(2) If we are given a collection of ﬂat maps {fα : Yα →X} which induces a ﬂat covering ` Yα →X,
and each pullback f ∗
α F has the property P, then F has the property P. In particular, if each f ∗
α F is
n-connective (almost connective, n-truncated, locally truncated, of Tor-amplitude ≤n, ﬂat, perfect to
order n, ﬁnitely n-presented, almost perfect, perfect), then F has the same property.
Deﬁnition 2.6.20. Let X be a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack and let F be a quasi-coherent sheaf on X.
We will say that F is coherent if it is almost perfect and locally truncated. We let Coh(X) denote the full
subcategory of QCoh(X) spanned by the coherent sheaves.
Remark 2.6.21. Let X be a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack, and suppose we are given a ﬁber sequence
F′ →F →F′′
in the stable ∞-category QCoh(X). If any two of F′, F, and F′′ are coherent, so is the third. That is, Coh(X)
is a stable subcategory QCoh(X). Moreover, Coh(X) is idempotent complete.
Remark 2.6.22. Propositoin 2.6.19 implies that the property of being coherent satisﬁes descent for the ﬂat
topology. That is, if f : Y →X is a ﬂat morphism of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks, and F ∈Coh(X),
then f ∗F ∈Coh(Y). The converse holds when f is a ﬂat covering.
We will primarily be interested in the class of coherent sheaves in the locally Noetherian setting.
Proposition 2.6.23. Let X be a locally Noetherian spectral Deligne-Mumford stack, and let F ∈Coh(X).
For every integer n, the truncations τ≥n F and τ≤n F belong to Coh(X). In particular, the subcategories
Coh(X)≤0 = QCoh(X)≤0 ∩Coh(X)
Coh(X)≥0 = QCoh(X)≥0 ∩Coh(X)
determine a t-structure on Coh(X) (see Proposition 2.3.16).
Proof. We may assume without loss of generality that X is aﬃne, in which case the desired result follows
immediately from Proposition A.7.2.5.17.
Proposition 2.6.24. Let X be a locally Noetherian spectral Deligne-Mumford stack and let F be a quasi-
coherent sheaf on X. If F is ﬁnitely n-presented for some integer n, then F is coherent.
Proof. We may assume without loss of generality that X is aﬃne, of the form Spec´et A for some connective
E∞-ring A. Then F corresponds to an A-module M. Arguing as in Remark 2.6.7, we see that M is a retract
of τ≤nM ′, where M ′ is a perfect A-module. The coherence of F now follows from Proposition 2.6.23.
2.7
Quasi-Coherent Sheaves on a Functor
In §2.3, we introduced the notion of a quasi-coherent sheaf on a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack X. Every
spectral Deligne-Mumford stack X determines a functor X : CAlgcn →S, given informally by the formula
X(R) = MapStk(Spec R, X).
In §V.2.4, we saw that X can be recovered (up to canonical equivalence) from the functor X. More precisely,
the construction X 7→X determines a fully faithful embedding h : Stk →Fun(CAlgcn, S) (Theorem V.2.4.1).
In particular, the ∞-category QCoh(X) can be recovered (up to equivalence) from the functor X. Our goal
in this section is to make the passage from X to QCoh(X) explicit.
More generally, we explain how to associate to any functor X : CAlgcn →S an ∞-category QCoh(X),
which we call the ∞-category of quasi-coherent sheaves on the functor X. Roughly speaking, an object
F ∈QCoh(X) can be viewed as a rule which assigns to each point η ∈X(R) an R-module F(η), which
depends functorially on R in the following sense: if φ : R →R′ is a map of connective E∞-rings and η′
denotes the image of η in X(R′), then we have a canonical equivalence R′ ⊗R F(η) ≃F(η′). Moreover,
we require that this equivalence is compatible with composition of morphisms in CAlgcn, up to coherent
69
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 70
homotopy. The ﬁrst part of this section is devoted to turning the above discussion into a precise deﬁnition
(Deﬁnition 2.7.8). We will then show that, in the special case where X is the functor represented by a
spectral Deligne-Mumford stack X, there is a canonical equivalence of ∞-categories QCoh(X) ≃QCoh(X)
(Proposition 2.7.18).
We begin with some general remarks. Suppose we are given a simplicial set S and a map ψ : S →Catop

Proposition 2.7.6. Let q : X →S be a Cartesian ﬁbration classiﬁed by a functor χ : Sop →Cat∞, let

Remark 2.7.7. There are evident dual versions of the constructions described above. For every coCartesian

j : S →P(Sop)op.
Deﬁnition 2.7.8. Let CAlgcn ×CAlg Mod denote the ∞-category whose objects are pairs (A, M), where A
is a connective E∞-ring and M is an A-module spectrum. Let q : CAlgcn ×CAlg Mod →CAlgcn denote the
projection onto the ﬁrst factor, so that q is a coCartesian ﬁbration. We let QCoh : Fun(CAlg, bS)op →d

denote the functor Φ′(q) obtained by applying Remark 2.7.7 to q (after a change of universe); here bS denotes
the ∞-category of spaces which are not necessarily small, and d
Cat∞is deﬁned similarly.
If X : CAlgcn →bS is any functor, we will refer to QCoh(X) ∈d
Cat∞as the ∞-category of quasi-coherent
sheaves on X.
Remark 2.7.9. There is no real need to restrict to connective E∞-rings in Deﬁnition 2.7.8. Using exactly
the same procedure, we can associate to any functor X : CAlg →bS an ∞-category QCoh′(F) of quasi-
coherent sheaves on X. In some sense, this deﬁnition is strictly more general than that of Deﬁnition 2.7.8:
if X0 : CAlgcn →bS is any functor, then there is a canonical equivalence of ∞-categories QCoh(X0) ≃
QCoh′(X), where X : CAlg →bS is a left Kan extension of X0. However, for most of our applications it will
be convenient to consider functors which are deﬁned only on the full subcategory CAlgcn ⊆CAlg spanned
by the connective E∞-rings.

Proposition 2.7.17.
(1) For every functor X : CAlgcn →bS, the ∞-category QCoh(X) is stable and
admits small colimits.
(2) For every natural transformation morphism α : X →X′ in Fun(CAlgcn, bS), the pullback functor

is stable under colimits in X. Since X0 contains every aﬃne object U ∈X, we conclude that X0 = X. In
particular, X0 contains a ﬁnal object of X, which proves that α induces an equivalence QCoh(X) ≃χ(X, O) →

Many of the local properties of quasi-coherent sheaves discussed in §2.6 make sense in the context of
quasi-coherent sheaves on an arbitrary functor.
Deﬁnition 2.7.19. Let P be a property of objects of CAlgcn ×CAlg Mod. We will say that P is stable under
base change if, whenever a pair (A, M) has the property P and f : A →B is a map of connective E∞-rings,
the pair (B, B ⊗A M) also has the property P.
Proposition 2.7.20. The following conditions on an object (A, M) ∈Mod ×CAlg CAlgcn are stable under
base change:
(1) The condition that M is n-connective (when regarded as a spectrum), where n is a ﬁxed integer.
(2) The condition that M is almost connective: that is, M is (−n)-connective for n ≫0.
(3) The condition that M is has Tor-amplitude ≤n, where n is a ﬁxed integer.
(4) The condition that M is ﬂat.
(5) The condition that M is perfect to order n over A, where n is a ﬁxed integer.
(6) The condition that M is almost perfect over A.
(7) The condition that M is perfect over A.
Proof. Assertions (1), (2), and (7) are obvious. Assertion (3) follows from Lemma 2.6.16 and assertion (5)
from Proposition 2.6.13. Assertions (4) and (6) are immediate consequences of (3) and (5).
Deﬁnition 2.7.21. Let P be a property of objects of CAlgcn ×CAlg Mod which is invariant under base
change, and let X : CAlgcn →bS be a functor. We will say that an object F ∈QCoh(X) has the property P
if, for every connective E∞-ring R and every point η ∈X(R), the pair (R, F(η)) has the property P, where
F(η) ∈ModR is the R-module of Remark 2.7.12.
Remark 2.7.22. Let P be a property of objects of CAlgcn ×CAlg Mod which is stable under base change,
and let α : F →F ′ be a natural transformation between functors F, F ′ : CAlgcn →bS. If F ∈QCoh(F ′) has
the property P, then α∗F ∈QCoh(F) has the property P.
Example 2.7.23. Let R be a connective E∞-ring and let Specf R : CAlgcn →S be the functor corepresented
by R, so that Remark 2.7.10 gives an equivalence of ∞-categories θ : QCoh(Specf R) ≃ModR. If P is a prop-
erty of objects of CAlgcn ×CAlg Mod which is stable under base change, then an object F ∈QCoh(Specf R)
has the property P if and only if the pair (R, θ(F)) has the property P.
Remark 2.7.24. Let α : X →X′ be a natural transformation between functors X, X′ : CAlgcn →bS, and
suppose that α induces an equivalence after sheaﬁﬁcation with respect to the ﬂat topology. Proposition
2.7.14 implies that the pullback functor α∗: QCoh(X′) →QCoh(X) is an equivalence of ∞-categories. If P
is a property of objects of CAlgcn ×CAlg Mod which is stable under base change and F ∈QCoh(X′) has the
property P, then the pullback α∗F ∈QCoh(X) has the property P (Remark 2.7.22). The converse holds
provided that P is local with respect to the fpqc topology, in the sense of Deﬁnition 2.6.14.
Example 2.7.25. Let X : CAlgcn →bS be a functor and let F ∈QCoh(X). We say that F is connective if,
for every point η ∈X(R), the R-module F(η) is connective. We let QCoh(X)cn denote the full subcategory
of QCoh(X) spanned by the connective quasi-coherent sheaves on X. It is clear that QCoh(X)cn is closed
under small colimits and extensions in QCoh(X). If F satisﬁes condition (3) of Proposition 2.7.17, then the
proof of Proposition 2.7.17 shows that QCoh(X)cn is a presentable ∞-category. It follows from Proposition
A.1.4.5.11 that the pair of subcategories (QCoh(X)cn, QCoh(X)≤0) determines an accessible t-structure on
QCoh(X), where QCoh(X)≤0 denotes the full subcategory of QCoh(X) spanned by those objects G for which
the mapping space MapQCoh(F )(F[1], G) is contractible for all F ∈QCoh(X)cn.
74
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 75
For a general functor X : CAlgcn →bS (satisfying condition (3) of Proposition 2.7.17), there is no obvious
way to test that an object F ∈QCoh(X) belongs to QCoh(X)≤0. For example, the inclusion F ∈QCoh(X)
does not imply that F(η) ∈(ModR)≤0 when η ∈X(R), since the property of being 0-truncated is not
stable under base change. However, QCoh(X)≤0 does admit an explicit description in the case when X is
representable by an algebraic stack X: in this case, one has F ∈QCoh(X)≤0 if and only if F(η) ∈(ModR)≤0
whenever η classiﬁes a ﬂat morphism Spec R →X.
Proposition 2.7.26. Let X be a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack, let X : CAlgcn →S be the functor
represented by X, and let θ : QCoh(X) ≃QCoh(F) be the equivalence of Proposition 2.7.18. Let P be a
property of objects of CAlgcn ×CAlg Mod which is stable under base change and local for the fpqc topology.
An object F ∈QCoh(X) has the property P if and only if θ(F) ∈QCoh(F) has the property P.
Proof. The “only if” direction is obvious.
For the converse, let us suppose that F has the property P.
Let A be a connective E∞-ring and let f : Spec A →X be a map of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks; we
wish to show that the pair (A, f ∗F) has the property P. In verifying this, we are free to replace X by
any open substack through which f factors; we may therefore assume without loss of generality that X is
quasi-compact. Choose an ´etale surjection u : U →X, where U ≃Spec R is aﬃne. We can then choose a
faithfully ﬂat ´etale map A →A′ such that the composite map Spec A′ →Spec A
f→X factors through U.
Since P is local for the fpqc topology, we may replace A by A′ and thereby reduce to the case where f factors
through U. Then f ∗F ≃A ⊗R u∗F. Since P is stable under base change, we are reduced to proving that
the pair (R, u∗F) has the property P, which follows from our assumption that F has the property P.
Let CAlg(d
Cat∞) denote the ∞-category of (not necessarily small) symmetric monoidal ∞-categories. We
have an evident forgetful functor
θ : CAlg(d
Cat∞) →d
Cat∞,
which preserves limits. The functor R 7→ModR factors as a composition
CAlgcn U→CAlg(d
Cat∞)
θ→d
Cat∞,
where U assigns to each connective E∞-ring the symmetric monoidal ∞-category Mod⊗
R (see §A.4.4.3). Let
QCoh⊗: Fun(CAlgcn, bS)op →CAlg(d
Cat∞) be a right Kan extension of U along the Yoneda embedding
(CAlgcn)op →Fun(CAlgcn, bS)op.
Then the functor QCoh⊗assigns to each functor F : CAlgcn →bS a
symmetric monoidal ∞-category QCoh(F)⊗, whose underyling ∞-category can be identiﬁed with QCoh(F).
We can describe the situation more informally by saying that for every functor F : CAlgcn →bS, the ∞-
category QCoh(F) admits a symmetric monoidal structure. Unwinding the deﬁnitions, we see that the tensor
product on QCoh(F) is given pointwise: that is, it is described by the formula
(F ⊗F′)(η) ≃F(η) ⊗R F′(η)
for η ∈F(R). It follows that the tensor product on QCoh(F) preserves small colimits separately in each
variable.
Notation 2.7.27. Let X : CAlgcn →bS is any functor, we let OX denote the unit object of QCoh(X) (with
respect to the symmetric monoidal structure deﬁned above). More informally, OX assigns to each point
η ∈X(R) the spectrum R, regarded as a module over itself.
Proposition 2.7.28. Let X : CAlgcn →bS be a functor, and let F ∈QCoh(X). The following conditions
are equivalent:
(1) The quasi-coherent sheaf F is perfect.
(2) The quasi-coherent sheaf is a dualizable object of the symmetric monoidal ∞-category QCoh(X).
75
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 76
Proof. Using Proposition A.4.2.5.11, we can reduce to the case where X is corepresentable by a connective
E∞-ring R. In this case, we can identify F with an R-module M. We wish to show that M is a dualizable
object of ModR if and only if M is perfect. The collection of dualizable objects of ModR forms a stable
subcategory which is closed under retracts. Since R ∈ModR is dualizable, it follows that every perfect
object of ModR is dualizable. Conversely, suppose that M admits a dual M ∨. Then the functor N 7→
MapModR(M, N) is given by N 7→Ω∞(M ∨⊗R N), and therefore commutes with ﬁltered colimits. It follows
that M is a compact object of ModR, and therefore perfect (Proposition A.7.2.5.2).
Note that for any functor X : CAlgcn →bS, the full subcategory QCoh(X)cn ⊆QCoh(X) contains OX and
is closed under tensor products, and therefore inherits the structure of a symmetric monoidal ∞-category.
We close this section by describing the dualizable objects of QCoh(X)cn.
Deﬁnition 2.7.29. Let A be a connective E∞-ring and let M be an A-module. We will say that M is
locally free of ﬁnite rank if there exists an integer n such that M is a direct summand of An.
Remark 2.7.30. Let A be a connective E∞-ring and let M be an A-module which is locally free of ﬁnite
rank. Then π0M is a locally free π0A-module of ﬁnite rank (in the sense of classical commutative algebra).
We can therefore choose elements x1, . . . , xn ∈π0A which generate the unit ideal, such that each localization
(π0M)[ 1
xi ] is a free module over (π0A)[ 1
xi ] of ﬁnite rank. It then follows from Corollary A.7.2.2.19 that M[ 1
xi ]
is a free module over A[ 1
xi ]. In other words, the condition of Deﬁnition 2.7.29 implies that M is free locally
with respect to the Zariski topology on SpecZ A.
We now prove a converse to Remark 2.7.30.
Proposition 2.7.31. The property of being a locally free module of ﬁnite rank is stable under base change
and local with respect to the fpqc topology.
Proof. Let A be a connective E∞-ring. According to Proposition A.7.2.5.20, an A-module M is locally free
of ﬁnite rank if and only if M is ﬂat and almost perfect. The desired assertions now follow from Propositions
2.6.15 and 2.7.20.
If X : CAlgcn →bS is a functor and F ∈QCoh(X) is a quasi-coherent sheaf on X, then we will say that
F is locally free of ﬁnite rank if F(η) ∈ModR is locally free of ﬁnite rank for every point η ∈X(R) (in other
words, if F satisﬁes the condition of Deﬁnition 2.7.21, where P is the property of being locally free of ﬁnite
rank).
Proposition 2.7.32. Let R be a connective E∞-ring and let M be a connective R-module. The following
conditions are equivalent:
(1) The module M is locally free of ﬁnite rank.
(2) The module M is a dualizable object of the symmetric monoidal ∞-category Modcn
R .
Proof. The collection of dualizable objects of Modcn
R is evidently closed under the formation of retracts and
direct sums.
Since the unit object R ∈Modcn
R is dualizable, we conclude that (1) ⇒(2).
Conversely,
suppose that M is a dualizable object of Modcn
R . Then M is a dualizable object of ModR, and therefore
a perfect R-module (Proposition 2.7.28). Let M ∨denote the dual of M. For any discrete R-module N,
we have isomorphisms πi(M ⊗R N) ≃πi MapModR(M ∨, N) for i ≥0. Since M ∨is connective, we deduce
that πi(M ⊗R N) vanishes for i > 0. It follows that M is ﬂat, so that M is locally free of ﬁnite rank by
Proposition A.7.2.5.20.
Corollary 2.7.33. Let X : CAlgcn →bS be a functor and let F ∈QCoh(X)cn. Then F is a dualizable object
of QCoh(X)cn if and only if F is locally free of ﬁnite rank.
Proof. Combine Propositions A.4.2.5.11 and 2.7.32.
