3
Geometric Stacks and Tannaka Duality
In [39], we prove the following:
Theorem 3.0.1. Let X be a quasi-compact Artin stack, and assume that the diagonal map X →X × X is
aﬃne (for example, we can take X to be any quasi-compact separated scheme). Then for every scheme Y ,
the construction
(f : Y →X) 7→f ∗
determines a fully faithful embedding θ : Map(Y, X) →C. Here Map(Y, X) denotes the groupoid of maps
from Y to X (in the 2-category of Artin stacks) and C denotes the category of symmetric monoidal functors
from the abelian category of quasi-coherent sheaves on X to the abelian category of quasi-coherent sheaves on
Y . The essential image of θ consists of those functors F which preserve small colimits and carry ﬂat objects
to ﬂat objects.
In this section, we will prove an analogous result in the setting of spectral algebraic geometry. In place
of an stack X, we will consider an arbitrary functor X : CAlgcn →S. Our goal is to give conditions which
guarantee that X can be recovered from the ∞-category QCoh(X) of quasi-coherent sheaves on X (together
with the symmetric monoidal structure on QCoh(X)). Our main result (Theorem 3.4.2) implies that this
recovery is possible whenever X is a geometric stack.
The bulk of this section is devoted to developing the tools necessary to formulate and prove Theorem
3.4.2. We will begin in §3.1 with a general discussion of properties of morphisms of spectral Deligne-Mumford
stacks φ : X →Y which can be tested locally on Y. Such a property then makes sense in the more general
setting of natural transformations f : X →Y between functors X, Y : CAlgcn →S (see Deﬁnition 3.1.24).
In particular, we obtain a notion of quasi-aﬃne morphism of functors, which we will study in detail in §3.2
(globalizing some of the ideas introduced in §2.4).
We will say that a functor X : CAlgcn →S is a quasi-geometric stack if the diagonal morphism X →
X × X is quasi-aﬃne.
In §3.3, we will study the class of quasi-geometric stacks.
Our main result is a
prototype version of Theorem 3.0.1: if X is a quasi-geometric stack and QCoh(X) is presentable, then for
any functor Y : CAlgcn →S, the construction (f : Y →X) 7→f ∗determines a fully faithful embedding
from MapFun(CAlgcn,S)(Y, X) to the ∞-category Fun⊗(QCoh(X), QCoh(Y )) of symmetric monoidal functors
from QCoh(X) to QCoh(Y ) (Proposition 3.3.11). In general, we do not know a convenient description of
the essential image of the full faithful embedding
θ : MapFun(CAlgcn,S)(Y, X) →Fun⊗(QCoh(X), QCoh(Y ))
of Proposition 3.3.11. However, we can say more in the case where X is a geometric stack: that is, a functor
which admits a faithfully ﬂat aﬃne covering by a corepresentable functor (see Deﬁnition 3.4.1). In §3.4 we
will formulate and prove Theorem 3.4.2, which asserts that when X is geometric, a symmetric monoidal
functor F : QCoh(X) →QCoh(Y ) belongs to the essential image of θ if and only if F preserves small
colimits, connective objects, and ﬂat objects. It follows that the functor X is completely determined by the
∞-category QCoh(X) together with its symmetric monoidal structure and t-structure.
3.1
Local Properties of Morphisms
In §1.5, we studied properties of morphisms of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks f : X →Y which can be
tested locally on X. In this section, we will study properties which can be tested locally on Y. We begin by
introducing some terminology.
Deﬁnition 3.1.1. Let P be a property of morphisms f : X →Y between spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks.
We will say that f is local on the target with respect to the ´etale topology if the following conditions are
satisﬁed:
77
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 78
(1) Suppose we are given a pullback diagram of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks
X′
/
f ′

X
f

Y′
g
/ Y
where g is ´etale. If f has the property P, then f ′ also has the property P.
(2) Let f : X →Y be a map of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks, and suppose we are given a surjective
´etale morphism `
α Yα →Y . If each of the induced maps Yα ×Y X →Yα has the property P, then
f has the property P.
Remark 3.1.2. Let P be a property of morphisms between spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks which is local
on the target with respect to the ´etale topology. Then a morphism f : X →Y has the property P if and
only if, for every ´etale map u : Spec R →Y, the pullback Spec R ×Y X →Spec R has the property P.
Remark 3.1.3. Suppose we are given a property P0 of morphisms of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks
having the form Z →Spec R.
Let f : X →Y be an arbitrary morphism of spectral Deligne-Mumford
stacks. We will say that f locally has the property P0 if, for every ´etale map Spec R →Y, the induced map
Spec R ×Y X →Spec R has the property P0. Suppose that P0 satisﬁes the following conditions:
(i) Let f : Z →Spec R be a map of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks and u : R →R′ an ´etale morphism
of E∞-rings. If f has the property P0, then the induced map Spec R′ ×Spec R Z →Spec R has the
property P0. The converse holds if u is faithfully ﬂat.
(ii) If we are given a ﬁnite collection of morphisms {Zi →Spec Ri} having the property P0, then the
induced map ` Zi →Spec(Q Ri) has the property P0.
Then the condition that a morphism f locally has the property P0 is local on the target with respect to the
´etale topology, in the sense of Deﬁnition 3.1.1. Moreover, a morphism f : Z →Spec R satisﬁes this condition
if and only if f has the property P0 (this follows immediately from (i)). Combining this observation with
Remark 3.1.2, we obtain a bijective correspondence between the following:
(a) Properties P of arbitrary morphisms f : X →Y of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks, which are local
on the target with respect to the ´etale topology.
(b) Properties P0 of morphisms of the form f : Z →Spec R which satisfy conditions (i) and (ii).
Example 3.1.4. The condition that a morphism f : X →Y of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks be ´etale is
local on the target with respect to the ´etale topology.
Example 3.1.5. The condition that a morphism f : X →Y of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks be an
equivalence is local on the target with respect to the ´etale topology.
Example 3.1.6. The condition that a morphism f : X →Y of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks be an
open immersion is local on the target with respect to the ´etale topology. This follows from Examples 3.1.4
and 3.1.5, since f is an open immersion if and only if f is ´etale and the diagonal map X →X ×Y X is an
equivalence.
Example 3.1.7. The condition that a morphism f : X →Y of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks be surjective
is local on the target with respect to the ´etale topology (see Proposition 1.1.20).
Example 3.1.8. The condition that a morphism f : X →Y of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks be ﬂat is
local on the target with respect to the ´etale topology.
78
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 79
Example 3.1.9. For 0 ≤n ≤∞, the condition that a morphism f : X →Y of spectral Deligne-Mumford
stacks be n-quasi-compact is local on the target with respect to the ´etale topology (Proposition 1.4.11).
Example 3.1.10. For every integer n ≥0, the condition that a map of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks
f : (X, OX) →(Y, OY) induce an equivalence (X, τ≤n OX) →(Y, τ≤n OY) is local on the target with respect
ot the ´etale topology.
Deﬁnition 3.1.11. Let f : X →Y be a map of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks and let n ≥−2 be an
integer. We will say that f is a relative Deligne-Mumford n-stack if, for every discrete commutative ring R,
the induced map
MapStk(Spec R, X) →MapStk(Spec R, Y)
is n-truncated.
Example 3.1.12. When n = −2, a map of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks f : (X, OX) →(Y, OY) is a
relative Deligne-Mumford n-stack if and only if, for every discrete commutative ring R, the map
MapStk(Spec R, (X, OX)) →MapStk(Spec R, (Y, OY))
is a homotopy equivalence. This is equivalent to the requirement that f induces an equivalence (X, π0 OX) →
(Y, π0 OY).
Remark 3.1.13. The condition that a map f : (X, OX) →(Y, OY) be a relative Deligne-Mumford n-
stack depends only on the underlying map of 0-truncated spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks (X, π0 OX) →
(Y, π0 OY).
Remark 3.1.14. If n ≥0, then a morphism f : X →Y is a relative Deligne-Mumford n-stack if and only
if, for every discrete commutative ring R and every map u : Spec R →Y, the pullback Spec R ×Y X is a
spectral Deligne-Mumford n-stack (Deﬁnition 1.3.1). Using Remark 3.1.13, we see that this is equivalent to
assertion that for every connective E∞-ring R and every map u : Spec R →Y, the pullback Spec R ×Y X is
a spectral Deligne-Mumford n-stack.
Proposition 3.1.15. Let n ≥−2 be an integer. The condition that a map f : X →Y of spectral Deligne-
Mumford stacks be a relative spectral Deligne-Mumford n-stack is local on the target with respect to the
´etale topology.
Proof. The proof proceeds by induction on n. When n = −2, the desired result follows from Examples 2.7.12
and 3.1.9. If n > −2, we observe that f : X →Y is a relative spectral Deligne-Mumford n-stack if and only
if the diagonal map X →X ×Y X is a relative Deligne-Mumford (n −1)-stack.
Proposition 3.1.16. The condition that a map f : X →Y of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks be aﬃne is
local on the target with respect to the ´etale topology.
Proof. Using Remark 3.1.3, we are reduced to verifying the following assertion:
(∗) Let f : X →Spec R be a map of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks, and suppose there exists a faithfully
ﬂat ´etale morphism R →R0 such that the ﬁber product X0 = Spec R0 ×Spec R X is aﬃne. Then X is
aﬃne.
To prove (∗), let R• be the ˇCech nerve of the map R →R0 (in the ∞-category CAlgop). For each n ≥0, the
ﬁber product Spec Rn ×Spec R X is an aﬃne spectral Deligne-Mumford stack, of the form Spec An for some
E∞-ring An. Let A denote the totalization of the cosimplicial E∞-ring A•. It follows from Theorem VII.6.12
that A• is the ˇCech nerve of the morphism A →A0 ≃R0 ⊗R A. Write X = (X, OX), so that the simplicial
spectral Deligne-Mumford stack X• corresponds to a simplicial object U• in X, whose geometric realization
is a ﬁnal object 1 ∈X. Then we have a chain of equivalences
OX(1) = OX(|U•|) ≃lim
←−OX(U•) ≃lim
←−A• ≃A.
79
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 80
The composite equivalence determines a map θ : X →Spec A. The map θ is an equivalence, since it can
be obtained as the geometric realization of an equivalence of simplicial spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks
X• ≃Spec A•. This proves that X is aﬃne, as desired.
Proposition 3.1.17. The condition that a map of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks f : X →Y be quasi-
aﬃne is local on the target with respect to the ´etale topology.
Proof. Using Remark 3.1.3, we are reduced to proving the following:
(∗) Let f : X →Spec R be a map of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks, and suppose there exists a faithfully
ﬂat ´etale morphism R →R0 such that the ﬁber product X0 = Spec R0 ×Spec R X is quasi-aﬃne. Then
X is quasi-aﬃne.
Write X = (X, OX). We ﬁrst claim that the pushforward f∗OX is a quasi-coherent sheaf on Spec R. This
assertion can be tested locally with respect to the ´etale topology on Spec R, and therefore follows from
Corollary 2.5.15. We can identify f∗OX with an E∞-algebra over R. Let A denote the connective cover of
this E∞-algebra. The map A →f∗OX classiﬁes a map of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks g : X →Spec A.
We claim that g is a quasi-compact open immersion. Since this assertion is local on the target with respect to
the ´etale topology (Examples 3.1.6 and 3.1.9), we may replace R by R′ and thereby reduce to the case where
X is quasi-aﬃne. In this case, the desired result follows from Proposition 2.4.3 (see the proof of Proposition
2.4.8).
Recall that the ∞-category Stk of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks can be identiﬁed with a full sub-
category of Fun(CAlgcn, S) (the identiﬁcation is given by carrying a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack X to
the functor given informally by the formula R 7→MapStk(Spec R, X)). In §3.1, we studied local properties
of morphisms between Deligne-Mumford stacks f : X →Y. Many of these properties can be deﬁned more
generally for natural transformations between functors X, Y : CAlgcn →S.
Deﬁnition 3.1.18. Let f : X →Y be a natural transformation between functors X, Y : CAlgcn →bS.
We will say that f is representable if, for every connective E∞-ring R and every natural transformation
Specf R →Y (corresponding to a choice of point η ∈Y (R)), the ﬁber product X ×Y Specf R is representable
by a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack.
Proposition 3.1.19. Let f : X →Y be a natural transformation between functors X, Y : CAlgcn →bS.
Assume that Y is representable by a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack Y. Then f is representable (in the
sense of Deﬁnition 3.1.18) if and only if X is representable by a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack.
The proof will require the following general observation about sheaves.
Lemma 3.1.20. Let C be a small ∞-category equipped with a Grothendieck topology, and let α : F →F′ be
a natural transformation of functors F, F′ : Cop →S. For every object C ∈C and every point η ∈F′(C), we
deﬁne a functor Fη : C/C →S by taking the ﬁber of the induced transformation F | C/C →F′ | C/C (over the
point determined by η).
Assume that F′ is a sheaf on C. Then the following conditions are equivalent:
(1) The functor F is a sheaf on C.
(2) For every object C ∈C and every point η ∈F′(C), the functor Fη is a sheaf on C/C (with respect to
the induced Grothendieck topology).
Moreover, if F′ is hypercomplete, then F is hypercomplete if and only if each Fη is hypercomplete.
Proof. The implication (1) ⇒(2) is obvious, since the full subcategory Shv(C/C) ⊆Fun(Cop
/C, S) is closed
under small limits. Suppose that (2) is satisﬁed. Fix an object C ∈C and a covering sieve C(0)
/C ⊆C/C;
80
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 81
we wish to prove that the canonical map θ : F(C) →lim
←−F | C(0)
/C is a homotopy equivalence. We have a
commutative diagram
F(C)
θ
/

lim
←−F | C(0)
/C

F′(C)
θ′
/ lim
←−F′ | C(0)
/C,
where the map θ′ is a homotopy equivalence. Consequently, to show that θ is a homotopy equivalence, it will
suﬃce to show that θ induces a homotopy equivalence after passing to the homotopy ﬁber over any point
η ∈F′(C); this is precisely the content of assumption (2).
Now suppose that F′ is hypercomplete. Since the collection of hypercomplete sheaves on C/C is closed
under limits, it is easy to see that F is hypercomplete only if each Fη is hypercomplete. Conversely, suppose
that each Fη is hypercomplete; we wish to prove that F is hypercomplete. Choose an ∞-connective morphism
β : F →G, where G is hypercomplete; we wish to prove that β induces an equivalence βC : F(C) →G(C)
for each C ∈C. Since F′ is hypercomplete, the map α factors through β; it will therefore suﬃce to show
that βC induces a homotopy equivalence after passing to the homotopy ﬁber over every point η ∈F′(C).
For this, it suﬃces to show that the induced map βη : Fη →Gη is an equivalence. This is clear, since βη is
∞-connective and both Fη and Gη are hypercomplete objects of Shv(C/C).
Proof of Proposition 3.1.19. Let d
Shv´et denote the full subcategory of Fun(CAlgcn, S) spanned by those func-
tors which are sheaves with respect to the ´etale topology. Since the ∞-category of spectral Deligne-Mumford
stacks admits ﬁber products, it is clear that if X is representable by a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack, then
f is representable. To prove the converse, write Y = (Y, OY). For every object U ∈Y, let YU denote the
functor represented by the spectral Deligne-Mumford stack YU = (Y/U, OY |U), and let XU = X ×Y YU. Let
us say that U is good if XU is representable by a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack XU. Assuming that f is
representable by spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks, we will show that every object U ∈Y. Our assumption
immediately implies that every aﬃne object U ∈Y is good. It will therefore suﬃce to show that the col-
lection of good objects of Y is closed under small colimits (Lemma V.2.3.11). To this end, suppose we are
given a diagram of object {Uα} in Y having a colimit U, and that each XUα is representable by a spectral
Deligne-Mumford stack XUα. Note that for every morphism Uα →Uβ in our diagram, the induced map
XUα →XUβ is ´etale (since it is a pullback of the ´etale morphism YUα →YUβ). It follows from Proposi-
tion V.2.3.10 that the diagram {XUα} has a colimit XU in the ∞-category Stk. Moreover, XU represents a
functor F : CAlgcn →bS which is the colimit of the diagram {XUα} in the ∞-category d
Shv´et spanned by the
´etale sheaves (Lemma V.2.4.13). To prove that U is good, it will suﬃce to show that F ≃XU: that is, that
XU is the colimit of the diagram {XUα} in d
Shv´et. Since colimits in d
Shv´et are universal, we are reduced to
proving the following pair of assertions:
(a) The functor X is a sheaf with respect to the ´etale topology.
(b) The functor YU is a colimit of the diagram {YUα} in d
Shv´et.
Assertion (a) follows from Lemma 3.1.20, and assertion (b) follows from Lemma V.2.4.13.
Corollary 3.1.21. Suppose we are given natural transformations X
f→Y
g→Z of functors X, Y, Z :
CAlgcn →bS, and assume that g is representable. Then f is representable if and only if g ◦f is repre-
sentable.
Proof. Without loss of generality, we may assume that Z is corepresentable by a connective E∞-ring R. Then
Y is representable by a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack Y. The desired equivalence now follows immediately
from Proposition 3.1.19.

Remark 3.1.26. Let P a property of morphisms of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks which is local on the
target with respect to the ´etale topology and stable under base change. Let φ : X →Y be a morphism of
spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks, let X, Y : CAlgcn →S be the functors represented by X and Y, and let
f : X →Y be the natural transformation determined by φ. Then f has the property P if and only if φ has
the property P.
Example 3.1.27. The following conditions on a morphism f : X →Y are stable under base change (and
local on the target with respect to the ´etale topology, as we saw in §3.1):
(1) The condition that f is ´etale.
(2) The condition that f is an equivalence.
(3) The condition that f is an open immersion.
(4) The condition that f is a ﬂat.
82
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 83
(5) The condition that f is a relative Deligne-Mumford n-stack, where n ≥−2 is some ﬁxed integer (see
Deﬁnition 3.1.11).
(6) The condition that f : (X, OX) →(Y, OY) induces an equivalence (X, τ≤n OX) →(Y, τ≤n OY), where
n ≥0 is some ﬁxed integer.
(7) The condition that f is surjective.
(8) The condition that f is aﬃne.
(9) The condition that f is quasi-aﬃne.
Consequently, we make make sense of each of these conditions for an arbitrary representable morphism
f : X →Y between functors X, Y : CAlgcn →S.
Deﬁnition 3.1.28. Let f : X →Y be a representable morphism of functors X, Y : CAlgcn →S. We will
say that f is faithfully ﬂat if it is ﬂat and surjective (in the sense of Example 3.1.27).
Remark 3.1.29. Let P be a property of morphisms of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks which is local on
the target with respect to the ´etale topology and stable under base change. Let f : X →Y be a natural
transformation of functors X, Y : CAlgcn →bS, and suppose that Y is given as the colimit of a diagram {Yα}
in Fun(CAlgcn, bS). Then f is representable and has the property P if and only if each of the induced maps
X ×Y Yα →Yα satisﬁes the same conditions. The “only if” direction is obvious, and the converse follows
from the observation that every map map Spec A →Y factors through some Yα.
3.2
Quasi-Aﬃne Morphisms of Functors
Let f : X →Y be a representable morphism between functors X, Y : CAlgcn →S. Our goal in this section
is to show that if f is quasi-aﬃne, then there is a close relationship between the ∞-categories QCoh(X)
and QCoh(Y ). We begin with some general remarks about adjunctions between symmetric monoidal ∞-
categories.

Lemma 3.2.8. Suppose we are given natural transformations f : Y →X, g : Z →X in Fun(CAlgcn, bS).
Assume that f is representable and quasi-aﬃne, and let A = f∗OY ∈CAlg(QCoh(X)). Then the canonical
map
θ : MapFun(CAlgcn,bS)/X(Z, Y ) →MapCAlg(QCoh(Z))(g∗A, OZ)
is a homotopy equivalence.
Proof. When regarded as functors of Z, both the domain and codomain of θ carry colimits of functors to
limits of spaces. Writing Z as a colimit of corepresentable functors, we may reduce to the case where Z is
corepresentable by a connective E∞-ring R. Replacing X by Z and Y by the ﬁber product Y ×X Z, we may
reduce to the case where Y is representable by a quasi-aﬃne spectral Deligne-Mumford stack Y equipped
with a map Y →Spec R. In this case, the desired result is a consequence of Proposition 2.4.9.
Proposition 3.2.9. Let X : CAlgcn →bS be a functor, and assume that the ∞-category QCoh(X) is pre-
sentable. For every object f : Y →X of QAﬀX, the ∞-category QCoh(Y ) is presentable. The construction
(f : Y →X) 7→QCoh(Y )⊗determines a fully faithful embedding QAﬀX →CAlg(PrL)QCoh(X)/.
Proof. For every map f : Y →X which is representable by quasi-aﬃne spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks, let
AY = f∗OX ∈CAlg(QCoh(X)). Proposition 3.2.5 supplies an equivalence QCoh(Y ) ≃ModAY (QCoh(X)).
Since QCoh(X) is assumed to be presentable, we deduce that QCoh(Y ) is presentable. If we are given

another map g : Z →X which is representable and quasi-aﬃne, then Corollary A.6.3.5.18 supplies a
homotopy equivalence
MapCAlg(PrL)QCoh(X)/(QCoh(Y ), QCoh(Z)) ≃MapCAlg(QCoh(X))(AY , AZ) ≃MapCAlg(QCoh(Z))(g∗AY , OZ).
The desired result now follows from Lemma 3.2.8.
Remark 3.2.10. Suppose we are given maps of functors f : Y →X and g : Z →X which are representable
by quasi-aﬃne spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks. Proposition 3.2.9 implies that if F : QCoh(Z) →QCoh(Y )
is a QCoh(X)-linear symmetric monoidal functor which preserves small colimits, then F is given by pullback
along some map h : Y →Z such that h◦f ≃g. Moreover, if we are given two such maps h, h′ : Y →Z, then
any QCoh(X)-linear symmetric monoidal equivalence α between h∗and h′∗can be lifted to a homotopy
between h and h′. In fact, the assumption that α is an equivalence is superﬂuous: any QCoh(X)-linear
symmetric monoidal functor from h∗to h′∗is automatically an equivalence (Remark A.6.3.5.8).
Proposition 3.2.9 gives in particular a fully faithful embedding
AﬀX →CAlg(PrL)QCoh(X)/.
Using Corollary A.6.3.5.18, we can describe the essential image of this embedding:
Proposition 3.2.11. Let Y : CAlgcn →bS be a functor for which the ∞-category QCoh(Y ) is presentable,
and let AﬀY be deﬁned as above. Then the construction
(f : X →Y ) 7→QCoh(X)
determines a fully faithful embedding Aﬀop
Y →CAlg(PrL)QCoh(Y )/. Moreover, a symmetric monoidal functor
f ∗: QCoh(Y ) →C lies in the essential image of θ if and only if the following conditions are satisﬁed:
(a) The ∞-category C is presentable, the tensor product on C preserves colimits separately in each variable.
(b) The functor f ∗admits a right adjoint f∗.
(c) The functor f∗is conservative and preserves geometric realizations of simplicial objects.
(d) For every quasi-coherent sheaf F on X and every object C ∈C, the canonical map F ⊗f∗(C) →
f∗(f ∗F ⊗C) is an equivalence in QCoh(Y ).
(e) The object f∗OX ∈QCoh(X) is connective, where 1 denotes the unit object of C.
Proof. In view of Proposition 3.2.9, it will suﬃce to show that if f ∗: QCoh(X) →C is a symmetric monoidal
functor satisfying conditions (a) through (e), then C has the form QCoh(Y ) for some map f : Y →X which
is representable and aﬃne. Equivalently, we must show that there is a symmetric monoidal equivalence
C ≃ModA(QCoh(X)), for some connective object A ∈CAlg(QCoh(X)). The existence of A follows from
conditions (a) through (d), together with Corollary A.6.3.5.18. In this case, the commutative algebra A is
given by f∗1, so that the connectivity of A follows from condition (e).
Remark 3.2.12. In the situation of Proposition 3.2.11, we can replace (c) by the following apparently
stronger condition:
(c′) The functor f∗is conservative and preserves small colimits.

3.3
Quasi-Geometric Stacks
Let X : CAlgcn →bS be a functor. Our goal in §3.4) is to address the following general question:
(∗) To what extent is the functor X determined by the ∞-category QCoh(X) of quasi-coherent sheaves
on X?
In this section, we consider a slightly easier question:
(∗′) Let f : X →Y be a morphism between functors X, Y : CAlgcn →bS. To what extent is f determines
by the pullback functor f ∗: QCoh(Y ) →QCoh(X)?
Our main result is Proposition 3.3.11, which asserts that f can be recovered from f ∗(as a symmetric
monoidal functor) provided that the functor Y is quasi-geometric in the sense of the following deﬁnition:
Deﬁnition 3.3.1. Let f : X →Y be a natural transformation between functors X, Y : CAlgcn →bS. We
will say that f is quasi-geometric if the diagonal map X →X ×Y X is representable and quasi-aﬃne.
We will say that a functor X : CAlgcn →bS is quasi-geometric if the projection map X →∗is quasi-
geometric, where ∗denotes a ﬁnal object of Fun(CAlgcn, bS).
Warning 3.3.2. The deﬁnition of a quasi-geometric morphism f : X →Y does not require that f be
representable.
The class of quasi-geometric morphisms enjoys the following closure property:
Proposition 3.3.3. Suppose we are given a commutative diagram

g

f

in Fun(CAlgcn, bS), and assume that g is quasi-geometric. Then f is quasi-geometric if and only if h is
quasi-geometric.
To prove Proposition 3.3.3, we ﬁrst need to establish the analogous assertion for the class of quasi-aﬃne
morphisms. This will require a few preliminary observations.
Lemma 3.3.4. Let f : X = (X, OX) →Y be a quasi-aﬃne morphism of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks,
and let A = τ≥0f∗OX. Then the canonical map X →SpecY A is a quasi-compact open immersion.
Proof. The assertion is local on Y. We may therefore assume that Y is aﬃne, in which case the desired
result follows from Proposition 2.4.3 (and the proof of Proposition 2.4.8).
Lemma 3.3.5. Let Y be a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack and let A ∈CAlg(QCoh(Y)cn). If Y is quasi-
aﬃne, then SpecY A is quasi-aﬃne.
Proof. Write Y = (Y, OY) and let 1 denote a ﬁnal object of Y. Let R = OY(1) and A = A(1). Proposition

Proposition 3.3.6. Let f : X →Y be a map of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks.
Suppose that Y is
quasi-aﬃne and that f is quasi-aﬃne. Then X is quasi-aﬃne.
Proof. Write X = (X, OX). Since f is quasi-aﬃne, the pushforward f∗OX is a quasi-coherent sheaf of E∞-
rings on Y. Let A = τ≥0f∗OX be its connective cover, and let X′ = SpecY A. Lemma 3.3.4 implies that
the canonical map X →X′ is a quasi-compact open immersion. It will therefore suﬃce to show that X′ is
quasi-aﬃne, which follows from Lemma 3.3.5.
Proposition 3.3.7. Suppose we are given a commutative diagram

in Fun(CAlgcn, bS), and assume that g is representable and quasi-aﬃne. Then f is representable and quasi-

Proof. Suppose ﬁrst that f is representable and quasi-aﬃne. Choose a point η ∈Z(R), classifying a natural
transformation Specf R →Z. Our hypothesis on g implies that Y ×Z Specf R is representable by a quasi-
aﬃne spectral Deligne-Mumford stack Y. Our hypothesis on f guarantees that X ×Z Specf R is representable
by a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack X, and that the map X →Y is quasi-aﬃne. Applying Proposition
3.3.6, we deduce that X is quasi-aﬃne.
Now suppose that h is representable by quasi-aﬃne spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks; we wish to show
that f has the same property. Choose a point η ∈Y (R), classifying a map Specf R →Y . We wish to show
that X ×Y Specf R is representable by a quasi-aﬃne spectral Deligne-Mumford stack. Our hypotheses on g
and h imply that the ﬁber products X ×Z Specf R and Y ×Z Specf R are representable by quasi-aﬃne spectral
Deligne-Mumford stacks X and Y. Then X ×Y Specf R is representable by the ﬁber product X ×Y Spec R.
Since Y is quasi-aﬃne, the map Spec R →Y is aﬃne, so the projection map X ×Y Spec R →X is aﬃne.
Invoking Proposition 3.3.6, we deduce that X ×Y Spec R is quasi-aﬃne, as desired.
Proof of Proposition 3.3.3. The diagonal map δ : X →X ×Z X factors as a composition

Here δ′′ is a pullback of the diagonal map Y →Y ×Z Y .
Our assumption on g guarantees that δ′′ is
representable and quasi-aﬃne. Invoking Proposition 3.3.7, we deduce that δ′ is representable and quasi-
aﬃne if and only if δ is representable and quasi-aﬃne.
Deﬁnition 3.3.8. Let f : X →Y be a map of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks. We will say that f is
quasi-geometric if it induces a quasi-geometric morphism X →Y of functors X, Y : CAlgcn →S. We will
say that X is quasi-geometric if the functor X : CAlgcn →S represented by X is quasi-geometric. In other
words, X is quasi-geometric if the diagonal morphism X →X × X is quasi-aﬃne.
Remark 3.3.9. The condition that a map f : X →Y of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks be quasi-
geometric is local on the target with respect to the ´etale topology and stable under base change.
This
follows immediately from the corresponding assertion for quasi-aﬃne morphisms (Example 3.1.27).

quasi-aﬃne. The desired result now follows from Proposition 2.4.10.

proof that the unit map X →Y is an equivalence, it will suﬃce to show that it is an eﬀective epimorphism
with respect to the ´etale topology. Choose a point η ∈Y (R) for some connective E∞-ring R. For every map
of connective E∞-rings R →A, let ηA denote the image of η in Y (A). We wish to prove that there exists a

is faithfully ﬂat, and each ηAα belongs to the essential image of the map X(Aα) →Y (Aα). Let A0 = Q

therefore classiﬁes a map of spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks φn : Spec An →X.
Choose a quasi-compact open substack X′ ⊆X such that φ0 factors through X′. Since X′ is quasi-compact,
we can choose an ´etale surjection U →X′, where U is aﬃne. For each n ≥0, let Vn = U ×X Spec An. Since
X is quasi-geometric, each Vn is a quasi-aﬃne spectral Deligne-Mumford stack over An. Using Proposition

such that V ×Spec R Spec A• ≃V•.
Since U →X′ is surjective, the map V0 →Spec A0 is surjective.
Since A0 is faithfully ﬂat over R, the composite map V0 →Spec A0 →Spec R is surjective. It follows
that V →Spec R is surjective. Using Lemma 1.2.12, we deduce that V →Spec R is ´etale. Choose an
´etale surjection Spec R′ →V, so that R′ is a faithfully ﬂat ´etale R-algebra. By construction, the point
ηR′ ∈Y (R′) lifts to X(R′).
We now begin to address the question raised at the beginning of this section.
Proposition 3.3.11. Let X, Y : CAlgcn →bS be functors. Assume that X is quasi-geometric and that the
∞-category QCoh(X) is presentable. Then the canonical map
θ : MapFun(CAlgcn,bS)(Y, X) →Fun⊗(QCoh(X), QCoh(Y ))
is fully faithful. Here Fun⊗(QCoh(X), QCoh(Y )) denotes the ∞-category of symmetric monoidal functors
from QCoh(X) to QCoh(Y ). In particular, if we are given maps f, f ′ : Y →X, then any QCoh(X)-linear
symmetric monoidal natural transformation α : f ∗→f ′∗is an equivalence.
Remark 3.3.12. In the situation of Proposition 3.3.11, the hypothesis that QCoh(X) be presentable can
be removed. We make this assumption to simplify the exposition (and because it is satisﬁed in all examples
of interest).
Proof of Proposition 3.3.11. Let us regard X as ﬁxed. The constructions

reduce to the case where Y is corepresented by a connective E∞-ring R. Since X is quasi-geometric, any map

3.4
Geometric Stacks
Consider a pair of functors X, Y : CAlgcn →bS. Suppose that X is quasi-geometric and that the ∞-category
QCoh(X) is presentable. Proposition 3.3.11 implies that a natural transformation f : Y →X is determined
(up to a contractible space of choices) by the symmetric monoidal functor f ∗: QCoh(X) →QCoh(Y ). In
this section, we ask the following question:
(∗) Which symmetric monoidal functors QCoh(X) →QCoh(Y ) are given by pullback along a natural
transformation of functors Y →X?
To obtain a reasonable answer, we will need to strengthen our assumptions on X.
Deﬁnition 3.4.1. Let X : CAlgcn →bS be a functor. We will say that X is a geometric stack if the following
conditions are satisﬁed:
(1) The functor X is a sheaf with respect to the ﬂat topology on CAlgcn.
(2) There exists a map f : X′ →X in Fun(CAlgcn, bS) with the following properties:
(a) The functor X′ is corepresentable by a connective E∞-ring A.
(b) The map f is representable, aﬃne, and faithfully ﬂat.
The main result of this section is the following:
Theorem 3.4.2 (Tannaka Duality for Geometric Stacks). Let Y be a geometric stack and X : CAlgcn →bS
an arbitrary functor. Then the construction
(f : X →Y ) 7→(f ∗: QCoh(Y ) →QCoh(X))
determines a fully faithful embedding
MapFun(CAlgcn,bS)(X, Y ) →Fun⊗(QCoh(Y ), QCoh(X)),
whose essential image is the full subcategory of Fun⊗(QCoh(Y ), QCoh(X)) spanned by those symmetric
monoidal functors f ∗: QCoh(Y ) →QCoh(X) which preserve small colimits, connective objects, and ﬂat
objects.
Remark 3.4.3. We will show in a moment that every geometric stack is quasi-geometric (Lemma 3.4.6).
Consequently, Theorem 3.4.2 can be regarded as a strengthening of Proposition 3.3.11.
91
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 92
Remark 3.4.4. A close relative of Theorem 3.4.2 (under some more restrictive hypotheses) is proven in
[18]).
We will give the proof of Theorem 3.4.2 at the end of this section.
First, let us study some of the
properties enjoyed by the class of geometric stacks.
Remark 3.4.5. Let f : X →Y be a natural transformation of functors X, Y : CAlgcn →bS which is
representable and aﬃne. Assume that Y is a sheaf with respect to the ﬂat topology. Then X is a sheaf with
respect to the ﬂat topology (see Proposition 3.4.11). Moreover, if f is faithfully ﬂat, then it is an eﬀective
epimorphism of ﬂat sheaves.
Lemma 3.4.6. Let X : CAlgcn →bS be a functor. The following conditions are equivalent:
(1) Let A be a connective E∞-ring and f : Specf A →X a natural transformation (classiﬁed by a point
η ∈X(A)). Then f is representable and aﬃne.
(2) For every pair of connective E∞-rings A and B and every pair of morphisms Specf A →X ←Specf B,
the ﬁber product Specf A ×X Specf B is a corepresentable functor.
(3) The diagonal map X →X × X is aﬃne.
Proof. The equivalence of (1) and (2) is clear.
We next prove that (2) ⇒(3).
Choose any morphism
Specf A →X × X, corresponding to a pair of maps f, g : Specf A →X. We have an equivalence
Specf A ×X×X X ≃(Specf A ×X Specf A) ×Specf A×Specf A Specf A.
Condition (2) guarantees that the functor Specf A ×X Specf A is corepresentable. Since the collection of
corepresentable functors CAlgcn →bS is closed under small limits, we conclude that Specf A ×X×X X is
corepresentable.
Now assume that (3) holds; we will prove (2). Let f : Specf A →X and g : Specf B →X be morphisms.
We have an equivalence
Specf A ×X Specf A ≃Specf(A ⊗B) ×X×X X.
Since the diagonal map X →X × X is aﬃne, we conclude that Specf A ×X Specf B is corepresentable.

Shvfpqc denote the full subcategory of Fun(CAlgcn, bS) spanned by those functors

Corollary 3.4.14. Let X be a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack, and let X : CAlgcn →S be the functor
represented by X. The following conditions are equivalent:
(1) The functor X is a geometric stack.
(2) The spectral Deligne-Mumford stack X is quasi-compact, and the diagonal map X →X × X is aﬃne.
Proof. Suppose ﬁrst that (1) is satisﬁed. Then there exists a faithfully ﬂat aﬃne morphism Spec A →X,
which proves that X is quasi-compact. It follows from Proposition 3.4.11 that the diagonal X →X × X is
aﬃne.
Conversely, suppose that X satisﬁes condition (2). Since X is quasi-compact, we can choose a surjective
´etale morphism Spec A →X. Because the diagonal of X is aﬃne, the induced map f : Specf A →X is
representable by aﬃne spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks. It is clear that f is faithfully ﬂat (in fact, it is even
´etale). To complete the proof, it will suﬃce to show that X is a sheaf with respect to the ﬂat topology. This
follows from Proposition 3.3.10, since X is quasi-geometric.
Deﬁnition 3.4.15. We will say that a spectral Deligne-Mumford stack X is geometric if it satisﬁes the
equivalent conditions of Corollary 3.4.14.
Lemma 3.4.16. Let P be a property of pairs (A, M) ∈CAlgcn ×CAlg Mod(Sp) which is stable under base
change and local with respect to the fpqc topology. Let X be a geometric stack and choose a faithfully ﬂat
morphism f : Specf A →X. Let F ∈QCoh(X) and set M = f ∗F ∈QCoh(Specf A) ≃ModA. Then F has
the property P if and only if the pair (A, M) has the property P. In particular, F is connective if and only
if M is connective.
Proof. The “only if” assertion is obvious. Conversely, suppose that the pair (A, M) has the property P. Let
B be a connective E∞-ring, g : Specf B →X be an arbitrary morphism, and N = g∗F ∈QCoh(Specf B) ≃
ModB. We wish to show that the pair (B, N) has the property P. We have Specf A ×X Specf B = Specf B′
for some connective E∞-ring B′ which is faithfully ﬂat over B. Since P is local with respect to the fpqc
topology, we may replace g by the induced map Specf B′ →X, and thereby reduce to the case where g
factors through f. In this case, the desired result follows immediately from our assumption that P is stable
under base change.
Proposition 3.4.17. Let X : CAlgcn →bS be a geometric stack. Then:
(1) The ∞-category QCoh(X) is presentable.
(2) There exists an accessible t-structure (QCoh(X)≥0, QCoh(X)≤0) on QCoh(X), where QCoh(X)≥0 is
the full subcategory of QCoh(X) spanned by the connective objects.

(4) The t-structure on QCoh(X) is compatible with ﬁltered colimits: that is, QCoh(X)≤0 is closed under
ﬁltered colimits.
(5) The t-structure on QCoh(X) is both right and left complete.

that QCoh(X) is presentable.
Since QCoh(X)≥0 can be described as the ﬁber product QCoh(X) ×ModA (ModA)≥0, it is presentable

Remark 3.4.18. Let X : CAlg →bS be a geometric stack. It is clear from the deﬁnitions that the t-structure
on QCoh(X) is compatible with the symmetric monoidal structure on QCoh(X): that is, the collection of
connective objects contains the structure sheaf OX and is stable under tensor products.
Remark 3.4.19. Let f : X →Y be a representable aﬃne morphism between geometric stacks, and let F
be a quasi-coherent sheaf on X. Then:
(a) The sheaf F belongs to QCoh(X)≥0 if and only if f∗F belongs to QCoh(Y )≥0.
(b) The sheaf F belongs to QCoh(X)≤0 if and only if f∗F belongs to QCoh(Y )≤0.
We will prove (a); the proof of (b) is similar. Choose a connective E∞-ring A and a faithfully ﬂat aﬃne
morphism g : Specf A →Y .
Then Specf A ×Y X ≃Specf B for some connective A-algebra B; let g′ :
Specf B →X denote the projection map. Note that F ∈QCoh(X)≥0 if and only if M = g′∗F is a connective
B-module, and f∗F is connective if and only if N = g∗f∗F is a connective A-module. The desired result
now follows by observing that N can be identiﬁed with the underlying A-module of M (Corollary 3.2.6).
Remark 3.4.20. Let X be a geometric stack, and let F ∈QCoh(X). The following conditions are equivalent:
(1) The quasi-coherent sheaf F is ﬂat.
(2) The operation of tensor product with F determines a t-exact functor from QCoh(X) to itself.

and a map f : Specf A →X. We wish to prove that f ∗F ∈QCoh(Specf A) ≃ModA is ﬂat. Let M be a

Remark 3.4.22. Let f : X →Y be a representable aﬃne morphism between functors X, Y : CAlgcn →bS.

(3) The composition θ ◦f ∗is right t-exact.

In other words, we must show that θ restricts to an equivalence from QCoh(X)≥0 to (ModA)≥0. Since θ is

The requirement that a symmetric monoidal functor f ∗: QCoh(Y ) →QCoh(X) preserve ﬂat objects is
sometimes technically inconvenient. When X is corepresentable, it can be avoided:
Corollary 3.4.29. Let Y be a geometric stack, X : CAlgcn →S a corepresentable functor, and f ∗:
QCoh(Y ) →QCoh(X) a symmetric monoidal functor. The following conditions are equivalent:
(1) The functor f ∗belongs to the essential image of the fully faithful embedding MapFun(CAlgcn,bS)(X, Y ) →
Fun⊗(QCoh(Y ), QCoh(X)) of Theorem 3.4.2.
(2) The functor f ∗preserves small colimits, connective objects, and ﬂat objects.
(3) The functor f ∗admits a right adjoint f∗: QCoh(X) →QCoh(Y ) which is left t-exact. Moreover, for
every pair of objects F ∈QCoh(Y ), F′ ∈QCoh(X), the canonical map F ⊗f∗F′ →f∗(f ∗F ⊗F′) is an
equivalence.
Proof. The equivalence (1) ⇔(2) follows from Theorem 3.4.2. We next prove that (1) ⇒(3). Suppose that f ∗
is given by the pullback functor associated to a natural transformation f : X →Y . Since X is corepresentable
and Y is geometric, the morphism f is representable and aﬃne. It follows that A = f∗OX is a connective
object of CAlg(QCoh(Y )), and we can identify f∗with the forgetful functor ModA(QCoh(Y )) →QCoh(Y ),
for which assertion (3) is evident.
We complete the proof by showing that (3) ⇒(2). The existence of a right adjoint f∗implies that
f ∗preserves small colimits, and the right t-exactness of f ∗is equivalent to the left t-exactness of f∗. Let
F ∈QCoh(Y ) be ﬂat; we wish to prove that f ∗F ∈QCoh(X) is ﬂat. Since QCoh(X) ≃ModA for some
connective E∞-ring A, it will suﬃce to show that for every discrete object F′ ∈QCoh(X), the tensor
product (f ∗F) ⊗F′ ∈QCoh(X) is discrete.
It is clear that (f ∗F) ⊗F′ is connective; it will therefore
suﬃce to show that (f ∗F) ⊗F′ ∈QCoh(X)≤0. Equivalently, we wish to show that the abelian groups

QCoh(X)(OX, (f ∗F) ⊗F′)) are trivial for i < 0. We have
Exti

QCoh(Y )(OY , f∗((f ∗F) ⊗F′)) ≃Exti
QCoh(Y )(OY , F ⊗f∗F′).
Since OY is connective, it is suﬃcient to show F ⊗f∗F′ belongs to QCoh(Y )≤0. Using the ﬂatness of F, we
are reduced to proving that f∗F′ ∈QCoh(Y )≤0. This follows from the left t-exactness of f∗.
Remark 3.4.30. Let X be a geometric stack. Corollary 3.4.29 implies that for every connective E∞-ring
A, we can describe the space X(A) in terms of the symmetric monoidal ∞-category QCoh(X), together
with its t-structure. If we are willing to restrict our attention to the case where A is a discrete E∞-ring,
then we can do better: X(A) can be identiﬁed with a full subcategory of the ordinary category of symmetric

4
Coaﬃne Stacks
Let X be an aﬃne scheme, and let R = Γ(X; OX) be the commutative ring OX(X) of global sections of OX.
Then:
(a) We can recover X as the spectrum Specc R. More precisely, for any scheme Y , the canonical map
Hom(Y, X) →Hom(R, Γ(Y ; OY )) is bijective; here the Hom-set on the left is computed in the category
of schemes, while the Hom-set on the right is computed in the category of commutative rings.
(b) The global sections functor F 7→Γ(X; F) determines an exact functor from the abelian category
of quasi-coherent sheaves on X to the category of abelian groups.
This construction underlies an
equivalence between the category of quasi-coherent sheaves on X and the category of R-modules.
Assertion (a) is essentially the deﬁnition of an aﬃne scheme. Under some mild additional hypotheses,
one can show that (b) is also equivalent to the requirement that X be aﬃne.
In the setting of spectral algebraic geometry, there is a large class of geometric objects which are not
aﬃne but nonetheless exhibit the sort of “aﬃne behavior” embodied by statements (a) and (b). For example,
suppose that X = (X, OX) is a quasi-aﬃne spectral Deligne-Mumford stack, and let R = Γ(X; OX) be the
E∞-ring of global sections of O.
For every spectral Deligne-Mumford stack Y = (Y, OY), the canonical
map MapStk(Y, X) →MapCAlg(R, Γ(Y; OY)) is a homotopy equivalence (Proposition 2.4.9), and the global
sections functor F 7→Γ(X; F) induces an equivalence of ∞-categories QCoh(X) →ModR (Proposition 2.4.4).
Let us now describe another example of the same phenomenon. Let k be a ﬁeld of characteristic zero, and
let CAlg0
k denote the ∞-category of discrete E∞-algebras over k (so that CAlg0
k is equivalent to the nerve of
the ordinary category of commutative k-algebras). We let BGa denote the classifying stack of the additive
group over k. We will identify BGa with a functor CAlg0

denotes the classifying space of the additive group of R. Let A denote the E∞-ring of global sections of the
structure sheaf of G⅁: that is, the limit
lim
←−

It follows that A can be identiﬁed with the free E∞-algebra Sym∗k[−1] on one generator (in homological

MapCAlgk(A, R) ≃MapModk(k[−1], R) ≃BR ≃BGa(R).
In other words, the classifying stack BGa satisﬁes a version of condition (a) above, and can be recovered
from its structure sheaf. One can also prove an analogue of (b) (thereby describing the derived category of
representations of the additive group Ga): see Proposition 4.5.2.
In this section, we will study a large class of geometric objects which exhibit behavior similar to that
of the classifying stack BGa, which we call coaﬃne stacks. Roughly speaking, a coaﬃne stack over a ﬁeld
k of characteristic zero is a functor X : CAlg0
k →S such that each of the spaces X(R) is connected, and
each of the functors R 7→πiX(R) (deﬁned relative to a choice of base point η ∈X(k)) is representable by a
prounipotent group scheme over k.
Remark 4.0.1. Our actual deﬁnition of coaﬃne stack will be slightly diﬀerent: we will consider functors
deﬁned on the larger ∞-category CAlgcn
k of all connective E∞-algebras over k. However, the distinction is
ultimately irrelevant: every coaﬃne stack is determined by its value on discrete k-algebras: see Proposition
4.4.6.
101
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 102
Our main goal in this section is to prove that coaﬃne stacks satisfy analogues of the statements (a) and
(b) given above. More precisely, every coaﬃne stack X is given by the formula X(R) = MapCAlgk(A, R) for
a canonically determined object A ∈CAlgk. Moreover, we can explicitly describe those objects A ∈CAlgk
which arise in this manner: they are precisely those algebras for which the unit map k →A exhibits k as a
connective cover of A. The collection of E∞-algebras over k satisfying this condition span a full subcategory
CAlgcc
k ⊆CAlgk. We will study this ∞-category in detail in §4.1 and §4.3. To carry out our analysis, we will
need some general facts about ∞-categories which are generated by noncompact projective objects, which
we explain in §4.2.
In §4.4, we will deﬁne a coaﬃne stack to be a functor X : CAlgcn →S which is corepresented by a
coconnective E∞-algebra A. We will show that A is uniquely determined (Theorem 4.4.1), and show that
the class of coaﬃne stacks can be characterized in the manner described above (Proposition 4.4.8).
Our next goal is to prove an analogue of (b) in the setting of coaﬃne stacks. In §4.5, we will study the
∞-category of quasi-coherent sheaves QCoh(X) on a coaﬃne stack X. We will see that QCoh(X) admits
a t-structure, whose heart is equivalent to the category of algebraic representations of π1X, regarded as a
prounipotent group scheme over the ﬁeld k. Moreover, we will show that there is a global sections functor
QCoh(X) →ModA which is fully faithful when restricted to the subcategory S
n QCoh(X)≤n (Proposition
4.5.2). Moreover, we can recover QCoh(X) as the completion of the stable ∞-category ModA with respect
to suitable t-structure.
Our ﬁnal objective in this section is to prove that coaﬃne stacks satisfy a version of Tannaka duality.
More precisely, suppose that we are given functors X, Y : CAlgcn →S, where X arises from a coaﬃne stack
over a ﬁeld of characteristic zero. We will prove that there is a fully faithful embedding
MapFun(CAlgcn,S)(Y, X) →Fun⊗(QCoh(X), QCoh(Y )),
whose essential image is the collection of symmetric monoidal functors F : QCoh(X) →QCoh(Y ) which
preserve small colimits and connective objects.
We will prove this in §4.6 (see Corollary 4.6.19) as an
application of some general remarks about completions of stable ∞-categories.
Remark 4.0.2. The notion of a coaﬃne stack was introduced by To¨en, who uses the term aﬃne stack.
We refer the reader to [63] for an exposition of To¨en’s theory of aﬃne stacks (over a ﬁeld of arbitrary
characteristic), which contains many of the results that we treat in this section.
4.1
Coconnective Algebras
Recall that an E∞-ring A is said to be connective if it is connective when regarded as a spectrum: that is, if
the homotopy groups πiA vanish for i < 0. The collection of all connective E∞-rings spans a full subcategory
CAlgcn ⊆CAlg, which is closed under small colimits in CAlg.
In this section, we will study E∞-ring A which satisfy the following alternative connectivity condition:
(∗) The homotopy groups πiA vanish for i > 0.
Though condition (∗) is formally analogous to the requirement that A is connective, it behaves quite diﬀer-
ently in practice. For example, if A is an arbitrary E∞-ring, then the spectrum τ≥0A inherits the structure
of a (connective) E∞-ring, which is universal among connective E∞-rings B equipped with a map B →A.
There is no analogous procedure for “killing” the higher homotopy groups of an E∞-ring: the spectrum
τ≤0A generally does not admit a ring structure. Consequently, the full subcategory C ⊆CAlg spanned by
those E∞-ring satisfying (∗) is poorly behaved. To obtain any kind of reasonable theory, it is necessary to
introduce a stronger version of condition (∗).
Deﬁnition 4.1.1. Let k be a ﬁeld and let A be an E0-algebra over k: that is, A is a k-module spectrum
equipped with a unit map u : k →A. Let n ≥1 be an integer. We will say that A is n-coconnective if the
following conditions are equivalent:
(1) The unit map u induces an isomorphism of abelian groups k →π0A.
102
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 103
(2) The homotopy groups πiA vanish for i > 0 and −n < i < 0.
If A is an Em-algebra over k for 0 ≤m ≤∞, then we will say that A is n-coconnective if it is n-coconnective
when regarded as an E0-algebra. We will say that A is coconnective if it is 1-coconnective: that is, if the
unit map u : k →A exhibits k as a connective cover of A.
We let CAlgcc
k denote the full subcategory of CAlgk spanned by the coconnective E∞-algebras over k.
The remainder of this section is devoted to establishing some of the formal properties of the ∞-category
CAlgcc
k in the case where k is a ﬁeld of characteristic zero. For example, we will show that every coconnective
E∞-algebra A over k can be built out of coconnective free algebas (Proposition 4.1.4), and use this to prove
that the ∞-category CAlgcc
k is compactly generated (Proposition 4.1.8).
Notation 4.1.2. Let k be a ﬁeld. We let Sym∗: Modk →CAlgk denote a left adjoint to the forgetful
functor. For every k-module spectrum V , the underlying k-module of Sym∗(V ) is given by L
n≥0 Symn(V ),
where Symn(V ) denotes the coinvariants for the natural action of the symmetric group Σn on V ⊗n (see
§A.3.1.3). We let Sym>0(V ) denote the coproduct L
n>0 Symn(V ); it can be identiﬁed with the ﬁber of the
canonical map of E∞-algebras Sym∗(V ) →k (induced by the zero map V →k).
Lemma 4.1.3. Let k be a ﬁeld of characteristic zero, let A be a coconnective E∞-algebra over k, and V
a k-module spectrum. Let m ≥1 be an integer such that πnV ≃0 for n ≥−m. Suppose we are given a

4.2
Digression: Strong Projectivity
Let R be an associative ring and let M be a left R-module. Recall that a projective resolution of M is an
exact sequence of left R-modules
· · · →P2 →P1 →P0 →M →0
where each Pi is projective. Using the Dold-Kan correspondence (§A.1.2.3), we can identify the chain complex
P∗with a simplicial left R-module P•. The exactness of the sequence
P1 →P0 →M →0
108
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 109
implies that M can be described as the geometric realization |P•| in the abelian category of left R-modules.
The exactness in higher degrees leads to a stronger assertion: we can identify M with the geometric realization
|P•| in the ∞-category LModR of left R-module spectra. This a special case of a much more general assertion:
if R is any connective E1-ring and M is a connective left module over R, then we can describe M as the
geometric realization of a simplicial object P• of LModR, where each Pn is projective. We can describe the
situation informally by saying that the ∞-category LModcn
R is generated by projective objects. Our goal in
this section is to make a general study of this phenomenon.
Let C be an arbitrary ∞-category which admits small colimits. Recall that an object P ∈C is said to
be projective it the functor C 7→MapC(P, C) preserves geometric realizations of simplicial objects. The
collection of projective objects of C comprise a full subcategory Cproj ⊆C. We would like to show that, in
good cases, we can functorially recover the ∞-category C from the subcategory Cproj. Note that if P• and
Q• are simplicial objects of Cproj, then we can describe the mapping space
MapC(|P•|, |Q•|) ≃lim
←−
[m]
MapC(Pm, |Q•|) ≃lim
←−
[m]
| MapCproj(Pm, Qn)|
entirely in terms of the mapping spaces in Cproj. The main question, then, is to decide when an arbitrary
object C ∈C can be obtained as a geometric realization |P•|, where each Pn is projective. In this case,
we have a map u : P0 →C which is surjective in the following sense: for every projective object Q ∈C,
every map Q →C factors through u up to homotopy. We might attempt to construct P0 by taking a large
coproduct of projective objects of C which map to C. However, we encounter the following technical obstacle:
though the collection of projective objects of C is always stable under ﬁnite coproducts (Remark T.5.5.8.19),
it is generally not stable under inﬁnite coproducts (for example, a discrete space is not a projective object
of S if it has inﬁnitely many path components). To address this point, we introduce a stronger notion of
projectivity.
Deﬁnition 4.2.1. Let C be an ∞-category which admits geometric realizations. We will say that an object
P ∈C is strongly projective if P corepresents a functor e : C →S with the following property:
(∗) For every simplicial object X• in C, the simplicial space e(X•) is a hypercovering of e(|X•|).
Remark 4.2.2. Every strongly projective object of an ∞-category C is projective.
Remark 4.2.3. Let C be the full subcategory of Fun(N(∆op
+ ), S) spanned by those augmented simplicial
spaces X• whose underlying simplicial space is a hypercovering of X−1. Then C is stable under products in
Fun(N(∆op
+ ), S): this follows from the observation that the collection of eﬀective epimorphisms in S is stable
under products.
Proposition 4.2.4. Let C be an ∞-category which admits geometric realizations. Then the collection of
strongly projective objects of C is stable under all coproducts which exist in C.
Proof. This is an immediate consequence of Remark 4.2.3.
The following result guarantees that ∞-categories of an algebraic ﬂavor have a good supply of strongly
projective objects.
Proposition 4.2.5. Let C be an ∞-category which admits geometric realizations, and let P be a cogroup
object of C (that is, a group object of the opposite ∞-category Cop). Then P is projective if and only if it is
strongly projective.
The proof of Proposition 4.2.5 will require some preliminaries.
Proposition 4.2.6. Let X be an ∞-topos, Grp(X) the ∞-category of group objects of X (which we regard
as a full subcategory of the ∞-category Fun(N(∆)op, X) of simplicial objects of X), and K a sifted simplicial
109
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 110
set. Suppose we are given a pullback diagram
W
/

X

Y
/ Z
in the ∞-category Fun(K▷, Grp(X)) satisfying the following conditions:
(i) The functors X, Y , and Z are colimit diagrams.
(ii) For every vertex v of K, the map Y (k) →Z(k) induces an eﬀective epimorphism in X.
Then W is a colimit diagram in Grp(X).
Proof. We observe that condition (ii) is also satisﬁed when v is the cone point of K◁, since the collection of
eﬀective epimorphisms in X is stable under colimits.
Since X is an ∞-topos, the formation of geometric realizations determines an equivalence of ∞-categories
from Grp(X) to the ∞-category X≥1

of pointed connected objects of X. Applying this equivalence, we have
a commutative diagram σ :
W ′
/

X′

Y ′
/ Z′
of functors from K◁to X∗. Since the forgetful functor Grp(X) →X is conservative and preserves sifted
colimits, we deduce that X′, Y ′, and Z′ are colimit diagrams, and we wish to prove that W ′ is a colimit
diagram. Using Lemma A.5.3.6.17, we are reduced to proving that σ is a pullback square. The diagram σ
is evidently a pullback square in Fun(K◁, X≥1
∗), so it will suﬃce to show that the ﬁber product X′ ×Z′ Y ′
(formed in the larger ∞-category Fun(K◁, X∗)) belongs to Fun(K◁, X≥1
∗). In other words, we wish to show
that for every vertex v ∈K◁, the ﬁber product X′(v) ×Z′(v) Y ′(v) is a connected object of X. Since the map
Y (v) →Z(v) is an eﬀective epimorphism, we deduce that its delooping Y ′(v) →Z′(v) is 1-connective. It
follows that the projection map X′(v) ×Z′(v) Y ′(v) →X′(v) is 1-connective. The desired result now follows
from the observation that X′(v) is connected.
Corollary 4.2.7. Let X be an ∞-topos, let X• be a simplicial object in the ∞-category Grp(X). Then X• is
a hypercovering of its geometric realization |X•|.
Proof. Without loss of generality, we may suppose that X is the essential image of a left exact localization
functor L : P(C) →P(C), for some small ∞-category C. We may assume without loss of generality that
X• ≃LY•, for some simplicial object Y• of Grp(P(C)) (for example, we can take Y• = X•). Since L : P(C) →X
preserves colimits, we have an equivalence L|Y•| ≃|X•|. Since L preserves hypercoverings, it will suﬃce to
show that Y• is a hypercovering of |Y•|. For this, we need only show that Y•(C) is a hypercovering of |Y•(C)|
in S, for each object C ∈C. In other words, we may assume that X is the ∞-topos S of spaces.

Corollary 4.2.8. Let Grp(S) denote the ∞-category of group objects of S. Let F : Fun(N(∆)op, Grp(S)) →S
denote the composition of the forgetful functor Fun(N(∆)op, Grp(S)) →Fun(N(∆)op, S) with the geometric
realization functor Fun(N(∆)op, S) →S. Then F commutes with small products.
Proof. It suﬃces to show that the collection of augmented simplicial objects of Grp(S) which determine
colimit diagrams in S is stable under products. This follows immediately from Corollary 4.2.7 together with
Remark 4.2.3.
Proof of Proposition 4.2.5. It is obvious that if P ∈C is strongly projective, then P is projective. For the
converse, we observe that because P is a cogroup object, the functor e : C →S corepresented by P can be
lifted to a functor e : C →Grp(S). It follows from Corollary 4.2.7 that e carries every simplicial object X•
of C to a hypercovering of |e(X•)|. If e is projective, then this geometric realization can be identiﬁed with
e|X•|.
Let C be a presentable ∞-category, and assume that there exists a set of of compact projective generators
for C (Deﬁnition T.5.5.8.23). Let C0 ⊆C be the full subcategory of C spanned by the compact projective
objects. According to Proposition T.5.5.8.25, the ∞-category C is equivalent to PΣ(C0), the full subcategory
of Fun(Cop
0 , S) spanned by those functors which preserve ﬁnite products. Our goal for the remainder of this
section is to establish a similar reconstruction result in case where the projective generators which are not
assumed to be compact.
Deﬁnition 4.2.9. Let C be an ∞-category. We will say that C is a socle if the following conditions are
satisﬁed:
(1) The ∞-category C is locally small.
(2) The ∞-category C admits small coproducts.
(3) There exists an essentially small full subcategory C0 ⊆C with the following property: every object
C ∈C is a retract of a coproduct `
α Cα, where each Cα ∈C0.
(4) Every object C ∈C has the structure of a cogroup (that is, we can regard C as a group object of Cop).
If C is a socle, we let Pσ(C) denote the full subcategory of Fun(Cop, S) spanned by those functors which
preserve small products.
Example 4.2.10. Let C be a pointed ∞-category which admits ﬁnite colimits. Then for every object X ∈C,
the suspension ΣX is a cogroup object of C. In particular, if the suspension functor Σ : C →C is essentially
surjective (for example, if C is stable), then C satisﬁes condition (4) of Deﬁnition 4.2.9.
Proposition 4.2.11. Let C be a socle. Then:
(1) The full subcategory Pσ(C) ⊆Fun(Cop, S) contains the essential image of the Yoneda embedding j :
C →Fun(Cop, S) and is stable under small limits in Fun(Cop, S).
(2) The full subcategory Pσ(C) ⊆Fun(Cop, S) is closed under the formation of geometric realizations of
simplicial objects.
(3) When regarded as a functor from C to Pσ(C), the Yoneda embedding j preserves small coproducts.
(4) For every object X ∈Pσ(C), there exists an augmented simplicial object X• ∈Pσ(C) with the following
properties:
112
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 113
(a) For n ≥0, the object Xn belongs to the essential image of j.
(b) The augmented simplicial object X• is a hypercovering; that is, for every C ∈C, the augmented
simplicial space X•(C) is a hypercovering. In particular, X• is a colimit diagram in Fun(Cop, S).
(c) We have an equivalence X−1 ≃X.
(5) The Pσ(C) is the smallest full subcategory of Fun(Cop, S) which contains the essential image of the
Yoneda embedding and is stable under geometric realizations of simplicial objects.
(6) Let D be any ∞-category which admits geometric realizations, and let Funσ(Pσ(C), D) denote the
full subcategory of Fun(Pσ(C), D) spanned by those functors which preserve geometric realization of
simplicial objects. Then composition with the Yoneda embedding induces an equivalence of ∞-categories
Funσ(Pσ(C), D) →Fun(C, D).
Proof. Assertion (1), (3) and the implication (4) ⇒(5) are obvious.
Assertion (4) is a special case of
Proposition A.7.2.1.4, and the implication (5) ⇒(6) follows from Remark T.5.3.5.9. We next prove (2).
Let X• be a simplicial object of Pσ(C), and let X denote the geometric realization |X•| formed in the ∞-
category Fun(Cop, S). We wish to prove that X ∈Pσ(C). In other words, we wish to show that if {Cα} is
a small collection of objects of C having a coproduct C ∈C, then the natural map X(C) →Q

The desired result now follows from Corollary 4.2.8.
Corollary 4.2.12. Let C be a socle and let X ∈Pσ(C). Then X is a projective object of Pσ(C) if and only
if X is a retract of j(C) for some C ∈C; here j : C →Pσ(C) ⊆Fun(Cop, S) denotes the Yoneda embedding.
Proof. Since Pσ(C) is stable under geometric realizations in Fun(Cop, S), we deduce immediately that j(C) ∈
Pσ(C) is projective for each C ∈C, and consequently any retract of j(C) is projective. Conversely, suppose
that X ∈Pσ(C) is projective, and write X as the geometric realization of a simplicial object X• as in the
proof of Proposition 4.2.11. Since X is projective, the identity map X ≃|X•| lies in the image of the map
MapPσ(C)(X, X0) →MapPσ(C)(X, |X•|). It follows that X is a retract of X0 ∈j(C).
Corollary 4.2.13. Let C be a socle. Then the ∞-category Pσ(C) is locally small.
Proof. Let D ⊆Pσ(C) be the full subcategory spanned by those objects X for which the mapping space
MapPΣ(C)(X, Y ) is essentially small for every Y ∈Pσ(C). We wish to prove that D = Pσ(C). In view of
Proposition 4.2.11, this follows from the following pair of observations:
(a) The full subcategory D contains the essential image of the Yoneda embedding j : C →Fun(Cop, S),
since MapPσ(C)(j(C), Y ) ≃Y (C).
(b) The full subcategory D is stable under geometric realizations of simplicial objects, since the collection
of essentially small spaces is stable under the formation of totalizations of cosimplicial objects.

Proposition 4.2.15. Let C be a socle, let D be an ∞-category which admits geometric realizations of
simplicial objects, let F : Pσ(C) →D be a functor which preserves geometric realizations of simplicial
objects, and let f denote the composition C
j→Pσ(C)
F→D (so that F is determined by f up to equivalence,
by Proposition 4.2.11). Assume that:
(i) The functor f is fully faithful.
114
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 115
(ii) For every object C ∈C, the image f(C) ∈D is projective.
Then F is fully faithful. Moreover, F is an equivalence if and only if the following additional conditions are
satisﬁed:
(iii) Let α : D →D′ in D be a morphism such that for every object C ∈C, composition with α induces a
homotopy equivalence MapD(f(C), D) →MapD(f(C), D′). Then α is an equivalence.
(iv) The functor f preserves small coproducts.
Proof. Let X denote the full subcategory of Pσ(C) spanned by those objects X such that, for every object
Y ∈Pσ(C), the map MapPσ(C)(X, Y ) →MapD(F(X), F(Y )) is a homotopy equivalence. We wish to prove
that X = Pσ(C). Since F preserves geometric realizations of simplicial objects, the full subcategory X is
stable under geometric realizations in Pσ(C). It will therefore suﬃce to show that X contains the essential
image of j.
Fix an object C ∈C, and let Y ⊆Pσ(C) be the full subcategory spanned by those objects Y such that
the map Y (C) ≃MapPσ(C)(j(C), Y ) →MapD(f(C), F(Y )) is an equivalence. Since j(C) is a projective
object of Pσ(C) (Corollary 4.2.12), f(C) is a projective object of D (by assumption (ii)), and F preserves
geometric realizations of simplicial objects, we conclude that Y is stable under geometric realizations of
simplicial objects in Pσ(C). It will therefore suﬃce to show that Y contains the essential image of j. That
is, we wish to show that if C′ ∈C, then the canonical map MapPσ(C)(j(C), j(C′)) →MapD(f(C), f(C′)) is
a homotopy equivalence. This follows from assumption (i), since the Yoneda embedding j : C →Pσ(C) is
fully faithful.
This completes the proof that F is fully faithful. Note that if F is an equivalence of ∞-categories, then
condition (iii) is obvious and (iv) follows from Proposition 4.2.11. Conversely, suppose that (iii) and (iv)
are satisﬁed. Using (iv), we deduce the existence of a functor G : D →Pσ(C), given informally by the
formula G(D)(C) = MapD(f(C), D). This functor is right adjoint to F, so that F is essentially surjective if
and only if G is conservative: this is equivalent to condition (iii).
4.3
Elementary Coconnective Algebras
Let k be a ﬁeld of characteristic zero, and let CAlgcc
k denote the ∞-category of coconnective E∞-algebras
over k.
Our goal in this section is to show that that the ∞-category (CAlgcc

(noncompact) projective objects, which we will call elementary coconnective k-algebras. We begin with a
concrete description of these k-algebras.
Proposition 4.3.1. Let k be a ﬁeld of characteristic zero, and let A be an E∞-algebra over k. The following
conditions are equivalent:
(1) The k-algebra A is coconnective and πiA ≃0 for i ≤−2.
(2) There exists a k-vector space V such that A is equivalent to the square-zero extension k ⊕V [−1].
Deﬁnition 4.3.2. We will say that a k-algebra A is elementary if it satisﬁes the equivalent conditions of
Proposition 4.3.1.
Remark 4.3.3. Proposition 4.3.1 asserts that if a coconnective k-algebra A satisﬁes πiA ≃0 for i ≤−2,
then A is formal: that is, it is determined up to equivalence by its homotopy groups π∗A.
Proof of Proposition 4.3.1. The implication (2) ⇒(1) is clear. Suppose that A satisﬁes (1), and let V =
π−1A. We will prove that there exists a map of k-algebras φ : A →k ⊕V [−1] such that the induced map

It will therefore suﬃce to show that the upper horizontal map is an equivalence for m ≥n + 1, which follows
from our assumption that C is equivalent to an n-category.
Notation 4.3.6. Let C be an ∞-category which admits small limits. It follows that the diagonal embed-
ding C →Fun(N(∆), C) admits a right adjoint, which carries each cosimplicial object X• of C to its limit
lim
←−[n]∈∆Xn. We will denote this functor by Tot : Fun(N(∆)op, C) →C, and refer to it as the totalization
functor.
Corollary 4.3.7. Let Sp≤0 denote the full subcategory of Sp spanned by those spectra X such that πiX ≃0
for i > 0. Then the totalization functor Tot : Fun(N(∆)op, Sp≤0) →Sp≤0 commutes with ﬁltered colimits.
Proof. It suﬃces to show that for every integer n, the (Ω∞−n◦Tot) : Fun(N(∆), Sp≤0) →S preserves ﬁltered

The last functor preserves ﬁltered colimits by Proposition T.5.3.3.3 (since N(∆s,≤n+1) has only ﬁnitely many

Lemma 4.3.8. Let k be a ﬁeld, let V • and W • be cosimplicial objects of Modk, and assume that πnV • ≃

θ : (Tot V •) ⊗k (Tot W •) →Tot(V • ⊗W •)

Proof. For every integer m, let V (m)• = τ≥−mV •, so that V • ≃lim

By the inductive hypothesis, we may assume that θm−1 is an equivalence; we are therefore reduced to proving
that ψ is an equivalence. Replacing V • by V (m)•/V (m −1)•[m], we are reduced to the situation where V •
is a cosimplicial discrete k-module. Similarly, we may assume that W • is a cosimplicial discrete k-module.
In this case, we can identify V • and W • with cosimplicial vector spaces over k. One can show that the map
θ induces on homotopy the classical Alexander-Whitney isomorphism
H∗(V •) ⊗k H∗(W •) →H∗(V • ⊗W •).
However, one can also argue more indirectly as follows.
Let A denote the abelian category of cochain
complexes of vector spaces
0 →U 0 →U 1 →· · ·
By the Dold-Kan correspondence, there is an equivalence of categories of A with the category of cosimplicial
k-vector spaces, which assigns to each cosimplicial vector space V • its normalized cochain complex 0 →
N(V )0 →N(V )1 →· · · . Let A0 denote the full subcategory of A spanned by those objects which correspond
to cosimplicial vector spaces V • such that the map θ : (Tot V •) ⊗k (Tot W •) →Tot(V • ⊗W •) is an
equivalence, for every cosimplicial k-vector space W •.
We wish to prove that A0 = A.
We make the
following observations:
(a) The subcategory A0 ⊆A is stable under ﬁltered colimits and ﬁnite direct sums, and therefore under
arbitrary direct sums.
(b) Given an exact sequence 0 →U ′• →U • →U ′′• →0 in A, if U • and U ′′• belong to A0, then U ′•
belongs to A0.
(c) Any acyclic complex belongs to A0 (in this case, the domain and codomain of θ are both zero objects).
(d) Since k is a ﬁeld, every object of A can be written as a direct sum of indecomposable objects
{C(n), D(n)}, where C(n) is the cochain complex · · · →0 →k →0 →· · · with k appearing in
cohomological degree n, and D(n) is the cochain complex · · · →0 →k
id
→k →0 →· · · with k
appearing in cohomological degreees n and n + 1.
By virtue of (a) and (d), it will suﬃce to show that for each n, the chain complexes C(n) and D(n) belong
to A0. The proof proceeds by induction on n. Since D(n) is acyclic, it belongs to A0 by (c). The exact
sequence 0 →C(n+1) →D(n) →C(n) →0 shows that C(n) ∈A0 implies C(n+1) ∈A0. We are therefore
reduced to proving that C(0) ∈A0. This is clear: the corresponding cosimplicial k-vector space V • is the
constant cosimplicial vector space k, and the map θ corresponds to the composition
(Tot V •) ⊗k (Tot W •) ≃k ⊗k Tot W • ≃Tot W • ≃Tot(V • ⊗k W •).

AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 119

Lemma 4.3.16. Let k be a ﬁeld, let A be a coconnective E1-algebra over k, and let η : A →k be a map of
E1-algebras over k, which induces a forgetful functor θ : Modk →LModA. Let M be a left A-module such
that πiM ≃0 for i̸ = 0. Then M belongs to the essential image of θ.
Proof. Let M ′ denote the image of M in Modk. We will prove that there exists a map α : M →θ(M ′) which
induces the identity map
π0M
α→π0θ(M ′) ≃π0(M ′) ≃π0M.
Such a map is automatically an equivalence and will therefore exhibit M as belonging to the essential image
of θ.
Supplying the map α is equivalent to giving a map of k-module spectra k ⊗A M →M ′. The collection
of homotopy classes of such maps can be identiﬁed with the k-vector space Homk(π0(k ⊗A M), π0M ′).
Consequently, constructing a map α is equivalent to splitting the map of k-vector spaces φ : π0M →
π0(k ⊗A M). Such a splitting exists, since φ is injective by Corollary 4.1.12.
Proof of Proposition 4.3.15. Let M = k, regarded as an A-module via the map η, and let
0 = M(0) →M(1) →M(2) →· · ·
be the sequence of A-modules constructed in the proof of Proposition 4.1.9, so that M(n + 1) is the coﬁber
of a map A ⊗k V (n) →M(n) where V (n) = ﬁb(M(n) →M). We prove the following by simultaneous
induction on n:
123
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 124
(an) The homotopy groups πiM(n) vanish for i /∈{0, −1}, and the map π0M(n) →π0M ≃k is injective.
(bn) The k-module spectrum V (n)[1] is discrete; that is, πiV (n) ≃0 for i̸ = −1.
Assertion (a0) is clear, and the implication (an) ⇒(bn) follows from the long exact sequence
πi+1M →πiV (n) →πiM(n) →πiM.
We claim that (an) and (bn) imply (an+1). To prove this, we consider the exact sequence
πiM(n) →πiM(n + 1) →πi−1(A ⊗k V (n)).
If i /∈{0, 1}, the left side vanishes by (an), and the right side vanishes by (bn) together with our assumption
that A is elementary. The injectivity of the map π0M(n) →π0M was established in the proof of Proposition
4.1.9.
We now prove (1).
We wish to show that Am is discrete for m ≥0.
For m = 0 this is clear; we
establish the general case using induction on m. We have Am ≃A1 ⊗k Am−1. The collection of discrete
k-module spectra is stable under tensor products, it will suﬃce to show that A1 is discrete.
We have
A1 ≃k ⊗A k ≃lim
−→k ⊗A M(n); it therefore suﬃces to show that each k ⊗A M(n) is discrete. We now proceed
by induction on n, the case n = 0 being obvious. If n > 0, then we have an exact sequence
πi(k ⊗A M(n)) →πi(k ⊗A M(n + 1)) →πi−1V (n).
If i̸ = 0, then the outer terms vanish (by the inductive hypothesis and (bn)) so that πi(k ⊗A M(n + 1)) ≃0
as desired.
We now prove (2). Let F : ModA →Fun(N(∆+), Modk) denote the functor given by M 7→M ⊗A A•.
We wish to show that F(A) is a limit diagram in Sp. The map η gives a coﬁber sequence of A-modules
A →k →M, whence a coﬁber sequence of diagrams F(A) →F(k) →F(M). Consequently, to prove
that F(A) is a limit diagram, it will suﬃce to show that F(k) and F(M) are limit diagrams. Since A is
elementary, we have πiM ≃0 for i̸ = 0. It follows from Lemma 4.3.16 that M belongs to the essential image
of the functor θ : Modk →ModA determined by η. To complete the proof, we will establish the following:
(∗) Let V be a k-module spectrum. Then F(θ(V )) admits the structure of a split augmented cosimplicial
object of Modk (see Deﬁnition A.6.2.1.2), and therefore a limit diagram in Modk (Lemma T.6.1.3.16).
To prove (∗), we note that F(θ(V )) can be identiﬁed with the composite functor
N(∆+)
F (k)
→Modk
⊗kV
→Modk .
It therefore suﬃces to show that F(k) admits the structure of a split augmented cosimplicial object of Modk.
This follows from the observation that F(k) can be identiﬁed with the cosimplicial object [n] 7→An+1.
Remark 4.3.17. In the proof of Proposition 4.3.15, it is possible to be much more precise. Let A = k⊕W[−1]
for some k-vector space W. An induction on n shows that each of the vector spaces V (n) appearing in the
proof can be identiﬁed with the nth tensor power W ⊗n; we therefore obtain a ﬁltration of A1 = k ⊗A k by
subspaces F nA1 ≃k ⊗A M(n) such that the successive quotients F nA1/F n−1A1 are isomorphic to W ⊗n.
Note that, for every discrete k-algebra R, the set Hom(A1, R) = π0 MapCAlgk(A1, R) ≃π1 MapCAlgk(A, R)
has a natural group structure: thus A1 can be regarded as a commutative Hopf algebra over k. This Hopf
algebra can be identiﬁed with the tensor algebra T(W) = L
n W ⊗n, equipped with the classical shuﬄe
product. If W is a vector space of ﬁnite dimension m, then A1 ≃T(W) is the algebra of functions on the
prounipotent completion of a free group on m generators.

4.4
Coaﬃne Stacks
Let k be a connective E∞-ring, and let A ∈CAlgk.
We let cSpec A : CAlgcn
k
→S denote the functor
corepresented by A, given by R 7→MapCAlgk(A, R). The construction A 7→cSpec A can be regarded as
a restricted Yoneda embedding.
It not fully faithful in general.
For example, if the ring π∗A contains
invertible elements of nonzero degree, then then the mapping space MapCAlgk(A, R) is empty for every
nonzero connective k-algebra R. However, there are many circumstances in which one can recover the object
A ∈CAlgk from the functor cSpec A. For example, if A is connective, then Yoneda’s lemma (Proposition
T.5.1.3.1) implies that A is determined by cSpec A, up to a contractible space of choices. In this section,
we will study a case which is in some sense at the opposite extreme: that in which A is a coconnective
E∞-algebra over a ﬁeld k of characteristic zero. The starting point for our investigation is the following
observation:
Theorem 4.4.1. Let k be a ﬁeld of characteristic zero. Then the functor cSpec : CAlgop
k →Fun(CAlgcn
k , S)
described above induces a fully faithful embedding
(CAlgcc
k )op →Fun(CAlgcn
k , S).
Proof. Let bS denote the ∞-category of spaces which are not necessarily small, and let [
CAlgk denote the
∞-category of E∞-algebras over k which are not necessarily small. Theorem T.5.1.5.6 implies that there
exists a limit-preserving functor Γ : Fun(CAlgcn
k , bS)op →[
CAlgk such that the composition of Γ with the
Yoneda embedding j′ : (CAlgcn
k ) ,→Fun(CAlgcn
k , bS)op is homotopic to the the inclusion CAlgcn
k ,→[
CAlgk;
moreover, Γ is determined uniquely up to homotopy equivalence. For every object X ∈Fun(CAlgcn
k , bS) and
every object A ∈Algcc
k , we have a canonical homotopy equivalence
MapFun(CAlgcn
k ,bS)(X, cSpec A) ≃Map [
CAlgk(A, Γ(X)).
To prove that cSpec is fully faithful, we must show that for every pair of coconnective k-algebras A and B,
the natural map
MapCAlgk(A, B) →MapFun(CAlgcn
k ,bS)(cSpec B, cSpec A) ≃Map [
CAlgk(A, Γ(cSpec B))
is a homotopy equivalence. To prove this, it suﬃces to show that the unit map ψB : B →Γ(cSpec B) is an
equivalence in [
CAlgk.
According to Corollary 4.3.14, we can write B as the limit of a cosimplicial k-algebra B•, where each Bn is
elementary. Proposition 4.3.11 implies that cSpec B can be identiﬁed with the colimit of the simplicial object
cSpec B• in Fun(CAlgcn
k , bS). Since Γ preserves all limits, we deduce that Γ(cSpec B) ≃Tot Γ(cSpec B•).
Consequently, to prove that ψB is an equivalence, it will suﬃce to show that ψBn is an equivalence for each
n ≥0. Replacing B by Bn, we may suppose that B is elementary.
Using Corollary 4.1.7, we can choose a map of k-algebras η : B →k. Let C• : N(∆+) →CAlgk be the
ˇCech nerve of η in the ∞-category CAlgop
k : that is, let C• be the cosimplicial k-algebra such that Cn is the
(n + 1)st tensor power of k over B. Let X• be the augmented simplicial object of Fun(CAlgcn
k , bS) given by
X•(R) = MapCAlgk(C•, R). There is an evident unit map u : C• →Γ ◦X•. For n ≥0, Proposition 4.3.15
implies that Cn is connective, so that Xn lies in the essential of j′ and so u induces an equivalence Cn →ΓXn
by construction. We wish to prove that u induces an equivalence B ≃C−1 →ΓX−1 ≃Γ(cSpec B). Since C•
is a limit diagram (Proposition 4.3.15), it will suﬃce to show that Γ◦X• is a limit diagram in [
CAlgk/. Since
Γ preserves limits by construction, we are reduced to proving that X• is a colimit diagram in Fun(CAlgcn
k , bS).
Note that X• can be identiﬁed with the ˇCech nerve of the map q : cSpec k →cSpec B determined by η. It is
therefore a colimit diagram if and only if q is an eﬀective epimorphism (see §T.6.2.3): that is, if and only if
the map
∗≃(cSpec k)(R)
q→(cSpec B)(R)
is surjective on connected components, for every connective k-algebra R. This is equivalent to the connec-
tivity of the mapping space MapCAlgk(B, R), which follows from Corollary 4.1.7.
