AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 126
Deﬁnition 4.4.2. Let k be a ﬁeld of characteristic zero. We will say that a functor X : CAlgcn
k →S is a
coaﬃne stack if has the form cSpec A, where A is a coconnective E∞-algebra over k.
Remark 4.4.3. If X : Fun(CAlgcn
k , S) is a coaﬃne stack, then X is automatically a hypercomplete sheaf
with respect to the ﬂat topology (Theorem VII.5.14).
The remainder of this section is devoted to studying the class of coaﬃne stacks over a ﬁeld k of character-
istic zero. We begin by showing that a coaﬃne stack X is determined by its restriction to discrete k-algebras
(Proposition 4.4.6).
Proposition 4.4.4. Let k be a ﬁeld of characteristic zero and let A be a coconnective E∞-algebra over k.
Then there exists an augmented cosimplicial object A• : N(∆+) →CAlgk with the following properties:
(1) We have equivalences A−1 ≃A and A0 ≃k.
(2) For n ≥0, the k-algebra An is discrete.
(3) The augmented cosimplicial object A• is a limit diagram.
(4) For every connective k-algebra R, the augmented simplicial space MapCAlgk(A•, R) is a colimit diagram.
(5) For every injective map [m] →[n] in ∆, the induced map of discrete k-algebras Am →Am is ﬂat.
Proof. Corollary 4.1.7 implies that there exists an augmentation η : A →k. Using Corollary 4.3.14, we can
choose a limit diagram B

CAlgk has the following properties:
(a) There is an equivalence of k-algebras B−1 ≃A.
(b) For n ≥0, the k-algebra Bn is elementary.
We can regard B

the constant augmented cosimplicial k-algebra taking the value k. Let B•,• : N(∆+ × ∆+) →CAlgk be a

−→CAlgk

Condition (1) is clear from the construction, and condition (2) follows from Proposition 4.3.15.
We

Since N(∆)op is sifted (Lemma T.5.5.8.4), it will suﬃce to show that the restriction

We now prove (5). Let α : [m] →[n] be an injection in ∆; we wish to prove that the induced map
γ : Am →An is a ﬂat map of discrete commutative k-algebras. Factoring α as a composition [m] ,→[m+1] ,→
· · · ,→[n], we can reduce to the case where m = n−1 and the image of α is {0, 1, . . . , i−1, i+1, . . . , n} ⊆[n].
Note that there exists a map β : [n] →[m] in ∆such that β ◦α is the identity. Let Hm = Bm,1 ≃k ⊗Bm k
and let Hn = Bn,1 ≃k ⊗Bn k. Then Hm and Hn are commutative Hopf algebras, and α induces a map
f : Hm →Hn.
Since f is an injective map of Hopf algebras (it has a left inverse given by β), it is
automatically ﬂat. The map γ factors as a composition
Am ≃(Hm)⊗m f ⊗m
−→(Hn)⊗m
θ→(Hn)⊗n ≃An.
We observe that θ is a tensor product of ﬁnitely many copies of the identity map idHn with either the unit
map k →Hn (if i ∈{0, n}) or the comultiplication ∆: Hn →Hn ⊗k Hn (if 0 < i < n); in either case, it is
clear that θ is ﬂat. It follows that the composition θ ◦f ⊗n is ﬂat, which completes the proof of (5).
Notation 4.4.5. Let k be a ﬁeld. We let CAlg0

Proposition 4.4.8. Let k be a ﬁeld of characteristic zero and let X : CAlg0

(1) For every discrete k-algebra R, the space X(R) is connected.
It follows from (1) that there exists a point η ∈X(k). For every k-algebra R, we will identify η with its
image in the space X(R).
(2) For every integer i > 0, the functor R 7→πi(X(R), η) is representable by a prounipotent group scheme
over k.
127
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 128
Remark 4.4.9. In the situation of Proposition 4.4.8, the functors R 7→πi(X(R), η) depend on the choice of
point η ∈X(k). However, since X(k) is connected, any other choice of point η′ ∈X(k) yields an isomorphic
functor R 7→πi(X(R), η′). The isomorphism πi(X(R), η) ≃πi(X(R), η′) is not canonically determined: it
depends on a choice of path from η to η′ in X(k) (which is generally not simply connected). Nevertheless,
the condition that R 7→πi(X(R), η) be representable by a prounipotent group scheme does depend on the
choice of point η.
Remark 4.4.10. If W and W ′ are vector spaces over the ﬁeld k, we let Homk(W, W ′) denote the set of
linear maps from W to W ′. For every vector space W, the scheme Specc Sym∗W is a prounipotent group
scheme over k, representing the functor R 7→Homk(W, R). Since k has characteristic zero, this construction
determines a fully faithful embedding of the category of k-vector spaces into the category of prounipotent
group schemes over k. The essential image of this functor is the category of commutative prounipotent group
schemes.
Proof of Proposition 4.4.8. Suppose ﬁrst that X is the restriction of a coaﬃne stack X, and write X =

Notation 4.4.11. Let k be a ﬁeld of characteristic zero and X : CAlgcn

η (however, the isomorphism class of πiX does not depend on η).
We now study the connectivity properties of coaﬃne stacks.
Proposition 4.4.12. Let A be a coconnective E∞-algebra over a ﬁeld k of characteristic zero, and let
X = cSpec A be the corresponding coaﬀﬁne stack. For each integer n > 0, the following conditions are
equivalent:
(1) For every connective k-algebra R, the space X(R) is n-connective.
(2) For every discrete k-algebra R, the space X(R) is n-connective.
(3) For 0 < i < n, the prounipotent group scheme πiX is trivial.
130
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 131
(4) If η : A →k is a map of k-algebras, then the homotopy groups πi(k ⊗A LA/k) vanish for i > −n.
(5) The homotopy groups πiLA/k vanish for i > −n.
(6) For 0 < i < n, the homotopy group π−iA vanishes.
Deﬁnition 4.4.13. We will say that a coaﬃne stack X is n-connective if it satisﬁes the equivalent conditions
of Proposition 4.4.12.
Remark 4.4.14. Let k be a ﬁeld of characteristic zero, X = cSpec A a coaﬃne stack over k, and choose a
base point η ∈X(k). For every integer n ≥1, let Ωn(X) denote the k-vector space of left invariant 1-forms
on the prounipotent group scheme πnX. For every k-vector space V , we can identify the set of k-linear maps
Homk(Ωn(X), V ) with the kernel of the group homomorphism (πnX)(k ⊕V ) →(πnX)(k). It follows that
Ωn(X) can be identiﬁed with the vector space π−n(k ⊗A LA/k).
Proof of Proposition 4.4.12. The implications (1) ⇒(2) ⇒(3) are obvious.
The implication (3) ⇒(4)
follows from Remark 4.4.14, the implication (4) ⇒(5) follows from Corollaries 4.1.16 and 4.1.12, and the
implication (5) ⇒(6) follows from Proposition 4.1.15. We will prove that (6) ⇒(1). Applying Proposition
4.1.4 to the map k →A, we deduce the existence of a sequence of algebra maps
k = A(0) →A(1) →A(2) →· · ·
with colimit A, where each A(i + 1) can be written as a tensor product A(i) ⊗Sym∗V (i) k with πjV (i) ≃0 for
j ≥−n. Let R be a connective k-algebra; we wish to prove that MapCAlgk(A, R) ≃lim
−→MapCAlgk(A(i), R)
is n-connective. It will therefore suﬃce to show that each of the restriction maps
MapCAlgk(A(i + 1), R) →MapCAlgk(A(i), R)
has n-connective homotopy ﬁbers. These can be identiﬁed with the homotopy ﬁbers of the restriction map
∗= MapCAlgk(k, R) →MapCAlgk(Sym∗V (i), R) ≃MapModk(V (i), R);
we are therefore reduced to proving that MapModk(V (i), R) is (n + 1)-connective. This follows from our
assumption that R is connective and πjV (i) ≃0 for j ≥−n.

4.5
Quasi-Coherent Sheaves on Coaﬃne Stacks
Let k be a ﬁeld of characteristic zero and let X : CAlgcn
k
→S be a coaﬃne stack, so that X = cSpec A
for some coconnective E∞-algebra A over k. In this section, we will study the ∞-category QCoh(X) of
quasi-coherent sheaves on X, and study its relationship to the ∞-category ModA of A-module spectra. Our
ﬁrst step is to deﬁne QCoh(X).
Notation 4.5.1. Let X be a coaﬃne stack over a ﬁeld k of characteristic zero. Then X classiﬁes a left
ﬁbration of ∞-categories q : C →CAlgcn
k . The composite map C
q→CAlgcn
k →CAlgcn is also a left ﬁbration,
classiﬁed by a functor X+ : CAlgcn →S. The functor X+ is a left Kan extension of X along the forgetful
functor CAlgcn
k →CAlgk. We let QCoh(X) denote the symmetric monoidal ∞-category QCoh(X+) deﬁned
in §2.7. More concretely, if we write X = cSpec A, where A is a coconnective E∞-algebra over k, then
QCoh(X) is given by the inverse limit lim
←−φ:A→R ModR, where the limit is taken over all maps of E∞-rings
φ : A →R for which the codomain R is connective. In particular, we have a symmetric monoidal pullback
functor ModA →QCoh(X).
We can now formulate the main result of this section.
Proposition 4.5.2. Let X be a coaﬃne stack over a ﬁeld k of characteristic zero, and ﬁx a point η ∈X(k).
For every object M ∈QCoh(X), we let M(η) denote the image of M in Modk. Then:
(1) The stable ∞-category QCoh(X) is presentable.
(2) Let QCoh(X)≥0 ⊆QCoh(X) be the full subcategory spanned by those objects M for which πiM(η) ≃0
for i < 0, and let QCoh(X)≤0 denote the full subcategory of QCoh(X) spanned by those objects M
for which πiM(η) ≃0 for i > 0.
Then (QCoh(X)≥0, QCoh(X)≤0) is an accessible t-structure on
QCoh(X).
(3) The t-structure of (2) is compatible with ﬁltered colimits: that is, the full subcategory QCoh(X)≤0 is
closed under ﬁltered colimits in QCoh(X).
(4) The t-structure of (2) is both right and left complete.
(5) The heart of QCoh(X) can be identiﬁed with the abelian category of algebraic representations of the
prounipotent group scheme π1X.
(6) Write X = cSpec A for some coconnective k-algebra A. The pullback functor F : ModA →QCoh(X)
is t-exact (with respect to the t-structure on ModA described in Proposition 4.5.4 below).
(7) The functor F induces an equivalence of ∞-categories (ModA)≤0 →QCoh(X)≤0.
Remark 4.5.3. Let X be a coaﬃne stack over a ﬁeld k of characteristic zero.
Since the ∞-categories
(Modk)≤0 and (Modk)≥0 both contain the unit object of Modk and are stable under tensor products, we
conclude that QCoh(X)≥0 and QCoh(X)≤0 contain the unit object of QCoh(X) and are stable under tensor
products.
The proof of Proposition 4.5.2 will be given at the end of this section. First, we need to describe the
t-structure on the ∞-category ModA appearing in assertion (6).

4.6
Completed Tensor Products of Stable ∞-Categories
Let PrL
St denote the ∞-category whose objects are presentable stable ∞-categories and whose morphisms
are colimit-preserving functors. We will regard PrL
St as a symmetric monoidal ∞-category (see §A.6.3.1): if
C and D are presentable stable ∞-categories, then the tensor product C ⊗D is universal among presentable
∞-categories E equipped with a functor C × D →E which preserves colimits separately in each variable.
Our goal in this section is to study the behavior of t-structures on stable ∞-categories with respect to
the tensor product operation described above. We can summarize our main results as follows:
(a) If C and D are presentable stable ∞-categories equipped with accessible t-structures, then the tensor
product C ⊗D inherits an accessible t-structure.
(b) Let C and D are presentable stable ∞-categories equipped with right complete accessible t-structures,
then the induced t-structure on C ⊗D is right complete (Proposition 4.6.11).
(c) If C and D are presentable stable ∞-categories which are equipped with t-structures that are both
right and left complete, then the tensor product C ⊗D need not be left complete. However, we can
instead form a completed tensor product C ˆ⊗D (given by the left completion of C ⊗D with respect to
the t-structure of (a)). The completed tensor product operation is commutative and associative up to
coherent homotopy (see Proposition 4.6.15).
As an application of these results, we will show that a coaﬃne stack X (over a ﬁeld k of characteristic
zero) can be functorially recovered from the symmetric monoidal ∞-category QCoh(X) of quasi-coherent
sheaves on X (Corollary 4.6.19).
Notation 4.6.1. If C and D are ∞-categories which admit small colimits, we let FunL(C, D) denote the full
subcategory of Fun(C, D) spanned by those functors which preserve small colimits. We let PrL denote the
∞-category whose objects are presentable ∞-categories and whose morphisms are functors which preserve
small colimits, and PrR the ∞-category whose objects are presentable ∞-categories and whose morphisms
are accessible functors which preserve small limits. There is a canonical equivalence of ∞-categories PrL ≃
(PrR)op.
We let PrL
St denote the full subcategory of PrL spanned by the presentable stable ∞-categories, and regard
PrL
St as a symmetric monoidal ∞-category, as explained in §A.6.3.1. The symmetric monoidal structure on
PrL
St is encoded by a coCartesian ﬁbration PrL
St
⊗→N(Fin∗). We let hPrL
St
⊗denote the homotopy category
of PrL

(1) We can identify objects of hPrL

stable ∞-category.
To such a sequence, the functor θ assigns the set of all sequences {C(i)≥0 ⊆
C(i)}1≤i≤n, where each C(i)≥0 determines an accessible t-structure on C(i).

Example 4.6.3. The unit object of PrL

If C is a stable ∞-category equipped with a t-structure, then we can deﬁne right and left completions of
C as in §A.1.2.1. Our next goal is to investigate the functoriality of these constructions. We begin with a
study of the right completion.
Lemma 4.6.4. Let C be a stable ∞-category equipped with a t-structure. Then C is right complete if and
only if the following conditions are satisﬁed:
(1) The intersection T
n C≤−n consists only of zero objects of C.
(2) Given any sequence of objects
X(0)
f(1)
−→X(1)
f(2)
−→X(2) →· · ·
in C with the property that coﬁb f(n) ∈C≤−n for each n, then there exists a colimit X of the sequence.
Moreover, for each n ≥0 the coﬁber of the map X(n) →X belongs to C≤−n−1.
Proof. Suppose ﬁrst that C is right complete. Condition (1) is obvious. Let {X(n)}n≥0 be a sequence as in
(2). We now prove that (2) ⇒(3). For each n ≥0, the sequence
τ≥−nX(0) →τ≥−nX(1) →· · ·
is eventually constant, and therefore admits a colimit τ≥−nX(n) in C≥−n. Moreover, each of these colimits is
preserved by the truncation functor τ≥−m : C≥−n →C≥−m. Since C ≃lim
←−C≥−n, it follows that the sequence
{X(n)} admits a colimit in X ∈C such that for n ≥0, τ≥−nX is a colimit of the sequence τ≥−nX(m)
and therefore equivalent to τ≥−nX(n).
To complete the proof that the coﬁber of the map X(n) →X
belongs to C≤−n−1, it suﬃces to show that the map π−n−1X(n) →π−n−1X is a monomorphism in the
abelian category C♥. Since τ≥−n−1X(n + 1) ≃τ≥−n−1X, this is equivalent to the assertion that the map
π−n−1X(n) →π−n−1X(n + 1) is a monomorphism, which follows from our assumption that coﬁb f(n + 1) ∈
C≤−n−1.
Now suppose that (1) and (2) are satisﬁed. Using (2), we see that the evident functor G : C →lim
←−C≥−n
admits a left adjoint F, which carries a compatible sequence of objects X(n) ∈C≥−n to the colimit lim
−→X(n) ∈
C. Moreover, the unit map id →G◦F is an equivalence, so that F is fully faithful. To complete the proof, it
suﬃces to show that the functor G is conservative. Let α : Y →Z be a morphism in C such that G(α) is an
equivalence, and let X = ker(α). Since τ≥−n(α) is an equivalence for n ≥0, we conclude that τ≥−nX ≃0
for n ≥0, so that X ∈T C≤−n. Thus X ≃0, so that α is an equivalence.

FunL(C, D≤n) spanned by those functors which carry C≥0 into D≤n ∩D≥0, and let FunL
0 (bC, D≤n) be de-

∞-groupoid of lim
←−n FunL
0 (C, D≤n).
Similarly, MapPrL

category lim
←−n FunL
0 (bC, D≤n). We are therefore reduced to showing that, for each n ≥0, the lower horizontal
map in the diagram
FunL(bC≤n, D≤n)
/
bφ

FunL(C≤n, D≤n)
φ

FunL
0 (bC, D≤n)
/ FunL
0 (C, D≤n)
is an equivalence of ∞-categories. The upper horizontal map is an equivalence of ∞-categories (since C≤n ≃
bC≤n by Proposition A.1.2.1.17). It therefore suﬃces to show that φ and bφ are equivalences of ∞-categories.
We will prove that φ is an equivalence of ∞-categories; the proof for bφ is similar. Since the truncation
functor τ≤n : C →C≤n is a localization, the functor φ induces an equivalence of FunL(C≤n, D≤n) with the
full subcategory of FunL(C, D≤n) spanned by those functors F with the following property:
(∗) For every map α : X →Y in C which induces an equivalence τ≤nX →τ≤nY , the image F(α) is an
equivalence in D≤n.
We wish to prove that a colimit-preserving functor F : C →D≤n satisﬁes (∗) if and only if F belongs to
FunL
0 (C, D≤n). We ﬁrst prove the “only if” direction. Assume that F satisﬁes (∗), and let X ∈C≥0; we wish
to prove that FX ∈D≥0. This is equivalent to the requirement that the (n + 1)-fold suspension of FX is
trivial in D≤n. Since F commutes with colimits, this is equivalent to the requirement that F(X[n + 1]) ≃0.
This follows from (∗), since τ≤nX[n + 1] ≃0.
We now prove the “if” direction. Assume that F ∈FunL
0 (C, D≤n), and let α : X →Y be a map which
induces an equivalence τ≤nX ≃τ≤nY . We wish to prove that F(α) is an equivalence. By the two-out-of-
three property, it will will suﬃce to show that the induced maps F(X) →F(τ≤nX) and F(Y ) →F(τ≤nY )
are equivalences. We treat the ﬁrst case, the second being similar. We have a coﬁber sequence
τ≥n+1X →X →τ≤nX.
Since F preserves colimits, we conclude that F(τ≤nX) can be identiﬁed with the coﬁber of the map
F(τ≥n+1X) →F(X) in D≤n. It will therefore suﬃce to show that F(τ≥n+1X) ≃0. Since τ≥n+1X ≃
(τ≥0X[−n −1])[n + 1], F(τ≥n+1X) is the (n + 1)-fold suspension of F(τ≥0X[−n −1]) in D≤n.
Since
F ∈FunL
0 (C, D≤n), we conclude that F(τ≥0X[−n −1]) ∈D≥0 and therefore its (n + 1)-fold suspension
vanishes in D≤n.

5
Tannaka Duality for Generalized Algebraic Gerbes
Let G be an aﬃne group scheme over a ﬁeld k, and let Rep(G)fd denote the category of ﬁnite dimensional
algebraic representations of G. Tannaka duality provides a means of reconstructing the group scheme G from
the category Rep(G)fd. Let R be a commutative algebra over k, and let Mod♥
R denote the abelian category
of (discrete) modules over R. Then Mod♥
R is a symmetric monoidal category, so we can consider the category
Fun⊗(Rep(G)fd, Mod♥
R) of symmetric monoidal functors from Rep(G)fd into Mod♥
R. This category contains
a distinguished object F0, given by the composition
Rep(G)fd
F ′
→Mod♥
k
⊗R
−→Mod♥
R
where F ′ is the forgetful functor (which assigns to each representation of G its underlying vector space).
Tannaka duality asserts that there is canonical isomorphism
G(R) ≃AutFun⊗(Rep(G)fd,Mod♥
R(F0),
which depend functorially on R (here G(R) denotes the group of R-valued points of G). In fact, something
slightly more general is true: the category of exact symmetric monoidal functors F : Rep(G)fd →Mod♥
R
can be identiﬁed with the groupoid of R-valued points of the classifying stack BG. In other words, giving
an exact symmetric monoidal functor F : Rep(G)fd →Mod♥
R is equivalent to giving a map of commutative
rings k →R, together with a G-torsor over Specc R.
The classifying stack BG of an aﬃne group scheme G is an example of an algebraic gerbe over k. More
generally, we deﬁne an algebraic gerbe over k to be a geometric stack X equipped with a map X →Specf k
having the following property: there exists a ﬁeld extension k →k′ such that the ﬁber product X ×Specf k
Specf k′ is equivalent to the classifying stack of an aﬃne group scheme over k′ (for a more extensive discussion
of this notion, see §5.1). To every algebraic gerbe over k, we can associate an abelian category QCoh(X)fd
of locally free sheaves of ﬁnite rank X. Using Tannaka duality, one can show that the construction X 7→
QCoh(X)fd determines a fully faithful embedding from the 2-category of algebraic gerbes to the 2-category of
symmetric monoidal abelian categories. A symmetric monoidal category C is said to be Tannakian if it lies in
the essential image of this embedding. In characteristic zero, Deligne has given an intrinsic characterization
of the class of Tannakian categories (see [7]):
Theorem 5.0.1 (Deligne). Let C be an abelian category with unit object 1.
Then C is equivalent to a
category QCoh(X)fd for some algebraic gerbe X over a ﬁeld of characteristic zero if and only if C satisﬁes
the following conditions:
(1) The category C is abelian.
(2) The tensor product functor ⊗: C × C →C is exact in each variable.
(3) The ring HomC(1, 1) is a ﬁeld of characteristic zero.
(4) Every object V ∈C admits a dual V ∨.
(5) For every object V ∈C, let dim(V ) ∈k denote the composition
1
c→V ⊗V ∨
e→1,
where c and e are compatible coevaluation and evaluation maps for the duality between V and V ∨.
Then dim(V ) is a nonnegative integer (here we identify the ring of integers Z with its image in the
ﬁeld k).
145
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 146
Our goal in this section is to prove an ∞-categorical analogue of Theorem 5.0.1. In §5.2 we will introduce
the notion of generalized algebraic gerbe over a ﬁeld k of characteristic zero. Roughly speaking, a generalized
gerbe over k is a functor X : CAlgcn →S which is an extension of an algebraic gerbe over k by a coaﬃne
stack (for a precise deﬁnition, see Deﬁnition 5.2.1). If X is a generalized algebraic gerbe, then QCoh(X) is a
symmetric monoidal stable ∞-category which is equipped with a natural t-structure. Our main results can
be stated as follows:
(a) If X is a generalized algebraic gerbe, then X can be recovered from the ∞-category QCoh(X). More
precisely, for any connective E∞-ring A, we have a fully faithful embedding
X(A) →Fun⊗(QCoh(X), ModA),
whose essential image is spanned by those symmetric monoidal functors F : QCoh(X) →ModA which
preserve small colimits and connective objects (Proposition 5.6.1).
(b) Let C be a symmetric monoidal stable ∞-category equipped with a t-structure. Then there exists a
generalized algebraic gerbe X and a symmetric monoidal t-exact equivalence C ≃QCoh(X) if and only
if C is locally dimensional (see Deﬁnition 5.6.4): that is, if and only C satisﬁes suitable analogues of
the conditions listed in Theorem 5.0.1 (Theorem 5.6.19). In this case, X is canonically determined up
to equivalence (by virtue of (a)).
We will prove both of these results in §5.6. Let us give a brief indication of the proof of (b). Suppose that
C is a locally dimensional ∞-category. Then the heart C♥is a compactly generated abelian category, whose
compact objects form an abelian category satisfying the hypotheses of Theorem 5.0.1. Deligne’s result then
guarantees the existence of an algebraic gerbe Y and a symmetric monoidal equivalence of abelian categories
f : QCoh(Y )♥≃C♥. The main step in the proof is to show that f extends to a symmetric monoidal
functor F : QCoh(Y ) →C. The construction of F will require a number of preliminary results concerning
the approximation of stable ∞-categories by abelian subcategories, which we discuss in §5.3, 5.4, and 5.5.
Assuming that F has been constructed and commutes with small colimits, the adjoint functor theorem
guarantees the existence of a right adjoint G to F. Then G carries the unit object of C to a commutative
algebra object A of QCoh(Y ). The commutative algebra A in some sense measures the failure of F to be
an equivalence of ∞-categories (for example, if F is an equivalence, then A is the unit object of QCoh(Y )).
The fact that the functor f is an equivalence of abelian categories translates to a vanishing condition on the
homotopy groups of A, which in particular guarantees that A is a coconnective E∞-algebra in QCoh(Y ).
Taking the spectrum of A relative to Y , we will obtain a functor X which is, in some sense, an extension of
Y by coaﬃne stacks. We will then complete the proof by showing that X is a generalized algebraic gerbe
and that QCoh(X) is equivalent to C.
5.1
Algebraic Gerbes
Let k be a ﬁeld and let G be an aﬃne group scheme over k. Then G determines a group object in the
∞-category Fun(CAlgcn
k , S). Applying the left Kan extension functor Fun(CAlgcn
k , S) →Fun(CAlgcn, S), we
obtain a simplicial object of Fun(CAlgcn, S), which we will denote by G•. More concretely, each Gn is the
functor corepresented by the commutative ring of functions on the nth power of the group scheme G (formed
in the category of k-schemes); in particular, G0 is the functor represented by the ﬁeld k itself. Let BG denote
the geometric realization |G•|, formed in the ∞-category d
Shvfpqc of sheaves with respect to the ﬂat topology.
Then BG is a geometric stack (in the sense of Deﬁnition 3.4.1), which we will refer to as the classifying stack
of the group scheme G. Our goal in this section is to study geometric stacks which are, in some sense, locally
equivalent to the classifying stack of an aﬃne group scheme.
Deﬁnition 5.1.1. Let k be a ﬁeld and suppose we are given a natural transformation α : X →Specf k
in the ∞-category d
Shvfpqc. We will say that α exhibits X as an algebraic gerbe over k if there exists a
nonzero commutative ring R (corepresenting a functor Specf R : CAlgcn →S) and a natural transformation
β : Specf R →X satisfying the following conditions:
146
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 147
(a) The map β is representable, aﬃne, and faithfully ﬂat. In particular, the ﬁber product Specf R ×X
Specf R is corepresentable by a commutative ring A which is ﬂat over R.
(b) The canonical map R ⊗k R →A (determined by the natural transformation of functors Specf R ×X
Specf R →Specf R ×Specf k Specf R) is faithfully ﬂat.
Our deﬁnition of an algebraic gerbe X requires that X be a functor deﬁned on the ∞-category CAlgcn
consisting of all connective E∞-rings. Let CAlg0 denote the full subcategory of CAlgcn spanned by the
discrete E∞-rings, so that CAlg0 is equivalent to the nerve of the ordinary category of commutative rings.
Our ﬁrst goal is to show that an algebraic gerbe X is determined by its restriction to the subcategory CAlg0.
Deﬁnition 5.1.2. Let X : CAlgcn →bS be a functor. We will say that X is discretely determined if the
following condition is satisﬁed:
(∗) Let X0 = X| CAlg0, and let X′ : CAlgcn →bS be a left Kan extension of X0. Then the canonical map
X′ →X exhibits X as a sheaﬁﬁcation of X′ with respect to the ﬂat topology.
Proposition 5.1.3. Let k be a ﬁeld which corepresents the functor Specf k : CAlgcn →S, and suppose we
are given a natural transformation α : X →Specf k in d
Shvfpqc. For every discrete k-algebra R, let Y (R)
denote the summand of X(R) given by the ﬁber of the map X(R) →Hom(k, R) (where Hom(k, R) denotes
the set of ring homomorphisms from k into R). The following conditions are equivalent:
(1) The map α exhibits X as an algebraic gerbe over k (Deﬁnition 5.1.1).
(2) There exists a nonzero discrete commutative ring R and a map β : Specf R →X. Any such map is
representable, aﬃne, faithfully ﬂat, and induces a faithfully ﬂat map of commutative rings R⊗kR →A,
where A corepresents the ﬁber product Specf R ×X Specf R.
(3) The functor X is discretely determined, the functor Y : CAlg0
k →bS is 1-truncated and 1-connective (as
a sheaf with respect to the ﬂat topology), and there exists a nonzero discrete k-algebra R and a point
η ∈Y (R) such that the sheaf of groups π1(Y, η) is representable by a ﬂat aﬃne group scheme over R.
(4) The functor X is discretely determined, the functor Y : CAlg0
k →bS is 1-truncated and 1-connective (as
a sheaf with respect to the ﬂat topology), and for every point η ∈Y (R), the sheaf of groups π1(Y, η) is
representable by a ﬂat aﬃne group scheme over R.
Proof. The implication (2) ⇒(1) is obvious. Suppose next that (1) holds, and choose a map β : Specf R →X
satisfying the requirements of Deﬁnition 5.1.1. Let X• be the ˇCech nerve of f. Since f is aﬃne and faithfully
ﬂat, each Xn is representable by an R-algebra which is ﬂat over R (and therefore discrete). In particular,
each Xn is discretely determined. Since f is an eﬀective epimorphism with respect to the ﬂat topology,
X can be identiﬁed with the sheaﬁﬁcation of |X•| and is therefore also discretely determined. Note that
the functor Y is 1-truncated (since f determines an eﬀective epimorphism Hom(R, •) →Y with discrete
ﬁbers). Choose an equivalence Specf R ×X Specf R ≃Specf A. We note that π0Y can be identiﬁed with the
coequalizer (in the category of sheaves of sets on (CAlg0
k)op with respect to the ﬂat topology) of the diagram
Hom(A, •)
// Hom(R, •).
Since A is faithfully ﬂat over R ⊗k R, this coequalizer can be identiﬁed with the restriction of the functor
Specf k: in other words, Y is 1-connective (as a sheaf in the ﬂat topology). Note that f determines a point
η ∈Y (R), and that π1(Y, η) is corepresented by the tensor product A ⊗R⊗kR R (and therefore representable
by a ﬂat aﬃne R-scheme). This proves (3).
We now prove that (3) implies (4). Assume that X satisﬁes (3), so that for some point η ∈Y (R) the
group π1(Y, η) is corepresented by a ﬂat R-algebra A. Choose any other point η′ ∈Y (R′); we wish to show
that the sheaf of groups π1(Y, η′) is corepresented by a ﬂat R′-algebra. This assertion is local with respect
147
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 148
to the ﬂat topology on R′. Since Y is connected (as a sheaf of spaces with respect to the ﬂat topology),
we may assume that η′ is the image of η under some map of k-algebras R →R′, in which case π1(Y, η′) is
corepresented by the R′-algebra A ⊗R R′.
We now prove that (4) implies (2). Since Y is 1-connective, it is nontrivial: that is, there exists a nonzero
discrete k-algebra R and a point η ∈Y (R). Let Y0 : CAlg0
k →S be the functor corepresented by R, so that
η determines a map f : Y0 →Y . Since Y0 is 1-connective, the map f is an eﬀective epimorphism (of sheaves
with respect to the ﬂat topology). Let Y• be the ˇCech nerve of f, so that Y is equivalent to sheaﬁﬁcation
of the geometric realization |Y•|. The point η determines a pair of points η0, η1 ∈Y (R ⊗k R). Since Y is
1-connective, the collection of paths from η0 to η1 determines a bitorsor for the ﬂat group schemes π1(Y, η0)
and π1(Y, η1). It follows that Y1 is corepresented by a faithfully ﬂat R ⊗k R-algebra A, so that each Yn
is corepresented by A ⊗R · · · ⊗R A. Let F : Fun(CAlg0
k, bS) →Fun(CAlgcn, bS) be the functor of left Kan
extension, so that F(Yn) is the functor corepresented by A ⊗R · · · ⊗R A. for each n ≥0. It follows that
F(Y•) is a groupoid object of d
Shvfpqc, whose geometric realization is the sheaﬁﬁcation of F(Y ). Since X
is discretely determined, we conclude that X is equivalent to the geometric realization |F(Y•)| (formed in
the ∞-category d
Shvfpqc). In particular, the point η induces an eﬀective epimorphism β : Specf R →X such
that the ﬁber product Specf R ×X Specf R is equivalent to Specf A. To complete the proof, it suﬃces to
show that β is representable, aﬃne, and faithfully ﬂat. Choose any other map γ : Specf R′ →X; we wish to
prove that the ﬁber product Specf R′ ×X Specf R has the form Specf A′, for some faithfully ﬂat R′-algebra
A′. This assertion is local with respect to the ﬂat topology on R′. Since β is an eﬀective epimorphism, we
may assume that γ factors through β and thereby reduce to the case γ = β. In this case, A′ ≃A is ﬂat over
R (since A is ﬂat over R ⊗k R, which is in turn ﬂat over R).
Remark 5.1.4. Let k be a ﬁeld. Then every algebraic gerbe X over k is a geometric stack (Deﬁnition
3.4.1). It follows that the space X(A) is essentially small for every connective E∞-ring A (Corollary 3.4.28).
Consequently, we can identify X with a functor CAlgcn →S.
Remark 5.1.5. Let k be a ﬁeld and let X be an algebraic gerbe over k. Choose a nonzero commutative
ring R and a natural transformation Specf R →X (such a morphism is automatically representable, aﬃne,
and faithfully ﬂat; see Proposition 5.1.3). Then the ˇCech nerve of f is given by Spec R• for some cosim-
plicial k-algebra R•. Let OX denote the unit object of QCoh(X). Unwinding the deﬁnitions, we see that
MapQCoh(X)(OX, OX) can be identiﬁed with the limit lim
←−R• of the cosimplicial ring R• (in the ordinary
category of commutative rings): that is, with the equalizer of the pair of maps
R
d0 /
d1 / R1.
Since R1 is faithfully ﬂat over R ⊗k R, this equalizer can be canonically identiﬁed with k. In other words,
the canonical map
k ≃MapModk(k, k)
f ∗
→MapQCoh(X)(OX, OX)
is a homotopy equivalence.
Remark 5.1.6. We will say that a functor X : CAlgcn →S is an algebraic gerbe if there exists a ﬁeld k and a
natural transformation α : X →Specf k which exhibits X as an algebraic gerbe over k. Remark 5.1.5 shows
that k can be recovered from X (up to canonical isomorphism) by the formula k ≃π0 MapQCoh(X)(OX, OX).
In particular, the characteristic of k depends only on X. We will say that X has characteristic zero if the
ﬁeld k has characteristic zero.
Remark 5.1.7. Let k be a ﬁeld and let α : X →Specf k be an algebraic gerbe over k. We will say that X
is neutral if the map α admits a section s (up to homotopy). In this case, s determines a map Specf k →X
which is representable, aﬃne, and faithfully ﬂat.
Let R• be as in Remark 5.1.5.
Taking the spectrum
termwise, we obtain a group object in the category of aﬃne schemes over k, which we will denote by G.
Unwinding the deﬁnitions, we can recover X as the classifying stack BG of the aﬃne group scheme G.
148
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 149
Remark 5.1.8. Let X be an algebraic gerbe. Then there exists a nonzero commutative ring R such that
X(R) is nonempty. Let κ be the residue ﬁeld of R at some prime ideal p ⊆R. The map of commutative
rings R →κ induces a map of spaces X(R) →X(κ), so that X(κ) is nonempty. Choosing a point of X(κ),
we obtain a map β : Specf κ →X. It follows from Proposition 5.1.3 that β is representable, aﬃne, and
faithfully ﬂat.
Remark 5.1.9. Let k be a ﬁeld and let X be an algebraic gerbe over k. If k′ is an extension ﬁeld of k, then
the ﬁber product X ×Specf k Specf k′ is an algebraic gerbe over k′.
Remark 5.1.10. If X is an algebraic gerbe over a ﬁeld k, then X need not be neutral. However, it can
always be neutralized after passing to some extension ﬁeld of k. Namely, choose a map Specf κ →X as in
Remark 5.1.8; then the ﬁber product X ×Specf k Specf κ is a neutral algebraic gerbe over κ.
Remark 5.1.11. If k is a ﬁeld, then a k-module spectrum M is ﬂat over k if and only if it is discrete. It
follows that if X is an algebraic gerbe, then a quasi-coherent sheaf F on X is ﬂat if and only if it belongs to
the heart of the t-structure on QCoh(X) described in Proposition 3.4.17. If these conditions are satisﬁed,
then F is faithfully ﬂat if and only if it is nonzero.
Let X : CAlgcn →S be a geometric stack. Using Proposition 3.4.11, we see that for every connective
E∞-ring R corepresenting a functor Specf R, every natural transformation f : Specf R →X is representable
by aﬃne spectral Deligne-Mumford stacks. Moreover, f is ﬂat (faithfully ﬂat) if and only if the functor f∗
carries the unit object R ∈ModR ≃QCoh(Specf R) to a ﬂat (faithfully ﬂat) object of QCoh(X). Combining
this observation with Remark 5.1.11, we obtain the following:
Lemma 5.1.12. Let X be an algebraic gerbe and let R be a connective E∞-ring. Then a map β : Specf R →
X is ﬂat if and only if R is discrete. It is faithfully ﬂat if and only if R is discrete and nonzero.
Lemma 5.1.13. Let X be an algebraic gerbe and F ∈QCoh(X)♥be a discrete quasi-coherent sheaf on X.
Then F can be written as a ﬁltered colimit lim
−→Fα, where each Fα is a subobject of F (in the abelian category
QCoh(X)♥) and is locally free of ﬁnite rank.
Proof. Choose a faithfully ﬂat morphism f : Spec κ →X, where κ is a ﬁeld. Then f ∗F can be identiﬁed
with a vector space V over κ, which is a direct limit of ﬁnite dimensional subspaces Vα. Since f∗commutes
with ﬁltered colimits, we have f∗f ∗F ≃lim
−→f∗Vα. For each index α, let Fα denote the inverse image of
f∗Vα under the unit map F →f∗f ∗F (formed in the abelian category QCoh(X)♥). Since QCoh(X)♥is a
Grothendieck abelian category (see Proposition 3.4.17), we conclude that F ≃lim
−→Fα. Moreover, for each
index α, the inclusion f ∗Fα →f ∗F ⊆V factors through Vα, so that f ∗Fα is a ﬁnite-dimensional vector
space over κ and therefore Fα is locally free of ﬁnite rank.
Proposition 5.1.14. Let X, Y : CAlgcn →bS be functors, where Y is an algebraic gerbe. Then the evident
functor
θ : MapFun(CAlgcn,bS)(X, Y ) →Fun⊗(QCoh(Y ), QCoh(X))
is a fully faithful embedding. Moreover, a symmetric monoidal functor f ∗: QCoh(Y ) →QCoh(X) belongs
to the essential image of θ if and only if f ∗preserves small colimits and connective objects.
Proof. By Theorem 3.4.2, it will suﬃce to show that if f ∗; QCoh(Y ) →QCoh(X) is a symmetric monoidal
functor which preserves small colimits and connective objects, then f ∗preserves ﬂat objects. Let F be a ﬂat
quasi-coherent sheaf on Y . According to Lemma 5.1.13, we can write F ≃lim
−→Fα where each Fα ∈QCoh(Y )
is locally free of ﬁnite rank. Corollary 2.7.33 guarantees that Fα is a dualizable object of QCoh(Y )≥0. Since
f ∗is a symmetric monoidal functor which preserves connective objects, we conclude that f ∗Fα is a dualizable
object of QCoh(X)≥0. Corollary 2.7.33 implies that f ∗Fα is locally free of ﬁnite rank. Since f ∗commutes
with colimits, we can use Theorem A.7.2.2.15 to conclude that f ∗F ≃lim
−→f ∗Fα is a ﬂat quasi-coherent sheaf
on X.

AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 150
5.2
Generalized Algebraic Gerbes
Let k be a ﬁeld. In §5.1, we introduced the notion of an algebraic gerbe over k. Every algebraic gerbe X is
a geometric stack: in particular, it has the property that the homotopy groups πi(X(R), η) vanish for i ≥2
(and any choice of base point η) for every commutative ring R (regarded as a discrete E∞-ring). In this
section, we will introduce a generalization of the notion of algebraic gerbe, where we loosen the restrictions
on the higher homotopy groups of X. Roughly speaking, we will obtain this generalization by mixing the
the theory of algebraic gerbes with the theory of coaﬃne stacks developed in §4.4.

Specf k in d
Shvfpqc. We will say that α exhibits X as a generalized algebraic gerbe over k if the following
conditions are satisﬁed:
(1) The map α factors as a composition

(2) There exists a ﬁeld κ and a pullback diagram

Remark 5.2.2. We will say that a functor X : CAlgcn →bS is a generalized algebraic gerbe if there exists a
ﬁeld k of characteristic zero and a map α : X →Specf k which satisﬁes the conditions of Deﬁnition 5.2.1. In
this case, the ﬁeld k and the map α are well-deﬁned up to canonical equivalence.

Lemma 5.2.9. Let CAlg0 denote the ∞-category of discrete E∞-rings (so that CAlg0 is equivalent oto the
nerve of the ordinary category of commutative rings) and let X : CAlg0 →bS be a functor. For each n ≥1,

R →Ab given by the formula A 7→
πn(X(A) ×(τ≤1X)(A) {ηA}), where ηA denotes the image of η in (τ≤1X)(A). The following conditions are
equivalent:
(1)
(a) The functor X is a hypercomplete sheaf with respect to the ﬂat topology.
(b) The sheaﬁﬁcation of τ≤1X (with respect to the ﬂat topology) is the restriction of an algebraic gerbe
CAlgcn →S.
(c) There exists a ﬁeld κ of characteristic zero and a map η : Specf κ →τ≤1X such that, for each
m ≥2, the sheaﬁﬁcation of the functor πm(X, η) : CAlg0
κ →Ab is given by A 7→Homκ(Vm, A),
for some κ-vector space Vm.
(2) (a′) The functor X is a sheaf with respect to the ﬂat topology.
(b′) The functor τ≤1X extends to an algebraic gerbe CAlgcn →bS over a ﬁeld of characteristic zero.
(c′) For every discrete commutative ring R, every point η : Spec R →τ≤1X, and every m ≥2, the
functor πm(X, η) is given by A 7→HomR(Vm, A) for some projective R-module Vm.
Proof. We ﬁrst consider the case where X is n-truncated for n ≥1. The implication (2) ⇒(1) is obvious.
Conversely, suppose that (1) is satisﬁed, and let Y denote the sheaﬁﬁcation of τ≤1X. For every map η :
Specf R →Y which factors through Specf κ, assumption (c) guarantees that the ﬁber product X ×Y Specf R
determines a functor satisfying the hypotheses of Lemma 5.2.6. Since these hypotheses are local with respect
to the ﬂat topology (Remark 5.2.8), the same conclusion holds for every η. It follows that the homotopy ﬁbers
of the map maps X(R) →Y (R) are 2-connective, so that the map τ≤1X →τ≤1Y ≃Y is an equivalence.
The implication (b) ⇒(b′) is now clear, and (c′) follows from Lemma 5.2.6.
We now remove the assumption that X is n-truncated. Let X : CAlg0 →bS be a ﬂat sheaf. For n ≥1,
let Yn denote the sheaﬁﬁcation of τ≤nX with respect to the ﬂat topology. We ﬁrst show that (1) ⇒(2).
If X satisﬁes (1), then each Yn satisﬁes (1) and therefore (by the ﬁrst part of the proof) we conclude that
each Yn satisﬁes (2). Let Y = lim
←−Yn. Since each Yn satisﬁes (2), the canonical maps τ≤mYn →Ym are
equivalences for m ≤n. It follows that the canonical map Y →Yn induces an equivalence τ≤nY ≃Yn for
each n ≥1, so that the map X →Y is ∞-connective. The sheaf X is hypercomplete by (a), and the sheaf Y
is hypercomplete since it is a limit of truncated sheaves. It follows that the map X →Y is an equivalence.
It therefore suﬃces to prove that Y satisﬁes (2), which is clear.
Now suppose that X satisﬁes (2). It is clear that X satisﬁes (b) and (c). Using Lemma 5.2.6, we see
that each τ≤mX is a sheaf with respect to the ﬂat topology, so that X ≃lim
←−τ≤mX is a limit of truncated
sheaves and therefore hypercomplete.

5.3
Symmetric Monoidal Structures on Chain Complexes
Let A be an abelian category with enough injective objects, and let D+(A) be its derived ∞-category (see
§A.1.3.1). In this section, we will show that if A is equipped with a suﬃciently nice symmetric monoidal
structure, then D+(A) inherits a symmetric monoidal structure (see Proposition 5.3.15). We will restrict
our attention to the case where the tensor product functor ⊗: A × A →A is exact in each variable.
We begin with some general remarks.
Notation 5.3.1. Let A be an abelian category. We let Fun(∆, A) denote the category of cosimplicial objects
of A. The category Fun(∆, A) is naturally cotensored over ﬁnite simplicial sets: if K is a ﬁnite simplicial
set and A• is an object of Fun(∆, A), then we set
(AK)n =
Y
σ∈Hom(∆n,K)
An.
In particular, we can regard Fun(∆, A) as a simplicial category, whose enrichment is characterized by the
formula
HomSet∆(K, MapFun(∆,A)(A, B)) = HomFun(∆,A)(A, BK).
155
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 156
Remark 5.3.2. Let A be an abelian category and let A• be a cosimplicial object of A. We can associate
to A• its unnormalized chain complex: this is the chain complex
A0
d→A1
d→A2
d→· · ·
where each diﬀerential is given by an alternating sum of the coface maps of A•. For each n ≥0, we let
Hn(A•) ∈A denote the nth cohomology of this unnormalized cochain complex. We say that a map f : A• →
B• of cosimplicial objects is a quasi-isomorphism if, for each n ≥0, the induced map Hn(A•) →Hn(B•) is
an isomorphism in A.
Remark 5.3.3. Let A be an abelian category, and let Ch(A) be the category of chain complexes
· · · →A1 →A0 →A−1 →· · ·
with values in A, and let Ch≤0(A) be the full subcategory spanned by those chain complexes for which
An ≃0 for n > 0. The Dold-Kan correspondence gives an equivalence of categories N ∗: Fun(∆, A) →
Ch≤0(A),which assigns to each cosimplicial object A• its normalized cochain complex (see §A.1.2.3). For
each A•, there is a natural quasi-isomorphism of cochain complexes from A• to N ∗(A•). In particular, a
map f : A• →B• is a quasi-isomorphism if and only if the induced map N ∗(A•) →N ∗(B•) is a quasi-
isomorphism.
Let A be an abelian category with enough injective objects, and let Ainj denote the full subcategory
of A spanned by the injective objects. We can regard Ch(A) as a simplicial category via the prescription
described in §A.1.3.1: that is, for every pair of chain complexes A• and B• and every ﬁnite simplicial set K,
we have a canonical bijection
HomSet∆(K, MapCh(A)(A•, B•)) ≃MapCh(A)(A• ⊗C•(K), B•)),
where C•(K) denotes the normalized chain complex (of free abelian groups) associated to the simplicial
set K (so that the generators of Cn(K) can be identiﬁed with nondegenerate n-simplices of K).
The
induced simplicial structure on Ch≤0(A) does not agree with N ∗-pullback of the simplicial structure on
Fun(∆, A) described in Construction 5.3.14. Nevertheless, the inverse of N ∗determines a simplicial functor
Ch≤0(A) →Fun(∆, A) which induces an equivalence of ∞-categories
D+(A)≤0 →N(Fun(∆, Ainj)),
where D+(A) is the derived ∞-category of A (see Proposition A.1.3.1.16 and Variation A.1.3.1.19).
Remark 5.3.4. Let A be an abelian category with enough injective objects, and let Funacyc(∆, Ainj) denote
the full subcategory of Fun(∆, Ainj) spanned by those cosimplicial objects A• such that Hn(A•) ≃0 for n > 0.
It follows from the above analysis (or from Proposition 5.3.12) that the functor A• 7→H0(A•) determines
an equivalence of simplicial categories Funacyc(∆, Ainj) →A (here we regard A as a simplicial category with
discrete mapping spaces).
Combining the above observations with Lemma A.1.3.2.10 and Proposition A.1.3.2.6, we deduce the
following:
Proposition 5.3.5. Let C be a presentable stable ∞-category equipped with a right complete t-structure such
that C≤0 is closed under ﬁltered colimits, and choose an equivalence f : N(A) →C♥where A is an abelian
category. Then A has enough injectives (Corollary A.1.3.4.7), and there exists an essentially unique functor
F : N(Fun(∆, Ainj)) →C≤0 with the following properties:
(1) The functor F is left exact.
(2) The restriction of F to N(Funacyc(∆, Ainj)) is equivalent to the composition N(Funacyc(∆, Ainj)) ≃
N(A) ≃C♥,→C.

Example 5.3.6. Let X : CAlgcn →S be a geometric stack, and let A = hQCoh(X )♥be the abelian category
of discrete quasi-coherent sheaves on X. Let F : N(Fun(∆, Ainj)) →QCoh(X)≤0 be the left-exact functor
of Proposition 5.3.5. Choose a connective E∞-ring A and a faithfully ﬂat morphism Specf A →X, where
Specf A denotes the functor corepresented by A. If A is discrete, then F is an equivalence of ∞-categories.
To see this, choose discrete sheaves F, G ∈QCoh(X)♥, where G is an injective object of A. We can identify
η∗G with a discrete A-module M. Choose a map M →I, where I is an injective A-module. Since the
pullback functor η∗: QCoh(X)♥→Mod♥
A is exact, we conclude that η∗I is an injective object of A. We
claim that the composite map
G →η∗η∗G →η∗I
is a monomorphism in A.
In other words, we claim that the map η∗G →η∗η∗I is a monomorphism
of discrete A-modules. This is clear, since the composite map η∗G →η∗η∗I →I is a monomorphism by
construction. Because G is injective, it follows that the monomorphism G →η∗I splits; consequently, to prove
that the abelian groups Exti
QCoh(X)(F, G) vanish for i > 0, it suﬃces to show that Exti
QCoh(X)(F, η∗I) ≃
Exti
ModA(η∗F, I) ≃0. Since A is discrete and I is injective, this is an immediate consequence of Proposition
A.7.1.1.15.
We now review some standard results concerning the interplay between the simplicial structure on the
category of cosimplicial objects of A and the notion of quasi-isomorphism between the corresponding chain
complexes.

Lemma 5.3.8. Let A be an abelian category, A• a cosimplicial object of A, and let f : K →K′ be a
weak homotopy equivalence of ﬁnite simplicial sets. Then the induced map (AK′)• →(AK)• is a quasi-

Proof. Fix n ≥0; we will show that the map Hm((AK′)•) →Hn((AK)•) is an isomorphism for m < n. Let

In this case, the map of unnormalized chain complexes
θ : ((AK′)•, d) →((AK)•, d) is obtained by tensoring B with the dual of the map of chain complexes of free
abelian groups given by
θ0 : C∗(K × ∆k, K × ∂∆k; Z) →C∗(K′ × ∆k, K′ × ∂∆k; Z).
If f is a weak homotopy equivalence, then θ0 is a quasi-isomorphism and therefore (since it is map of chain
complexes of free abelian groups) admits a homotopy inverse. It follows that θ admits a homotopy inverse
and is therefore a quasi-isomorphism.

Proposition 5.3.11. Let A be an abelian category, and suppose we are given cosimplicial objects A•, B• of
A such that each Bn is injective. Then MapFun(∆,A)(A•, B•) is a Kan complex.
Proof. It suﬃces to show that if f : K →K′ is a trivial coﬁbration ﬁnite simplicial sets, then every map
A• →(BK)• factors through (BK′)•. Since f is a weak homotopy equivalence, the map (BK′)• →(BK)• is
a quasi-isomorphism and the desired result follows from Lemma 5.3.10.
Proposition 5.3.12. Let A be an abelian category, and suppose we are given a quasi-isomorphism f :
A• →B• between cosimplicial objects of A. Let C• be another cosimplicial object of A such that each Cn is
injective. Then composition with f induces a homotopy equivalence of Kan complexes
θ : MapFun(∆,A)(B•, C•) →MapFun(∆,A)(A•, C•).
Proof. Let C(f)• denote a mapping cylinder of f, so that f factors as a composition
A• f ′
→C(f)• f ′′
→B•.

where f ′ is a monomorphism, f ′ and f ′′ are quasi-isomorphisms, and and f ′′ admits a section s. It will
therefore suﬃce to show that composition with s and f ′ induce homotopy equivalences
MapFun(∆,A)(A•, C•) ←MapFun(∆,A)(C(f)•, C•) →MapFun(∆,A)(B•, C•).
Replacing f by f ′ or s, we can reduce to the case where the original map f is a monomorphism. In this case,
we claim that θ is a trivial Kan ﬁbration. In other words, for every monomorphism g : K →K′ of ﬁnite
simplicial sets, the map (CK′)• →(CK)• has the right lifting property with respect to f. This is a special
case of Lemma 5.3.10.
We now study symmetric monoidal structures on ∞-categories of chain complexes. Our starting point is
the following observation.

5.4
Approximation by Connective Objects
Let C be a presentable stable ∞-category equipped with a t-structure.
If the t-structure on C is right
complete, then we can recover C as the homotopy inverse limit of the tower
· · · →C≥−2 →C≥−1 →C≥0
This homotopy inverse limit can be described as the stabilization of C≥0, or equivalently as the tensor product
Sp ⊗C≥0 (formed in the symmetric monoidal ∞-category PrL of presentable ∞-categories).
Our goal in this section is to obtain an analogous result for ∞-categories which are not assumed to be
presentable. Suppose that C is a stable ∞-category equipped with a right-bounded t-structure. Our main
result (Proposition 5.4.3) asserts that we can recover C as Spﬁn ⊗C≥0, where Spﬁn denotes the ∞-category of
ﬁnite spectra and the the tensor product is formed in the setting of ∞-categories which admit ﬁnite colimits
(see Notation 5.4.2).
Lemma 5.4.1. Let C be a small stable ∞-category equipped with a t-structure (C≥0, C≤0). Then Ind(C)
inherits a t-structure (Ind(C)≥0, Ind(C)≤0), where Ind(C)≥0 is the essential image of the fully faithful functor
Ind(C≥0) →Ind(C), and Ind(C)≤0 is deﬁned similarly. If the t-structure on C is right bounded, then the
t-structure on Ind(C) is right complete.
Proof. The ﬁrst assertion is straightforward. To prove the second, let us assume that the t-structure on C
is right bounded. It is clear from the construction that Ind(C)≤0 is closed under ﬁltered colimits in Ind(C).
To prove that Ind(C) is right complete, it will suﬃce to show that the intersection T
n Ind(C)≤−n consists
only of zero objects of Ind(C) (Proposition A.1.2.1.19). To this end, let us suppose that X ∈T
n Ind(C)≤−n.
Then MapInd(C)(Y, X) is contractible for any Y ∈S
n Ind(C)≥−n, and therefore for any Y belonging to the
essential image of the Yoneda embedding j : C →Ind(C). Since Ind(C) is generated under ﬁltered colimits
by the essential image of j, we conclude that MapInd(C)(Y, X) is contractible for all Y and therefore X is a
ﬁnal object of Ind(C).
Notation 5.4.2. Let K denote the collection of all ﬁnite simplicial sets and let Cat∞(K) denote the subcat-
egory of Cat∞spanned by those ∞-categories which admit ﬁnite colimits and those functors which preserve
ﬁnite colimits. We will regard Cat∞(K) as a symmetric monoidal ∞-category: if C and D are objects of
Cat∞(K), then the tensor product C ⊗D is universal among ∞-categories E which are equipped with a func-
tor C × D →E which preserves ﬁnite colimits separately in each variable. We will regard the construction
Ind : Cat∞(K) →PrL as a symmetric monoidal functor from Cat∞(K) to the ∞-category PrL of presentable
∞-categories.
Proposition 5.4.3. Let C be a stable ∞-category equipped with a right-bounded t-structure. Then there is
a canonical equivalence Spﬁn ⊗C≥0 ≃C (where the tensor product is formed in the ∞-category Cat∞(K) of
Notation 5.4.2).

AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 163
Proof. Without loss of generality, we may assume that C is small. We note that Ind(C) is a stable ∞-
category, which inherits a right complete t-structure (Lemma 5.4.1). It follows that Ind(C) can be identiﬁed
with the stabilization of the ∞-category Ind(C)≥0, which (according to Example A.6.3.1.22) is given by
Sp ⊗Ind(C)≥0 ≃Ind(Spﬁn ⊗C≥0). It follows that there are fully faithful embeddings
C
j→Ind(C)
j′
←Spﬁn ⊗C≥0 .
We will complete the proof by showing that the essential images of j and j′ are the same.
Let ⊠:
Spﬁn × C≥0 →Ind(C) be the functor classiﬁed by j′. It is clear that the essential image of j is contained in
the essential image of j′: if C ∈C, then C[n] ∈C≥0 for n ≫0, so that j(C) ≃j′(S−n ⊠C[n]). We now
prove the reverse inclusion. Since Spﬁn ⊗C≥0 is generated under ﬁnite colimits by the essential image of ⊠
(and the functors j and j′ preserve ﬁnite colimits), it suﬃces to show that j′(K ⊠C) belongs to the essential
image of j, for every pair (K, C) ∈Spﬁn × C≥0. Fixing C, we see that the collection of ﬁnite spectra K for
which the conclusion holds is closed under translations and ﬁnite colimits; it therefore suﬃces to treat the
case where K is the sphere spectrum S. In this case, j′(K ⊠C) ≃j(C).
Corollary 5.4.4. Let C be a stable ∞-category. Then the inclusion {S} ,→Spﬁn induces an equivalence
C →Spﬁn ⊗C.
Proof. We can regard C as equipped with a right-bounded t-structure by setting C≥0 = C; the desired result
then follows from Proposition 5.4.3.
Remark 5.4.5. Let C be an ∞-category which admits ﬁnite colimits. Then Spﬁn ⊗C is stable. To see this,
we note that Ind(Spﬁn ⊗C) ≃Sp ⊗Ind(C) is equivalent to Stab(Ind(C)) (Example A.6.3.1.22), and therefore
stable.
It therefore suﬃces to show that the essential image of the Yoneda embedding j : Spﬁn ⊗C →
Ind(Spﬁn ⊗C) is closed under translation and ﬁnite colimits. Closure under ﬁnite colimits is obvious. Closure
under translation follows from the commutativity of the diagram

∞be the full subcategory of Cat∞(K) spanned by the stable ∞-categories. Then
the inclusion CatEx

Proof. Let L : Cat∞(K) →Cat∞(K) be the functor given by C 7→Spﬁn ⊗C. There is an evident natural

Remark 5.4.7. It is clear that the localization of Proposition 5.4.6 is compatible with the symmetric
monoidal structure on Cat∞(K): that is, if f : C →D is a functor which preserves ﬁnite colimits and induces
an equivalence Spﬁn ⊗C →Spﬁn ⊗D, then the induced map C ⊗E →D ⊗E has the same property, for every
object E ∈Cat∞(K). It follows from Proposition A.2.2.1.9 that CatEx

monoidal ∞-category, and that the construction C 7→Spﬁn ⊗C determines a symmetric monoidal functor
from Cat∞(K) to CatEx
∞.

is a homotopy equivalence. This follows from Corollary 5.4.8.
5.5
Approximation by Abelian Categories
Let C be a stable ∞-category equipped with a right complete t-structure, and let A denote the heart of C.
Assume that the abelian category A has enough injective objects, so that the derived ∞-category D+(A) is
deﬁned (see §A.1.3.1). Using Theorem A.1.3.2.2, we see that the inclusion N(A) ,→C extends to an exact
functor φ : D−(A) →C between stable ∞-categories. Our goal in this section is to prove a symmetric
monoidal analogue of this result. Let us suppose that the stable ∞-category C is equipped with a symmetric
monoidal structure such that the tensor product functor ⊗: C × C →C is t-exact in each variable. Then the
abelian category A inherits a symmetric monoidal structure (and the tensor product functor ⊗: A × A →A
is exact in each variable). Combining the results of §5.3 with the dual of Corollary 5.4.8, we deduce that
D−(A) inherits a symmetric monoidal structure. We will show that φ can be promoted to a symmetric
monoidal functor (Theorem 5.5.5).
Notation 5.5.1. Let A be a symmetric monoidal abelian category.
Assume that A has enough in-

Proposition 5.5.2. Let A be an abelian category with enough injective objects, and suppose that A is

Let C⊗be a symmetric monoidal ∞-category. Then the restriction functor

(∗) For every morphism α : (A•

Proof. The simplicial category Fun(∆, Ainj)⊗admits path objects. It follows from Example A.1.3.3.21 that
the restriction map

(∗′) For every equivalence α in Fun(∆, Ainj)⊗, the image f(α) is an equivalence in C⊗.
It follows immediately that θ is fully faithful. It is clear from the proof of Proposition 5.3.15 that every functor

map of ∞-operads, we conclude that f satisﬁes (∗′) and therefore extends (in an essentially unique way)
to a functor f : N⊗Fun(∆, Ainj) →C⊗. This functor is evidently a map of ∞-operads, and condition (∗)
guarantees that it is a symmetric monoidal functor.

Lemma 5.5.4. Let C be a symmetric monoidal stable ∞-category equipped with a right-complete t-structure.

5.6
Quasi-Coherent Sheaves on Generalized Algebraic Gerbes
Let X be either a geometric stack or a coaﬃne stack over a ﬁeld of characteristic zero. Then X can be
recovered from the symmetric monoidal ∞-category QCoh(X) (see Theorem 3.4.2 and Corollary 4.6.19). If
Y is a generalized algebraic gerbe over a ﬁeld k of characteristic zero, then we can regard Y as an extension
of an algebraic gerbe by a coaﬃne stack (see Deﬁnition 5.2.1). It is therefore natural to expect that Y can be
recovered from the ∞-category QCoh(Y ), together with its symmetric monoidal structure. In this section,
we will prove that this is indeed the case. One of our main results can be stated as follows:
Proposition 5.6.1. Let X be a generalized algebraic gerbe and let A be a connective E∞-ring. Then the
construction
(η ∈X(A)) 7→(η∗: QCoh(X)⊗→Mod⊗
A)
determines a fully faithful embedding X(A) →Fun⊗(QCoh(X)⊗, Mod⊗
A), whose essential image is the col-
lection of symmetric monoidal functors F : QCoh(X) →ModA which preserve small colimits and connective
objects.
We will give the proof of Proposition 5.6.1 later in this section.
Corollary 5.6.2. Let X, Y : CAlgcn →bS be functors. Assume that X is a generalized algebraic gerbe. Then
the canonical map
MapFun(CAlgcn,bS)(Y, X) →Fun⊗(QCoh(X), QCoh(Y ))
is fully faithful, and its essential image is the collection of symmetric monoidal functors f : QCoh(X)⊗→
QCoh(Y )⊗which preserve small colimits and carry QCoh(X)cn into QCoh(Y )cn.
Let X be a generalized algebraic gerbe. We will prove below that the ∞-category QCoh(X) is presentable,
and that the full subcategory QCoh(X) determines an accessible t-structure on QCoh(X) (Proposition
5.6.18). We may therefore view the pair (QCoh(X), QCoh(X)cn) as a commutative algebra object of the
∞-category PrL
t (see Notation 4.6.1). Combining this observation with Corollary 5.6.2, we deduce:
Corollary 5.6.3. Let Grb ⊆Fun(CAlgcn, S) be the full subcategory spanned by the generalized gerbes. Then
the construction X 7→QCoh(X)⊗determines a fully faithful embedding Grb →CAlg(PrL
t ).
In fact, we can be more precise: it is possible to give an explicit description of the essential image of
the fully faithful embedding Grb →CAlg(PrL
t ) (Theorem 5.6.19). For this, we need to introduce a bit of
terminology.
Deﬁnition 5.6.4. A locally dimensional ∞-category is a symmetric monoidal stable ∞-category C⊗equipped
with a t-structure satisfying the following additional conditions:
(1) The ∞-category C is presentable and the tensor product ⊗preserves colimits separately in each variable.
168
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 169
(2) The t-structure on C is excellent: that is, it is an accessible t-structure which is both right complete
and left complete, and the full subcategory C≤0 ⊆C is closed under ﬁltered colimits.
(3) The unit object 1 ∈C belongs to the heart of C, and the tensor product functor ⊗: C × C →C is
t-exact: that is, the subcategories C≥0 and C≤0 are both closed under tensor products.
(4) The unit object 1 is a compact object of C≤n for each n ≥0.
(5) There exists an abelian subcategory C♥
fd ⊆C♥which generates C♥under ﬁltered colimits, such that
every object V ∈C♥
fd admits a dual V ∨in C♥
fd.
(6) The commutative ring k = π0 MapC(1, 1) is a ﬁeld of characteristic zero.
(7) For every object V ∈C♥
fd, the composition
1
c→V ⊗V ∨
e→1
corresponds to a nonnegative integer dim(V ) ∈Z ⊆Q ,→k. Here c and e denote compatible coevalu-
ation and evaluation maps for the duality between V and V ∨.
We begin with some general observations about the class of locally dimensional ∞-categories.
Remark 5.6.5. Let C⊗be a locally dimensional ∞-category. Suppose that V ∈C≥0 admits a dual V ∨∈C.
For W ∈C≥1, we have MapC(W, V ∨) ≃MapC(W ⊗V, 1) ≃0 since W ⊗V ∈C≥1 and 1 ∈C≤0. It follows
that V ∨∈C≤0.
Remark 5.6.6. Let C⊗be a locally dimensional ∞-category. Then every dualizable object V ∈C♥is
a compact object of C≤n for n ≥0. To see this, it suﬃces to show that the functor W 7→MapC(V, W)
commutes with ﬁltered colimits (when restricted to C≤n). Since V is dualizable, we can rewrite this functor
as W 7→MapC(1, V ∨⊗W). Since tensor product with V ∨carries C≤n to itself (Remark 5.6.5) and commutes
with colimits, the desired conclusion follows from the assumption that 1 is a compact object of C≤n.
Remark 5.6.7. Let C⊗be a locally dimensional ∞-category. It follows from Remark 5.6.6 and Proposition
T.5.3.5.11 that the inclusion C♥
fd ⊆C♥induces a fully faithful embedding i : Ind(C♥
fd) →C♥. Since C♥
fd
generates C♥under ﬁltered colimits, we conclude that i is an equivalence of ∞-categories. In particular, an
object of C♥is compact if and only if it is a retract of an object of C♥
fd. Since C♥
fd is the nerve of an abelian
category, it is idempotent complete. It follows that C♥
fd consists precisely of the compact objects of C♥.
Remark 5.6.8. Combining Remarks 5.6.6 and 5.6.7, we deduce that C♥
fd consists precisely of those objects
of C♥which admit a dual in C. It follows immediately that C♥
fd contains the unit object 1 and is stable under
tensor product.
Remark 5.6.9. It follows easily from the deﬁnitions that the construction V 7→dim(V ) is multiplicative:
that is, we have
dim(V ⊗W) = dim(V ) dim(W)
dim(1) = 1
dim(V ) = dim(V ∨).
Lemma 5.6.10. Let C⊗be a locally dimensional ∞-category. Then the collection of dualizable objects of C
span a stable subcategory of C. In particular, C♥
fd is closed under extensions in C♥.
Proof. For every pair of objects X, Y ∈C, let XY denote a classifying object for maps from Y into X.
According to Lemma A.4.2.5.6, an object Y ∈C is dualizable if and only if, for every object X ∈C, the
canonical map X ⊗1Y →XY is an equivalence in C. It is clear from this description that the collection of
dualizable objects is a stable subcategory of C.

Lemma 5.6.12. Let C⊗be a locally dimensional ∞-category and let U be a subobject of the unit object 1
in C♥
fd. Then either U ≃0 or the monomorphism U →1 is an equivalence.
Proof. We have a short exact sequence
0 →U
i→1
j→V →0
in the abelian category C♥. We will show that the maps i and j∨exhibit 1 as a coproduct of U and V ∨in
C. It will then follow that there exists a map r : 1 →U such that r ◦i ≃idU. We can regard i ◦r is an
element of the endomorphism ring k = π0 MapC(1, 1). If i ◦r = 0, then U ≃0. Otherwise, since k is a ﬁeld,
we deduce that i ◦r is invertible, so that r is a homotopy inverse to i and U ≃1.
It remains to show that the map f : U ⊕V ∨→1 given by i and j∨is an equivalence. Let C0 ⊆C be the
full subcategory spanned by those objects W for which idW ⊗f is an equivalence. We wish to prove that
1 ∈C0. Since C0 is evidently a stable subcategory of C, it will suﬃce to show that U and V belong to C0.
We begin by observing that there is an epimorphism U ⊗1 →U ⊗V and a monomorphism U ⊗V →1⊗V
in the abelian category C♥. It follows that U ⊗V can be described as the image of the composite map
U ⊗1 ≃U →V ≃1 ⊗V , which is nullhomotopic. It follows that U ⊗V ≃0. The short exact sequence
0 →U ⊗U →U ⊗1 →U ⊗V →0
shows that idU ⊗i is an equivalence U ⊗U →U. Consequently, to prove that idU ⊗f is an equivalence, it
will suﬃce to show that U ⊗V ∨≃0. This is clear: the identity map from U ⊗V ∨to itself factors as a
composition
U ⊗V ∨→U ⊗V ∨⊗V ⊗V ∨→U ⊗V ∨.
170
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 171
Since U ⊗V ≃0, the middle term vanishes, so that idU⊗V ∨is nullhomotopic and therefore U ⊗V ∨≃0.
This completes the proof that U ∈C0.
We now prove that V ∈C0: that is, the map
V ⊗(U ⊕V ∨) ≃(V ⊗U) ⊕(V ⊗V ∨) →V ⊗1 ≃V
is an equivalence. Since V ⊗U ≃0, this is equivalent to the statement that idV ⊗j∨. It now suﬃces to
observe that the coﬁber of idV ⊗j∨can be identiﬁed with V ⊗U ∨≃(U ⊗V ∨)∨≃0.
Lemma 5.6.13. Let C⊗be a locally dimensional ∞-category. An object V ∈C♥
fd is zero if and only if
dim(V ) = 0.
Proof. The “if” direction is obvious. For the converse, let K denote the kernel of the coevaluation map
c : 1 →V ⊗V ∨. If V is not a zero object of C, then c is nonzero so the monomorphism K →1 is not an
equivalence. It follows from Lemma 5.6.12 that K ≃0: that is, we have a short exact sequence
0 →1
c→V ⊗V ∨→K′ →0
in C♥
fd. Using Lemma 5.6.11 and Remark 5.6.9, we obtain
dim(V )2 = dim(V ⊗V ∨) = dim(1) + dim(K′) = 1 + dim(K′)
so that dim(V )̸ = 0.
Lemma 5.6.14. Let C⊗be a locally dimensional ∞-category, and let f : U →V be a monomorphism in
C♥
fd. Then dim(U) ≤dim(V ), with equality if and only if f is an equivalence.
Proof. We have an exact sequence
0 →U →V →U ′ →0.
Using Lemma 5.6.11, we obtain dim(V ) = dim(U) + dim(U ′) ≥dim(U).
Equality holds if and only if
dim(U ′) = 0, which (according to Lemma 5.6.13) is true if and only if U ′ ≃0.
Proposition 5.6.15. Let C⊗be a locally dimensional ∞-category. Then the subcategory C♥
fd is closed under
the formation of subobjects and quotient objects in C♥.
Proof. Let V ∈C♥
fd and let V0 ∈C♥be a subobject of V . Write V0 as the colimit of a diagram {Uα}α∈A in

so that V0 ≃lim

Proposition 5.6.17. Let C⊗be a locally dimensional ∞-category. Let D⊗be another symmetric monoidal

Proof. We wish to prove that for every object C ∈C, the map α induces an equivalence f(C) →g(C) in D.

Since C is right complete, we can write C = lim

The left vertical map is an equivalence by the inductive hypothesis, so we are reduced to proving that the

Since C is locally dimensional, we can write C as a ﬁltered colimit of objects of C♥

the map αC : f(C) →g(C) has a homotopy inverse, given by the dual of the map αC∨: f(C∨) →g(C∨).
Proposition 5.6.18. Let X be a generalized algebraic gerbe.
Then the ∞-category QCoh(X) is locally

(1) The ∞-category QCoh(X) is presentable.
(2) The subcategory QCoh(X)cn ⊆QCoh(X) spanned by the connective objects of QCoh(X) determines
an accessible t-structure (QCoh(X)≥0, QCoh(X)≤0) on QCoh(X).
(3) The t-structure on QCoh(X) is excellent.
(4) The subcategories QCoh(X)≤0 and QCoh(X)≥0 both contain the unit object and are closed under tensor
products.
(5) The unit object OX ∈QCoh(X) is a compact object of QCoh(X)≤n for each n ≥0.

is a map which exhibits X as a generalized algebraic gerbe over k, then the canonical map k ≃
π0 MapModk(k, k) →π0 MapQCoh(X)(OX, OX) is an isomorphism.

Our goal for the remainder of this section is to prove the following converse of Proposition 5.6.18, which
can be regarded as an ∞-categorical of Deligne’s characterization of Tannakian categories over ﬁelds of
characteristic zero.
Theorem 5.6.19. Let C be a locally dimensional ∞-category. Then there exists a ﬁeld k of characteristic
zero, a generalized algebraic gerbe X over k, and a t-exact symmetric monoidal equivalence C ≃QCoh(X).

Lemma 5.6.21. Let C be a locally dimensional ∞-category, and let X ⊆C≤0 be the full subcategory spanned
by those objects X satisfying the following conditions:
(∗) The objects πnX belong to C♥
fd for all n, and πnX ≃0 for n ≪0.
Then the inclusion X ⊆C≤0 induces an equivalence of ∞-categories θ : Ind(X) →C≤0.
Proof. Lemma 5.6.10 implies that every object X ∈X is dualizable, and therefore a compact object of C≤n
for all n ≥0. It follows from Proposition T.5.3.5.11 that θ is a fully faithful embedding. Note that X is
stable under ﬁnite colimits in C≤0, so that θ preserves small colimits (Proposition T.5.5.1.9) and therefore
admits a right adjoint G. To show that θ is an equivalence of ∞-categories, it will suﬃce to show that G
is conservative. To this end, let α : V →W be a morphism in C≤0 such that G(α) is an equivalence. We
wish to prove that α is an equivalence. Let U denote the ﬁber of the map α, so that G(U) ≃0; we wish to
prove that U ≃0. Assume otherwise. Since C is right complete, there exists an integer n such that π−nU is
nontrivial; let us assume that n is chosen as small as possible. Since C♥is generated by C♥
fd under ﬁltered
colimits, there exists a nonzero map u : X →π−nU for some X ∈C♥
fd. Then u determines a nontrivial point
of MapC(X[−n], U) ≃MapInd(X)(X[−n], G(U)), contradicting our assumption that G(U) ≃0.
Proposition 5.6.22. Let C be a locally dimensional ∞-category and let X be an object of C. The following
conditions are equivalent:
(1) We have πnX ∈C♥
fd for all n, and πnX ≃0 for all but ﬁnitely many n.
(2) The object X is dualizable and belongs to C≤n for some n.
(3) The object X is a compact object of C≤n for some n.
Proof. The implication (1) ⇒(2) follows from Lemma 5.6.10. The implication (2) ⇒(3) follows from the
equivalence MapC(X, Y ) ≃MapC(1, X∨⊗Y ) together with the assumption that 1 is a compact object of
C≤n for each n ≥0. We prove that (3) ⇒(1). Without loss of generality, we may assume that n = 0.
Lemma 5.6.21 implies that X is can be realized as a ﬁltered colimit of objects {Xα} in C≤0 which satisfy
condition (1). Since X is compact in C≤0, the identity map X →lim
−→Xα factors through some Xα. It follows
that X is a retract of Xα and therefore also satisﬁes (1).

spanned by the perfect objects, and Cperf

M ≃lim
−→M(n). Using (b), we are reduced to proving that each M(n) satisﬁes the conclusion of (d). The
proof proceeds by induction on n, the case n = 0 being trivial. If n > 0, we have a coﬁber sequence
A ⊗C(n) →M(n −1) →M(n).
Using the inductive hypothesis, we are reduced to proving that the free A-module A⊗C(n) satisﬁes condition
(d). We note that F ′(A ⊗C(n)) ≃F(C(n)) belongs to C≤−n since F is left t-exact, and that the unit map
G(1) ⊗C(n) ≃A ⊗C(n) →G(C(n)) ≃G(1 ⊗C(n))
is an equivalence by virtue of (c).
We next claim that the functor G| C≤0 is conservative. Indeed, if α : V →W is a morphism in C≤0
such that G(α) is an equivalence. We wish to prove that α is an equivalence. Suppose otherwise, and let
U = ﬁb(α). Since C is right complete and U ∈C≤0, the assertion that U is nonzero implies that π−nU̸ = 0
for some n ≥0; choose n as small as possible. Since F induces an equivalence from QCoh(Y )♥to C♥, we
conclude that there exists a nontrivial map F(M[−n]) →U for some M ∈QCoh(Y )♥. It follows that there
is a nontrivial map M[−n] →G(U) ≃ﬁb(G(α)), contradicting our assumption that α is an equivalence.
It follows that G′| C≤0 is also a conservative functor. Combining this observation with (d), we obtain:
(e) The functor F ′ induces an equivalence ModA(QCoh(Y ))≤0 →C≤0, and therefore exhibits C as a left
completion of ModA(QCoh(Y )).
178
AUTHOR PDF PHYSICAL PAGE 179
Let X′ : CAlgcn →bS be the functor described by the formula
X′(R) = MapCAlg(PrL
t )(ModA(QCoh(Y )), ModR).
The symmetric monoidal functor F ′ induces a natural transformation X →X′. Using (e) and Corollary
4.6.17, we conclude that this natural transformation is an equivalence.
Since Y is an algebraic gerbe over k, there exists a ﬁeld extension κ of k corepresenting a functor
Specf κ : CAlgcn →S and a natural transformation g : Specf κ →Y , which is representable, aﬃne, and
faithfully ﬂat. Let A0 = g∗A, regarded as a k-algebra. It follows from (a) that the algebra A0 is coconnective
and that π−1A0 ≃0. Theorem A.6.3.4.6 gives an equivalence QCoh(Specf κ) ⊗QCoh(Y ) ModA(QCoh(Y )) ≃
ModA0. It follows that the functor X0 = X ×Y Specf κ ≃X′ ×Y Specf κ can be described by the formula
X0(R) = MapCAlg(PrL
t )(ModA0, ModR). Corollary 4.6.18 shows that X0 can be identiﬁed with the coaﬃne
stack determined by A0; this completes the proof of (1).
We now prove (2). There is an evident symmetric monoidal functor ψ : C →QCoh(X), which is right
t-exact and preserves small colimits. We claim that ψ is left t-exact. In view of (e), it will suﬃce to show
that the composite functor ψ ◦F ′ carries ModA(QCoh(Y ))≤0 into QCoh(X)≤0. Let g′ : X0 →X be the
projection map; we wish to prove that g′∗◦ψ◦F ′ carries ModA(QCoh(Y ))≤0 into QCoh(X0)≤0. This functor
factors as a composition
ModA(QCoh(Y ))≤0
g∗
→ModA(QCoh(Specf κ))≤0 ≃(ModA0)≤0 →QCoh(X0),
so that the desired result follows from Proposition 4.5.2.
Since C and QCoh(X) are both left complete, to show that ψ is an equivalence it will suﬃce to show
that ψ induces an equivalence C≤0 →QCoh(X)≤0. In view of (e), this is equivalent to the statement that
ψ induces an equivalence θ : ModA(QCoh(Y ))≤0 →QCoh(X)≤0.
Let Y• be the ˇCech nerve of the map g : Specf κ →Y , and let X• denote the pullback Y• ×Y X. Then θ
can be identiﬁed with the totalization of a cosimplicial functor
θ• : ModA(QCoh(Y•))≤0 →QCoh(X•)≤0.
It therefore suﬃces to show that each θn is an equivalence.
Every map [0] →[n] in ∆induces a map
Yn →Y0 ≃Specf k which is representable and aﬃne, so that so that Yn ≃Specf Bn for some commutative
k-algebra Bn. We observe that θn can be identiﬁed with the functor
ModBn(ModA(QCoh(Y0))≤0) →ModBn(QCoh(X0)≤0)
induced by θ0. We may therefore reduce to the case n = 0: that is, we are reduced to proving that the functor
ModA0 →QCoh(X0) induces an equivalence (ModA0)≤0 →QCoh(X0)≤0. This follows from Proposition
4.5.2.
